However, the predicted abundances are systematically offset from measured values. Recalibration fit on as few as 25 labeled rocks recovers 76 percent of the likelihood improvement from fitting on the full 368-rock fitting set, while abundance error responds more slowly (11.0% zero-shot and 9.5% with the full fitting set). This work demonstrates how a machine learning model in the geochemical sciences can both leverage an imperfect simulator and mitigate its systematic biases.

new Learning atomic interactions for the thermodynamics of liquid mixtures

Authors: Marco Hoffmann, Sebastian Schmitt, Hans Hasse, Fabian Jirasek

Abstract: Predictive models for activity coefficients, which describe the non-ideality of liquid mixtures, are foundational for process design and optimization. Group-contribution methods, such as UNIFAC, are computationally cheap and well established in process simulators. Modern machine learning models, such as HANNA, offer higher accuracy and broader applicability, but their integration into process simulation is expensive or even infeasible. In this work, we introduce COGNAC, a graph neural network that learns atom-level geometry and interaction parameters that depend on the molecular neighborhood and evaluates them in the UNIFAC framework. Trained on about 820,000 experimental data points, COGNAC achieves significantly higher accuracy than UNIFAC and HANNA for binary and multi-component mixtures, reduces error relative to HANNA by 13-63%, reliably predicts multi-phase equilibria and identifies azeotropes, yields interpretable parameters, and is strictly thermodynamically consistent. COGNAC evaluations run at a cost comparable to UNIFAC, thereby unlocking the potential of machine-learning models for process simulations.

new High-Fidelity Aerostructural Dataset of Transonic Wings in Cruise Conditions

Authors: Pavankumar Koratikere, Abhijnan Dikshit, Leifur Leifsson

Abstract: Recent advances in machine learning (ML) have the potential to substantially accelerate aircraft wing design, a process that has traditionally required extensive high-fidelity simulation and design iteration. Existing public datasets for ML-based wing design have focused primarily on aerodynamic performance, with limited consideration of the multidisciplinary interactions that govern practical aircraft design. In particular, efficient wing design requires simultaneous consideration of aerodynamic performance and structural integrity, yet no publicly available datasets capture this coupled aerostructural behavior at high-fidelity. To address this need, a high-fidelity multidisciplinary dataset for static aerostructural analysis of transonic wings is introduced. The dataset includes 2471 samples, along with three out-of-distribution samples. The wing planform geometry is parameterized by span, sweep, dihedral, tip chord, and tip twist, while the airfoil cross-sections are parameterized using the class-shape transformation method. The analysis is further parameterized by Mach number, angle of attack, altitude, and structural load factor. The utility of the dataset is demonstrated by training state-of-the-art ML models to predict aerodynamic surface quantities, integrated aerodynamic performance quantities, and the failure distribution of the wingbox. ML models achieve at most 0.0309 normalized root mean squared error (NRMSE) in the prediction of aerodynamic quantities and at most 0.1189 NRMSE in the prediction of failure distribution of the wingbox. By providing coupled aerodynamic and structural responses for a diverse set of transonic wings, this dataset establishes a benchmark for developing, evaluating, and comparing multi-physics ML models for wing design. Broadly, it is intended to encourage creation of similar high-fidelity multi-physics benchmark datasets across engineering domains.

new Split Federated Unlearning

Authors: Jia Chen, Yecheng Lv, Xinrong Hu, Mingdi Huang, Jun Bai

Abstract: Split federated learning (SFL) combines partitioned model execution with federated aggregation to support collaborative learning across distributed Web and edge environments. However, removing learned contributions in SFL is challenging due to cross-partition coupling and historical influence propagation through dual-side aggregation. To the best of our knowledge, we provide the first systematic formulation of Split Federated Unlearning (SFU), encompassing client-wise and class-wise removal, counterfactual retraining, and partition-specific update constraints. We theoretically characterize historical influence propagation and mode-dependent unlearning feasibility, revealing structural limitations of restricted model updates. As an initial solution, we propose SURE, integrating dual-importance estimation, selective parameter perturbation, and importance-guided recovery. Extensive experiments with existing unlearning methods adapted to SFL reveal substantial forgetting-utility trade-offs across model architectures, data distributions, and update modes. SURE achieves competitive agreement with mode-matched retraining at modest additional cost, while further analyses highlight the impact of split position and update constraints on unlearning behavior. These findings establish SFU as a distinct research problem and provide a foundation for unlearning in partitioned federated systems.

new CoHARL: Co-Evolving Agent Policies and Executable Harnesses with Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Jianwen Chen, Peng Xia, Kaide Zeng, Siwei Han, Zhen-Yu Zhang, Charles Fleming, Xinyu Ye, Huaxiu Yao

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents interact with their environments through executable harnesses that govern tool use and feedback. Optimizing a harness for a fixed policy overlooks how its behavior changes during learning, while training a policy under a fixed harness leaves the interface that generates its training experience unchanged. This motivates adapting the harness throughout policy training. We introduce CoHARL, a population-based reinforcement learning framework that co-evolves a shared policy and a population of executable harnesses. The policy learns from rollouts across the population, and a proposer uses its execution traces to revise harness code during training. CoHARL achieves a 27.6% mean resolved rate on SWE-rebench, improving over harness-only search by 6.4 percentage points. Experiments on SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench 2.0 show gains in software engineering and terminal-based tasks. Under matched training budgets, CoHARL also outperforms searching for a harness once before RL and alternating policy updates with revisions to a single active harness.

new Underdamped Anchored Langevin Dynamics

Authors: Qi Feng, Hoang M. Nguyen, Lingjiong Zhu

Abstract: Classical Langevin Monte Carlo algorithms, such as the unadjusted Langevin algorithm (ULA), are based on discretization schemes of the overdamped Langevin dynamics and are widely used for sampling in machine learning because they scale to high dimensions and large datasets. However, they require differentiable log-densities, and are less efficient for sampling heavy-tailed distributions. Although various approaches have been proposed in the literature for sampling non-differentiable and heavy-tailed targets, all of them are developed in the overdamped setting only. We propose a novel momentum-based underdamped anchored Langevin dynamics (UALD), and underdamped anchored Langevin Monte Carlo (UALMC) algorithm based on a discretization scheme of UALD. Our paper is the first to provide a kinetic (momentum-based) Langevin algorithm for non-differentiable and heavy-tailed targets that is free of asymptotic bias. The method replaces the original potential with a smooth reference potential and modifies the underdamped Langevin dynamics via multiplicative scaling in the drift term. We establish non-asymptotic guarantees in the 2-Wasserstein distance to the target distribution and show that the resulting iteration complexity has better dependence on dimension and accuracy level compared to overdamped-based anchored Langevin algorithms. We provide numerical experiments to illustrate the theory and practical performance of our proposed approach.

new Proposing Better Rollouts for On-Policy Distillation

Authors: Sirui Chen, Yunzhe Qi, Haobo Xu, Jiaru Zou, Mengting Ai, Zihao Li, Jingrui He

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) improves language-model post-training by letting the student learn from its own sampled trajectories under teacher supervision, aligning the training process with inference-time behavior. However, the rollout policy in OPD is usually treated as fixed: each round samples directly from the current student. We show that this choice can create a compounding teacher--student mismatch, because it requires the teacher to provide effective supervision on whichever trajectories the student samples, including ones less reachable by the teacher itself. On such trajectories, teacher supervision can become noisy or even misleading, leading to undesirable student patterns such as repetition. Moreover, since the updated student in turn defines the rollout policy of subsequent rounds, these patterns persist and potentially amplify over training. This failure mode suggests that improving OPD requires treating the rollout policy itself as an optimization target, rather than only modifying the distillation objective. We propose Mixture Proposal On-Policy Distillation (MPOPD), which derives a student-anchored, teacher-guided rollout distribution from a local trust-region objective based on relative teacher support. The resulting target is a geometric mixture of the student and teacher policies, and we introduce a proposal policy that approximates this mixture, enabling rollout generation in a single forward pass. Experiments on 16 math, knowledge, and medical reasoning benchmarks show consistent gains over OPD and recent variants. On math reasoning, our method improves average accuracy by 6.91 over OPD on Qwen3-4B-Base while reducing average generation length by 41%, suggesting that rollout policy design provides an effective way to improve OPD.

new PRISM: Pathology Reliability In Scarce-label Medicine. A Tile-level Benchmark of Pathology Foundation Models

Authors: Alicem Koyun

Abstract: Pathology foundation models are typically benchmarked on classification performance at full label availability and on in-distribution test sets, while calibration, behavior at low label budgets, and robustness under cross-institutional shift are seldom evaluated jointly. We introduce PRISM, a tile-level benchmark that measures the reliability of pathology foundation models along four axes: model choice, label fraction, calibration, and out-of-distribution transfer. PRISM evaluates eight foundation models (CLIP, PLIP, CONCH, UNI, Virchow2, GigaPath, H-Optimus-0, Midnight) on six tile-level datasets at six label fractions (1% to 100%) with three seeds (864 linear probe runs), extended with a five-hospital Camelyon17 study of 20 directed transfer pairs. Four phenomena emerge. (i) Under full supervision, AUROC rank predicts post-temperature calibration rank consistently across datasets (Spearman rho = 0.52, I^2 = 0%), whereas at 1% labels the relationship becomes dataset-specific (rho = -0.24, I^2 = 67%). (ii) On harder datasets the linear probe collapses to a single predicted class below a dataset-specific label fraction. (iii) Whether additional source-domain labels help or harm target calibration tracks how well the probe transfers: below a transfer AUROC of roughly 0.85, more labels worsen target ECE in 86-88% of combinations. (iv) Temperature scaling fails to reduce ECE in 49 of 288 cells, miscalibration is predominantly under-confidence, and a source-fitted temperature is harmful under failing transfer. PRISM is released as a pip-installable package with reference results for all 288 cells. Code and data: https://github.com/alicemkyn/prism-benchmark

URLs: https://github.com/alicemkyn/prism-benchmark

new Folding Attention Biases into Keys Makes Compacted KV Caches Run on Standard Kernels

Authors: Utkarsh Kanwat

Abstract: Attention Matching (AM) compacts a KV cache by keeping some keys per head, refitting their values and adding a bias to each kept key's logit. FlashAttention and vLLM's paged caches have no input for it. We fold the bias into the keys along one closed-form direction per head, fitted on context queries, giving a plain cache with AM's size and head dimension and the model's own query path; the output error is bounded by how far each query's projection on that direction is from 1. With equal per-head budgets, unmodified vLLM serves folded caches through a KV connector, matching Hugging Face's answers on all 51 Qwen3-4B questions and its greedy output on 180 of 200 Qwen3-14B LongBench samples. With equal budgets on QuALITY (Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, 8B, Llama-3.1-8B) and LongHealth (three Qwen3 models), the fold is within noise of AM's accuracy in 13 of 14 settings, with lower KL to the full cache than dropping the bias in all 12 where both ran. Under the published per-head budgets, on Qwen3-4B, its Instruct-2507 variant and Llama-3.1-8B, it is within noise of AM in 9 of 10, no longer beats dropping the bias in KL, and the two each win once in accuracy.

new NextEval: An Agent-Based Solver and Benchmark for Derivative-Free Optimization

Authors: Shuo Chen, Li Cui, Cunxin Huang, Ya-Feng Liu, Minghang Xie, Meng Xu, Zaikun Zhang

Abstract: Derivative-free optimization (DFO) addresses problems where derivatives are unavailable or costly to obtain. This setting often arises in simulation-based and experimental applications, where evaluating the black-box objective can itself be costly. Thus, it remains a central challenge in DFO to effectively exploit accumulated observations to select informative evaluation points under a limited budget. In this light, we introduce the NextEval solver, an agent-based DFO solver, which integrates the capabilities of a large language model (LLM) to guide the sequential selection of evaluation points. Specifically, we establish a system that combines an LLM agent with a numerical environment. The environment provides tools for analyzing past evaluations and executing numerical methods, while maintaining a shared evaluation history and method-specific state to support continuation across method switches. Using observations and tool feedback, the LLM agent selects tools, configures their parameters, and allocates the evaluation budget, while numerical methods compute and evaluate candidate points. Building on this system, we also introduce NextEval Bench, a benchmark protocol for comparing agent configurations on shared DFO tasks using the same numerical tools under matched evaluation budgets and information constraints. Numerical results on low-dimensional DFO problems demonstrate that the NextEval solver outperforms the state-of-the-art DFO baselines in function-evaluation efficiency.

new Holomorphic Cauchy Networks for Efficient Stationary Tail Computation in High-Dimensional Reflected Brownian Motions

Authors: Kehan Wang

Abstract: Stationary tail computation for high-dimensional reflected Brownian motions (RBMs) remains computationally challenging. Building on NN4MGF's shared-coordinate basic-adjoint-relationship (BAR) framework, we develop Holomorphic Cauchy Networks (HCNs). Native complex-valued rational Cauchy subnetworks enforce holomorphicity on pole-free domains, removing sampled Cauchy-Riemann penalties and their input-derivative graphs. Exact origin anchoring and boundary-coordinate exclusion enforce structural identities, while anchored interactions represent dependent transforms. Under holomorphic-log assumptions, we establish simultaneous value and first-derivative approximation for the constrained architectures and a mixed-derivative lower bound for coordinate-only representations under nonzero cross-covariance. Closed-form real-axis derivatives and sequential backpropagation further control memory. On the same NVIDIA H100 NVL, the tested 30D HCN configuration uses approximately tenfold less peak allocated memory and has 9.7-fold lower mean update time than NN4MGF; extrapolation to the respective scheduled update counts gives a projected full-schedule update-time ratio of 58.2. With 64 million sampled base inputs per high-dimensional run, HCN reaches 50 dimensions on a laptop GPU with 6.367% mean relative error over thresholds 1 through 8. Degree-matched comparisons yield lower reported mean relative errors over the primary evaluation ranges, including 1.422% versus 6.684% on the 2D Harrison non-product benchmark. An exploratory 10D correlated example illustrates the interaction extension.

new Learning from Points and Patches: A Pre-trained Hybrid Attention Transformer for Time Series Analysis

Authors: Peiwang Tang, Cheng HE, Yupeng Ye, Weitai Zhang, Qinghua Sun, Defu Lian

Abstract: Time-series data generated by Web services and connected systems support applications such as user activity analysis, service monitoring, and resource management. However, learning transferable representations from heterogeneous time series remains challenging due to diverse temporal patterns, variable sequence lengths, and limited high-quality annotations. Despite the success of pre-training in natural language processing and computer vision, effective time-series pre-training requires strategies tailored to temporal data. We propose TimeHAT (Hybrid Attention Transformer), a general-purpose pre-training framework for time-series analysis that addresses two complementary questions: what to learn and how to learn. For what to learn, we introduce a Unified Point-Patch Masking Strategy (UPPMS) that integrates point-level and patch-level supervision within a unified framework for contextual reconstruction and causal extrapolation, enabling the model to capture both fine-grained temporal variations and broader temporal patterns. For how to learn, we develop a Hybrid Attention Encoder (HAE) that combines prefix-constrained bidirectional attention in initial layers with directional hybrid attention in deeper layers, aiming to preserve effective contextual interactions while mitigating rank degeneration. We evaluate TimeHAT across real-world datasets and four downstream tasks: classification, long-term forecasting, imputation, and anomaly detection. Experiments cover fine-tuning, few-shot, and zero-shot transfer settings, demonstrating competitive performance across diverse time-series analysis tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/TangPeiwang/TimeHAT.

new Less is more: learning what to profile for GPU kernel optimization

Authors: Yihan Wang, Peiyu Liu, Runyu Chen, Wei Xu

Abstract: Large language models have recently shown strong potential for automatically optimizing GPU kernels, yet their effectiveness depends critically on the hardware information available to the kernel optimization model. Existing systems typically rely on predefined profiling strategies, leaving what to profile largely outside the optimization process. We show that effective profiling is not simply about collecting more information: increasing the number of profiling metrics does not monotonically improve kernel optimization, while the most useful profiling information varies across kernels. Our theoretical analysis further shows that, under such heterogeneous profiling utility, kernel-conditioned selection can outperform any single fixed profiling configuration. Motivated by this observation, we propose AutoProfiler, a feedback-driven framework for learning a deterministic profiling tree from optimization feedback. The tree is iteratively refined through REWRITE and SPLIT operations, with an LLM generating candidate modifications and empirical evaluation determining their acceptance. Once constructed, the tree quickly gathers the required profiling information for any kernel, allowing the LLM rewriter to be invoked only once. Experiments on KernelBench show that our approach improves kernel optimization over fixed profiling configurations under constrained profiling budgets. These results suggest that hardware profiling should evolve from static metric collection toward profiling reasoning, making profiling itself a new dimension for improving kernel optimization. The codes are available at https://github.com/Satissss/AutoProfiler.

new MM-Net: A Multimodal Multi-Task Network for Sleep Staging and Sleep Apnea Assessment After Ischemic Stroke

Authors: Md Wahiduzzaman Suva, Esm E Moula Chowdhury Abha, Md Imtiaj Alam Sajin, Md Iftekharul Mobin, Jia Uddin

Abstract: Automated polysomnography after ischemic stroke is challenging because of altered sleep-related neural activity and concurrent respiratory disturbances. Existing stroke-specific approaches mainly address sleep-stage classification, leaving joint analysis of sleep architecture and respiratory events underexplored. Moreover, adapting pretrained EEG encoders introduces a representation inconsistency when downstream models are trained on embeddings from previously seen patients but evaluated on unseen individuals. We propose MM-Net, a multimodal multi-task framework that resolves this inconsistency through cross-fitting and ridge-based embedding alignment. Sleep-adapted EEG-EOG representations and engineered neural descriptors are fused with learned cardiorespiratory features through cross-attention and shared temporal modeling for simultaneous sleep staging and respiratory-event detection. On iSLEEPS (99 patients with subacute ischemic stroke), ten-fold patient-independent evaluation achieved 76.3% staging accuracy (Cohen's $\kappa$ = 0.656) and 0.825 respiratory-event AUC, outperforming the evaluated baselines under matched protocols. The sleep-adapted encoder improved the downstream model when its embeddings were aligned, raising staging accuracy by 1.4 percentage points over the frozen encoder ($p$ = 0.020) versus 0.2 points without alignment. MM-Net also achieved 86.4% staging accuracy on Sleep-EDF, above every single-EEG architecture reimplemented on the same recordings, and 80.3% staging accuracy with 0.889 respiratory AUC on ISRUC-I, exceeding its retrained staging baselines. Without retraining, the iSLEEPS-trained model retained respiratory AUCs of 0.833 on ISRUC-I and 0.841 on UCD. These findings support representation-consistent neural adaptation and joint physiological modeling across stroke, healthy-sleep, and sleep-disorder cohorts.

new Recall or Relearn? A Mixture of In-Context Experts for Recurring Concept Drift

Authors: Zhangkai Wu, Xu Zhang, Zhihong Cui, Yiqi Wang, Yi Zhang

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) are being applied to Web data streams such as network intrusion detection, where they adapt by updating the labelled rows of their context without retraining. When a past concept returns after leaving the context, a TFM faces a recall-or-relearn decision between restoring stored rows of the concept and relearning it from new labels. We propose MICE (Mixture of In-Context Experts), a training-free learner that cuts the stream into segments at detected changes. It merges a closed segment into a stored context when the TFM's estimated additional error on the segment is within a tolerance, under a capacity limit on stored contexts. A fast level weights the stored contexts and first-in-first-out (FIFO) windows by the log-loss of their predictions on the last labelled batch. We show that, with pure stored contexts and a margin condition, this fast level gains over relearning, in expected accuracy, the normalised positive area between the stored context's accuracy and the TFM's learning curve, up to a first-batch term. A slow level mixes the fast level's prediction with that of the FIFO window of the last labelled rows and bounds the discounted half-Brier regret of MICE against this window at every time. Learning curves measured beforehand predict the ordering of the observed recall gains. MICE outperforms reset, which empties the context at detected drifts, on grid streams with random boundary phases. Merging keeps fewer experts than pools that store every segment, and with fixed segmentation a time-matched snapshot pool matches MICE on the shifted grid. On real streams, MICE has the highest mean accuracy of all policies we compare.

new Efficient Quantization Training with Stochastic Rounding via Structured Subspace Randomness

Authors: Zeyu Wang, Yiheng Duan, Tao Chen, Donghyun Koo, Sihan Liu, Wenbo Zhang, Chuan He, Liang Liu

Abstract: Low-precision training often relies on stochastic rounding (SR) to keep quantized gradients unbiased and prevent small updates from rounding to zero. Standard SR draws an independent random threshold for every gradient element. However, accelerators generate random numbers on vector units whose throughput lags far behind their matrix units. Drawing one threshold per element saturates the vector unit and can make INT8 training with SR slower than BF16 training. Sharing random thresholds across tensor elements removes this cost but forces those elements to round in the same direction and shifts their rounding errors by a common offset. We find that this offset degrades training primarily through the activation gradient, which propagates the error backward into earlier layers. Because earlier layers cannot cancel an incoming offset using their own local rounding errors, the offset must be removed at the layer where it originates. We propose Structured Subspace Stochastic Rounding (S3R), which quantizes each upstream gradient once for both the activation and weight gradient matrix multiplications and shares a single threshold per token across the hidden dimension. Its error correction, S3R-EC, subtracts the structured mean rounding error from the activation and weight gradients while leaving the optimizer unchanged. In LLM pre-training experiments, uncorrected threshold sharing degrades convergence, whereas S3R-EC eliminates this degradation and matches the validation loss of independent SR. Furthermore, S3R-EC accelerates full INT8 training steps of dense LLMs on TPU v5p by 1.24x to 1.32x over BF16 and by 1.11x over independent SR.

new Normalized Cuts and Data Clustering via Matrix Fractional Programming

Authors: Yannan Chen, Beichen Huang, Licheng Zhao, Chris Ding, Kaiming Shen

Abstract: The normalized cut (NCut) problem is a fundamental one in the area of data clustering. Despite its fractional structure, the NCut problem is seldom considered from a fractional programming (FP) perspective in the existing literature. The main reason is that the conventional FP method is inadequate for this problem case: the classical Dinkelbach's transform can only handle a single ratio and hence is limited to the two-class clustering, whereas the multi-class clustering involves multiple ratio terms. This work proposes a novel so-called multidimensional quadratic transform that is capable of dealing with the multi-ratio case; it recasts the NCut problem into a bipartite matching problem---which can be immediately solved by linear search. Furthermore, we explore the connection between the proposed FP method and the minorization-maximization (MM) theory, and in turn characterize the convergence behavior of our algorithm. Finally, we conduct experiments to numerically demonstrate the advantages of the proposed FP-based algorithm in a variety of data clustering and image segmentation tasks.

new Balanced State Subspaces for Mamba-2 State Compression

Authors: Shang Tingyu, Cao Wenfei

Abstract: Mamba-2 compresses all context into a fixed-size recurrent state that dominates decoding memory and bandwidth. Existing compressors quantize state values or prune state coordinates, scoring each channel independently in the native basis. We examine trained Mamba-2 states and find that energy is almost rank-one, but the output does not read the energetic directions: energy concentrates in a bias-like direction accumulated by the recurrence, while the content the output reads lives in a low-dimensional subspace that is not aligned with the state's coordinates. Even output-aware scores are computed coordinate by coordinate, so they miss the directions that determine the input--output map. Compression should therefore target the write--read subspace rather than individual coordinates. We propose \method{}, a post-training transform that instantiates balanced truncation with full controllability and observability Gramians and applies the rotation exactly via Mamba-2's scalar-identity transition. Because the useful rank varies widely across layers, we further introduce HARP, which allocates ranks per layer from the Hankel spectrum and protects retrieval-sensitive layers, improving both perplexity and retrieval over uniform budgets. Calibration takes a single forward pass, an order of magnitude faster than GHOST's released protocol, with lower peak memory. With HARP, \method{} halves the state dimension at an average WikiText-2 perplexity increase of $0.3$ points ($1.8\%$) across 130M--2.7B, versus $1.6$ ($9.3\%$) for GHOST, and the gap widens at 25\% retention.

new Dream-COP: Guiding Algorithm Discovery for Combinatorial Optimization Problems in an Experience World

Authors: Kuan Xu, Jianming Wang, Ni Zhang, Yaoxin Wu, Yong Wang, Zhiguang Cao

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) enable algorithm discovery for combinatorial optimization problems (COPs) through iterative program generation and evaluation. A central challenge is how to organize search to discover better algorithms within a limited budget. Our key insight is that past search trajectories and their recorded outcomes form an experience world, which can be used to facilitate the COP solving through replay. We introduce Dream-COP, in which a \emph{policy agent} learns executable policies through \emph{dreaming} in this experience world to guide a \emph{discovery agent} in generating and refining COP programs. Specifically, policy replay provides feedback on the quality of the programs revealed and the search steps used, to guide policy selection more effectively. Across five COPs, \textsc{Dream} discovers better algorithms than fixed exploration in both heuristic component design and complete solver generation. Particularly, it matches the optimal values on KP and reduces the gap on OBPP from 2.112\% to 1.199\% while using 42.5\% fewer search requests. Moreover, the learned exploration policies transfer effectively across different COPs with the assitance of the experience world.

new The Batch Size Can Be One: Test-Time Adaptation from Classifier Geometry

Authors: Jaeho Hwang, Yujin Oh, Juhyeon Shin, Juhye Lee, Sungroh Yoon, Uiwon Hwang

Abstract: Test-time adaptation updates a deployed model on an unlabeled test stream. In practice this stream often arrives one sample at a time, with the classes in no particular order. A single sample then provides limited information for estimating distribution-level statistics, and selecting samples by confidence can admit predictions that are wrong yet confident. A corruption, however, shifts the features while leaving the decision regions of the frozen classifier unchanged. We therefore propose Origin-recentered Test-Time Adaptation (OriTTA), which constructs fixed geometric targets from the source model and adapts selected features toward them. Seen from the origin of the feature space, the classes lean toward a common direction. We choose a new origin to bring class scores closer together while keeping it near the mean feature of one synthetic prototype per class. From this origin, prototype directions spread more evenly, and each class obtains an axis and an ideal point before any test data arrives. During adaptation, OriTTA aligns selected features with their predicted class axis and penalizes excessive radius, keeps one input per predicted class in memory to limit feature changes for classes absent from the stream, and resets the model when re-classifying this memory reveals collapse. Without tuning for each setting, OriTTA achieves the highest mean accuracy at batch size 1 on CIFAR-10-C, CIFAR-100-C and ImageNet-C in all sixteen combinations of four backbones, two protocols and two stream orders. On ImageNet-C fog, recent methods collapse in up to two thirds of one hundred runs, while OriTTA never does.

new Deep Bayesian Approaches for Interpretable Nonlinear Structural System Identification

Authors: Wei Liu, Konstantinos Vlachas, Eleni Chatzi

Abstract: System identification, namely the inference of mathematical representations of dynamical systems from measured data, is fundamental to engineering diagnostics, prognostics, and decision-making. Bayesian filtering offers a principled framework for probabilistic state estimation but traditionally relies on prescribed transition and observation models. Recent advances in deep Bayesian filtering address this limitation by embedding learnable representations within probabilistic state-space formulations, enabling data-driven identification of unknown dynamics while retaining physical structure and uncertainty-aware inference. This tutorial provides a systematic introduction to deep Bayesian filtering for nonlinear structural system identification, focusing on the interplay between learned dynamics, physical inductive biases, and posterior inference. The Deep Markov Model serves as a data-driven reference, while the Physics-guided Deep Markov Model (PgDMM) and Neural Extended Kalman Filter (Neural EKF) illustrate complementary approaches based on amortized variational inference and differentiable Kalman filtering, respectively. Their capabilities are examined through dynamic state estimation and joint dynamic state and parameter estimation on a three-dimensional shear-frame benchmark with hysteretic Bouc-Wen connections and sparse acceleration measurements. The results highlight important distinctions between measurement-conditioned reconstruction and open-loop forecasting, demonstrate how physical structure can improve latent-state interpretability without necessarily ensuring predictive fidelity, and reveal differences in the robustness of response and parameter estimation. Practical guidelines for model construction, training, evaluation, and deployment are provided, together with an outlook on remaining challenges in uncertainty calibration, identifiability, and real-world validation.

new When Does Perturbation Consistency Add Information Beyond Softmax Confidence? Theory and Evidence for the Decision Consistency Framework

Authors: Amit Kalitaa, Moirangthem Tiken Singh

Abstract: Perturbation consistency scores judge whether a prediction survives small input changes. The Decision Consistency Framework (DCF) scores each image by label stability (LS) and confidence deviation (CD) under six mild operators, combines them into a consistency score (CS), and summarises a model by the gap between accuracy and mean consistency (CAG). We ask when these scores carry information that the clean softmax output lacks. Across 201 evaluations with three seeds per configuration, CS detected misclassifications on clean data less well than the maximum softmax probability (MSP) in every model family. For frozen CNNs, the deficit was 0.057 in area under the ROC curve (AUROC; 95% CI 0.043 to 0.073), at 8 to 15 times the compute. A label flip requires the logit margin to be at most the square root of two times the logit displacement, and at a fixed margin, the displacement predicted errors at AUROC 0.515. The same inequality gives a label-free split-conformal no-flip certificate, never violated in 84 runs. Consistency did add information where confidence is misled: under noise corruptions of CIFAR-10-C, it beat the margin, and under projected gradient attacks, it separated clean from attacked inputs with AUROC 0.83 against 0.37 for MSP at a budget of 2/255, as pre-specified. The gain under noise did not transfer to unseen shift families. The large negative CAG on OCTMNIST persists under full fine-tuning and in-domain training and is partly a class-prior effect: correcting the prior recovers about 40% of the gap.

new When Is a Cheap Fairness Edit Safe? Screening Post-Hoc Edits in Two-Sided Matching Markets

Authors: Moirangthem Tiken Singha, Rabinder Kumar Prasada

Abstract: Two-sided matching systems, such as admissions pipelines, allocate opportunities jointly. Reducing a protected at tribute's influence usually requires encoder retraining. We study a closed-form linear projector that does this at inference time. For markets that stay vertically ordered before and after the edit, we bound the mean welfare change by the edit-induced appeal shift and an explicit remainder. A probabilistic refinement replaces worst-case constants with a data-dependent deviation term. A pre-deployment screen combines a verticality test with a welfare sweep and returns PASS, KILL, or OUT-OF-SCOPE. An exact tier requires every opportunity to rank applicants by appeal, and a graded tier admits near-vertical markets. On 30 held-out synthetic markets, the screen agrees with every pre-specified regime verdict, with no observed false PASS. On 60 exactly vertical markets, no bound is violated, and the probabilistic bound is informative in 69% to 87% of configurations. On 2700 configurations from three realistic markets, which do not reach the exact tier, no plug-in bound expression is violated. A closed-form factorization stays within graded scope in 90% to 100% of instances, whereas two-tower and graph neural network encoders fall outside it in every instance. Architecture choice thus appears to affect whether a post-hoc edit is admissible.

new TACO-Graph: Automated Construction of Text-Atributed Graphs

Authors: Amiri Hayes, Kristina Wicke

Abstract: Graph-structured representations of text data have become foundational across machine learning, yet it remains unclear how to systematically construct a graph from a given dataset for a target task. Choices about which entities become nodes, which relationships become edges, and what text each node contains can substantially affect downstream performance. In practice, finding optimal constructions often requires expert knowledge or manual trial and error. We introduce TACO-Graph, a decision tree framework for learning which graph construction choices are most effective for a given dataset and task. Specifically, we focus on text-attributed graphs (TAGs) constructed from multi-attribute tabular data. TACO-Graph enumerates valid TAG representations, scores each using fast proxy evaluation on small representative subsets, and trains an interpretable decision tree on those scores. Across five datasets spanning citation networks, product reviews, and bibliographic relationships, our decision trees predict construction quality on separately drawn evaluation samples with mean Spearman $\rho$ above $0.9$ for node classification and above $0.8$ for GraphRAG retrieval. Our trees systematically identify which constructions perform better across both GNN and GraphRAG tasks, and the full pipeline is released as an open-source library to support evaluation on new tasks and datasets at https://github.com/AmiriHayes/text_attributed_graphs. TACO-Graph provides a systematic and interpretable guide for determining how to convert tabular data to text-attributed graphs for downstream tasks.
