new Sharpness-Aware Muon: Matrix-Aware Optimization with Momentum-Guided Perturbations

Authors: Zhiwei Zhai, Wenjing Yan, Ying-Jun Angela Zhang

Abstract: Muon is a matrix-aware optimizer that combines momentum with orthogonalization to produce structured update directions with strong empirical behavior. However, it only shapes the geometry of the update itself and does not explicitly control neighborhood worst-case loss or bias training toward flat regions, which are often linked to stronger generalization. To address this limitation, we introduce the sharpness-aware formulation of Muon. We first develop \textsc{MuSAM}, which incorporates Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM)-style perturbation into Muon's orthogonalized update and establishes a principled bridge between sharpness-aware training and matrix-aware optimization. We then show that this formulation faces an efficiency--stability tension: constructing the perturbation requires an extra gradient evaluation, while the perturbation itself is generated from a single minibatch gradient and is therefore noisy. To resolve this issue, we propose \textsc{MoMuSAM}, which turns Muon's momentum from an update variable into a sharpness-probing signal. By using normalized momentum as the perturbation direction, \textsc{MoMuSAM} removes the extra clean-gradient computation and replaces the single-batch perturbation proxy with a temporally aggregated one. We prove that both \textsc{MuSAM} and \textsc{MoMuSAM} attain the same asymptotic first-order stationarity order, and further show that \textsc{MoMuSAM} yields a more stable stochastic linearization than \textsc{MuSAM}. Experiments on standard benchmarks demonstrate that sharpness awareness consistently improves Muon.

new When Is a Relation Worth Trusting? Manipulation Costs, Exposure, and the Trust Trap in Multi-Relational Graph Learning

Authors: Vincess Dongmo, Astride Melvin Fokam Ninyim

Abstract: Multi-relational graph detectors learn from clean data how much to trust each relation, but an adversary pays a different price to manipulate each relation and each action; taking such costs as given, we ask when using a relation is worse than ignoring it. Our first main result proves that degree gating strictly beats every selection or reweighting of a relation when part of the population is isolated on it and the attacker cannot afford to open the gate, and that this window is sharp; on real graphs, validated gating gains exactly on the relations that meet these conditions and nowhere else. Our second shows that a per-node exposure bound, which extends to message passing, tells a platform which relation to harden: exposure per unit cost, computed without any attack, picks the relation whose hardening raises the exact trap threshold most in 15 of 16 cases, against at most 7 for informativeness, cheapness, or an analysis with equal edit costs. Supporting tools give exact worst cases for linear relational models, certified brackets for R-GCN and tree ensembles, and a finite-sample guarantee for adversarial validation, under which clean-trained graph detectors fall below their graph-free counterparts in 58 of 90 settings on six graphs. A selection protocol built on these results is never significantly worse than ignoring the graph; with at most four relations it mostly abstains, as validation alone would, and its cost-aware proposals help when relations are many, so far shown on synthetic graphs. Without cost information, the safe choice is to abstain.

new Your Policy Is Secretly a Harness: The Decision Geometry of Agent Interfaces

Authors: Jian Mu, Ruibo Shen, Yao Shu

Abstract: Language model agents are post-trained and deployed through a harness, the prompts, tools, and control logic that present a task to the model and execute its outputs. Harnesses are often revised and judged by how an already trained model performs under them, which assumes that what the model learned under one harness carries over to the next. We ask what a policy learns from its harness and what survives when the harness changes. Under KL-regularized control, we show that the log odds of an optimal policy recover how strongly its harness favors each action over the others, so a trained policy carries the preferences of its harness. Deployed return then equals the ceiling of the harness, the best return it offers, minus the mismatch of the policy, the return lost to its own suboptimal decisions. Training changes only the mismatch, while changing the harness moves both terms. Comparing harnesses with a fixed model therefore mixes what each harness offers with how well that model uses it. In MiniWoB++, post-training makes a model's preferences specific to its training harness. Reversing the order of the observation fields leaves every optimal decision unchanged yet lowers a trained model's success from 85.0% to 19.2%. Conversely, every new harness that a fixed-model comparison rejects but that raises the ceiling outperforms the original agent after further training under it, and ceiling and mismatch measured beforehand predict these gains. For coding-agent harnesses on Terminal-Bench Lite, ceiling minus mismatch estimated with a stronger model as a stand-in for the optimum reproduces the accuracy ranking, while either term alone does not. Code is available at https://github.com/2020-qqtcg/HRP.

URLs: https://github.com/2020-qqtcg/HRP.

new Where Should the Evaluation Budget Go in LLM-Driven Agent Scaffold Evolution?

Authors: Sichun Luo, Yi Huang, Mukai Li, Haochen Luo, Guanzhi Deng, Haibo Wang, Jian Xu, Zefa Hu, Junlan Feng, Qi Liu

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-driven agent scaffold evolution improves agent workflows through iterative candidate generation and evaluation. Evaluation both guides subsequent search and determines the final scaffold, but evaluating every candidate on the full task set incurs substantial execution costs. We investigate how evaluation budgets can be allocated across these two roles. Our experiments show that sparse feedback can sustain productive evolution, while additional targeted evaluation improves final selection. Replay analyses further show that shared informative items and a common scoring reference support comparisons among closely matched candidates. Motivated by these findings, we propose \method, an evaluation allocation framework that combines sparse search-time feedback with targeted final confirmation. A short fully evaluated warm-up establishes a shared informative core and calibrates a response model whose item parameters remain fixed during search. After search, \method refits the model and applies paired successive halving, reusing accumulated observations and executing only missing evaluations. Across four benchmarks and two evolution frameworks, \method achieves higher mean performance than full evaluation in most settings while using approximately half as many evaluation cells.

new Bayesian Inference of Expensive Simulators: Survey and Comparison of Design Choices

Authors: \v{S}imon Sold\'at, V\'aclav \v{S}m\'idl

Abstract: We present a survey of approaches to Bayesian inference with simulator forward models, focusing on settings with prohibitively expensive simulators. In this domain, Bayesian optimization-based methods have emerged as the most promising approach. We unify existing methods into a general framework, enabling consistent reasoning about shared algorithmic structure across the fragmented literature. Within this framework, we identify the primary design choices and perform a systematic empirical comparison of their effects on inference efficiency in terms of required simulation budget. A key result of our study is that inference efficiency is often dominated by the choice of surrogate-model response variable, as it directly governs the complexity of the modeled function. Specifically, we demonstrate that the prevalent scalar log-likelihood is a poor response variable, as it discards crucial information. Based on this insight, we introduce the concept of custom \emph{proxy variables}: transformations of simulator outputs engineered to retain more information and provide smoother response surfaces, substantially improving inference efficiency over standard response variables. We provide actionable guidance and a numerically robust Julia package to facilitate practical adoption of Bayesian optimization for simulator inverse problems.

new Structured Representation Probing for Risk Anticipation in End-to-End Autonomous Driving

Authors: Jiawei Liang, Tianrui Lou, Le Yang, Xianghao Jiao, Wei Wang, Xiaochun Cao

Abstract: End-to-end driving policies form structured internal representations of traffic, ego behavior, and road constraints. These representations offer a monitoring interface for anticipating failures, complementing risk signals from trajectories, controls, and manually designed features. We introduce the Structured Representation Probe (SRP), a lightweight monitor that reads actor, ego, and map representations from a frozen policy. SRP learns a separate linear risk direction for each source and aggregates element scores through cardinality-normalized log-mean-exp pooling, retaining sensitivity to salient evidence while reducing dependence on token count. Event-window supervision links the readout to upcoming hard failures, and auxiliary event type heads jointly train the source directions across failure modes. Online alerting uses a single generic score without modifying the driving policy. Bench2Drive experiments on DriveTransformer, UniAD, and VAD show higher frame-level PR-AUC and ROC-AUC than the evaluated runtime baselines. On DriveTransformer at a horizon of 2 seconds, ROC-AUC reaches 0.833 versus 0.715 for temporal consistency, with 2.67% readout runtime overhead. The trained probes also retain the ability to distinguish impending failures on new routes.

new WCC: A Learning-Based Framework for Deciding When to Apply Cooperative Coevolution in Large-Scale Optimization

Authors: Wenjie Qiu, Hepei Li, Jianfeng Wang, Jun Zhang, Yue-Jiao Gong

Abstract: Cooperative Coevolution (CC) and Non-Decomposition Algorithms (NDAs) are two primary approaches for large-scale global optimization (LSGO). Although both have achieved success, they exhibit markedly different performance across problem types and each faces approach-specific limitations, leaving the question of "when to use CC" unresolved. We address this gap with WCC, a novel learning-based framework that dynamically decides when to deploy CC in LSGO. WCC employs a streamlined design for efficient adaptive learning. We formulate the decision as a Markov decision process, designing a targeted feature set, a robust reward, and a tailored network architecture to provide the agent with rich information about the problem state and optimization progress. Our auto-regressive policy enables WCC to perform fine-grained, dynamic switching between CC and NDAs while conditionally allocating the computational budget without expert specifications. WCC shows strong competitiveness and superior generalization across problems, optimization horizons, and baseline optimizers. It realizes a "train-once and reuse" paradigm under a low budget, significantly curbing LSGO cost. Additionally, in-depth analyses yield insights not captured by existing theory, offering practical guidance on when and how to effectively deploy CC.

new CoS-VQ: Coordinated Shared-Scale Vector Quantization for Large Language Models

Authors: Zukang Xu, Zhixiong Zhao, Xing Hu, Zhe Jiang, Xin Si, Dawei Yang

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) require substantial memory, motivating low-bit weight quantization for efficient deployment. Existing vector quantization (VQ) methods often improve accuracy by increasing the effective coding dimension, which can complicate independent lookup and local codeword updates. We propose CoS-VQ (Coordinated Shared-Scale Vector Quantization), which preserves low-dimensional codewords while jointly optimizing hard indices and their shared scale across positions. The key challenge is scale-induced coupling: the optimal scale depends on the entire codeword combination and, in turn, changes candidate rankings across positions, so pointwise updates can stall even after scale re-optimization. CoS-VQ collects candidates at multiple probe scales and constructs a tangent separable upper bound for the residual-aware quadratic objective. By reducing candidate comparisons to lower envelopes of lines over positive scales, it identifies finitely many representative intervals for joint index--scale updates without exhaustively enumerating codeword combinations. On Qwen3.8-27B, CoS-VQ outperforms QTIP and LLVQ on the evaluated language modeling, coding, and reasoning benchmarks. Ablations from the same initialization further show larger gains from joint updates than from scale-only refinement, highlighting joint selection as an alternative to increasing codeword dimensionality. The code is available at https://github.com/xzktx003/Cos_VQ.

URLs: https://github.com/xzktx003/Cos_VQ.

new Normative Deviation Modeling for EEG-based Depression Detection

Authors: Wan Jiang, Jingqi Cheng, Hui Zhang, Xu Zhao, Xiaojing Chen, Shengeng Tang, Jingjing Wu, Richang Hong

Abstract: Web-enabled digital health services are expanding access to physiological monitoring and mental health screening. However, electroencephalography (EEG)-based major depressive disorder (MDD) detection remains challenging across heterogeneous data sources, where individual variability and acquisition differences may affect disease-related representations. In this work, we investigate the use of healthy normative references for EEG-based MDD detection. Specifically, structural and distributional reference models are independently trained using healthy control (HC) recordings to characterize departures from healthy EEG patterns. We develop two diagnostic approaches that incorporate normative deviations at different representation levels. Scalar-level Normative Deviation-guided Diagnosis (S-NDD) summarizes structural and distributional deviations into scalar scores and integrates them with deep EEG representations. Feature-level Normative Deviation-guided Diagnosis (F-NDD) retains dimension-wise residuals and combines them with deep EEG representations through attention-based aggregation and auxiliary supervision. We also employ target HC-assisted score calibration for cross-source evaluation without updating the trained models. Experiments on two public EEG datasets examine both approaches under subject-independent within-source and bidirectional cross-source settings. The results demonstrate the effectiveness of normative deviation-guided diagnosis across different evaluation settings.

new EmbeddingGemma 2: Unified and Efficient Multimodal Representations

Authors: Henrique Schechter Vera, Sahil Dua, Jovan Andonov, Sara Smoot, Sebastian Russo, Ivan Llanos, Max Matveev, Jae Hyeon Yoo, Kat Black, Shih-Cheng Huang, Josiah Aklilu, Maarten Grootendorst, Omar Sanseviero, Chandramouli Amarnath, Ian Ballantyne, Zhe Li, Daniel Salz, Ibrahim Alabdulmohsin, Ilya Philippov, Kristen Quan, Glenn Cameron, Edouard Yvinec, Luciano Martins, Doug Reid, Sindhu Raghuram Panyam, Weiyi Wang, Hengxuan Ying, Madhuri Shanbhogue, Tanmaya Dabral, Shanfeng Zhang, Juyeong Ji, Renjie Wu, Joe Zou, Xu Chen, Yunan Zhang, Yu-Hui Chen, Matthew Soulanille, Vinod Mamillapalli, Yi-Chun Kuo, Wai Hon Law, Matt Kreileder, Alice Zheng, Suril Shah, Mogan Shieh, Pooja Consul, Alberto Montes, Andreas Hess, Andres Sevilla, Babak Samari, Brendan McCloskey, Brian Potetz, Daniel Cer, Georgios Kathareios, Gustavo Hern\'andez \'Abrego, Hesen Zhang, Ji Ma, Jiaxing Wu, Keegan Mosley, Paul Suganthan, Rui Meng, Shen Gao, Shengcao Cao, Yi-Ting Chen, Yichang Chen, Zhongli Ding, Kendra Byrne, Cormac Brick, Olivier Lacombe, Qin Yin, Tris Warkentin, Armand Joulin, Tom Duerig, Mojtaba Seyedhosseini

Abstract: We present EmbeddingGemma 2, an open multimodal embedding model which natively represents text, images, audio, video, and combinations thereof in a unified space. Our approach introduces improvements such as simplified per-layer embeddings and a multi-stage curriculum with modality and objective progression which ensure strong quality across unimodal, cross-modal, and mixed-modal evaluations. Notably, in comprehensive embedding benchmarks across all modalities, EmbeddingGemma 2 establishes a new Pareto frontier, outperforming both specialist models and models two to twelve times larger. new TRIDENT-Seq: Profile-Matched Prefix-Future Contrast for Label-Efficient Payment Risk Modeling

Authors: Weiyu Han, Haoyu Jiang, Jie Sheng, Ruobing Zuo, Huilin He, Ze Zhao, Xiao Tan, Nairong Zhang, Shuai Chen, Tianyi Zhang

Abstract: Ant International accumulates large volumes of transaction history across users and payment businesses. Confirmed fraud and chargeback outcomes, by contrast, are scarce, delayed, and specific to each business. A single encoder pretrained on unlabeled histories could serve all businesses, but only if pretraining captures the behavior that separates risk. Coarse activity differences can make contrastive pretraining on transaction logs too easy: transaction volume, amount, and timing can distinguish a history's continuation from arbitrary candidates without modeling how payment resources are used. We present TRIDENT-Seq, a transaction foundation model trained with Prefix-Future Contrast (PFC). PFC matches each prefix to a summary of its subsequent behavior among candidates with similar activity profiles; activity prediction and dispersion regularization act on the encoder output. Across three production payment businesses, the pretrained encoder improves average precision (AP) and recall at a 0.5% intervention budget over architecture-matched scratch training in all six business-budget settings. With a fixed validation set, it exceeds full-label scratch training in mean AP and recall using 10% of the training labels on Wallet and 25% on Shopping and SME. Pretraining only on another business's histories still helps. Replacing profile-matched candidates with random ones cuts recall from 24.62% to 5.64%. In Ant International's deployment evaluation, recorded amount-based chargeback and overall loss rates are 37.7% and 37.9% lower than those of the previous XGBoost-based system under the same intervention budget.

new Euclidean Crowding-Driven Adaptive Euclidean-Hyperbolic Graph Learning

Authors: Hao Chen, Jian Sun, Chuan Shi, Hongying Zhang

Abstract: Hyperbolic graph representation learning has emerged as a powerful paradigm for modeling hierarchical data. Unlike Euclidean space, which exhibits polynomial volume growth, hyperbolic space grows exponentially with radius, naturally accommodating tree-like structures without the geometric distortion known as Euclidean crowding. Despite strong empirical performance, the theoretical mechanisms underlying hyperbolic embeddings remain poorly understood. In this work, we provide a geometric quantifcation research for Euclidean crowding, a generalization-theoretic explanation for the superiority of hyperbolic space on hierarchical data, and further develop an adaptive Euclidean-hyperbolic fusion learning framework toward bridging this gap. Specifically, we \textbf{first} establish a geometric quantification of Euclidean crowding by analyzing layer-wise minimum separation in node embeddings, proving that fixed-dimensional Euclidean embeddings of tree-like graphs inevitably exhibit layer-wise crowding that leads to weaker margin-based generalization bounds. \textbf{Second}, we provide a generalization-bound for the superiority of hyperbolic geometry on hierarchical data. \textbf{Third}, we propose EH-Fusion, a geometry-adaptive framework that lifts Euclidean GNN representations into hyperbolic space via exponential mapping and adaptively fuses both geometries based on node-level neighborhood expansion patterns. Extensive experiments validate our theoretical insights and demonstrate the effectiveness of EH-Fusion on benchmark datasets.

new Channel Selection for Mixture-of-Experts Decoding Needs No Calibration Data

Authors: Geunhyoung Kim, Sung Ju Hwang

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models are bottlenecked by loading routed-expert weights from memory during interactive decoding at small batch sizes. Because most intermediate channels in a routed expert contribute little to any single token, channel selectors predict which channels matter and read only their weights, typically keeping channels above a threshold calibrated on offline data. We show that channel selection needs no calibration data. First, a calibrated threshold does not fix how much a selector reads, because the number of channels passing it varies across inputs; Prox with thresholds calibrated for an 18% read budget actually reads only 12.9% on average across our benchmarks. Second, top-k keeps each token's k highest-scoring channels and needs no calibration data. At the calibrated budget, a selector's top-k version matches or beats its threshold version on the same score in 40 of 43 comparisons at 13% and 18% budgets, including all 23 on generation tasks. The top-k versions also avoid the repetition-loop collapse that calibrated thresholds cause on Gemma 4. Calibrating a threshold thus provides neither of the two things selectors use it for: a known read budget and a consistent accuracy gain at that budget. We furthermore introduce a reader built entirely from the model's weights, which screens channels with a ternary sketch and recovers the skipped ones with a low-rank map. Across three post-trained MoE models, our reader retains 98.1% of full-model accuracy at 17.8% of routed-expert reads, with Triton kernels running MoE blocks 1.60 to 1.90x faster than optimized vLLM at batch one and up to 2.69x at larger batches on the same three models.

new Para-Gaussian Approximations of Random Deep Neural Networks

Authors: Mihai Nica, Janosch Ortmann

Abstract: It is known that randomly initialized neural networks have Gaussian behaviour in the $n\to \infty$ limit. Unfortunately, this approximation can be poor for finite $n$ and gets worse as one goes deeper into the network. In this work, we develop a more accurate approximation that is \emph{slightly} non-Gaussian, which we call the para-Gaussian distribution and is constructed from the Hermite polynomials. We show how this para-Gaussian approximation can lead to a practical recursive method to approximate the deeper layers of the network. Finally, we apply this theory to linear neural networks (where things can be solved exactly) and to ReLU neural networks (where things can be approximated).

new How Many and How Much? Contrastive Learning with Negative Samples for Knowledge Graph Embedding

Authors: Sooho Moon, Yunyong Ko

Abstract: Knowledge graph embedding (KGE), a fundamental technique for knowledge-centric Web and KG-augmented AI applications, is commonly trained through contrastive learning with negative samples to learn discriminative representations. Despite its empirical success, two crucial questions regarding negative samples remain underexplored: (Q1) how many negative samples should be used for each positive sample and (Q2) how much should each negative sample contribute to learning? To answer these questions, we propose RADAR, a novel contrastive learning framework for KGE models with two effective strategies, respectively: (1) Risk-aware negative budget allocation quantifies false-negative sampling risk by modeling an open-world KG as a stochastic partial observation of its closed-world counterpart and adaptively assigns a negative budget to each positive sample based on its risk; (2) Distribution-calibrated negative weighting assigns negative weights by calibrating the weighting strength for each positive based on two complementary characteristics of the negative-score distribution: dispersion and concentration. Extensive experiments on 3 real-world KGs with 4 KGE models reveal that RADAR achieves the best KGE performance on average across all model--KG combinations and reduces sampled false negatives by over 90% compared with conventional fixed-budget methods.

new LinMix: Data Mixing as Linear Domain Attribution

Authors: Hubert Baniecki, Przemyslaw Biecek, Martin Pawelczyk

Abstract: Language model performance depends strongly on the mixture of domains in the pretraining data corpus. Popular data mixing methods select these domain weights by training many small proxy models on random mixes and then fitting a regression model to predict performance from the mix. We recognize this practice as a domain attribution problem, where the regression measures each domain's contribution to the target metric. Through this lens, the standard Dirichlet sampling of proxy data mixes is a poor regression design that requires tuning and cannot support a linear fit for hundreds of domains. Building on classical optimal experimental design, we introduce LinMix, which samples mixes at the vertices of the capped simplex so that each domain is at its cap or absent. This hyperparameter-free vertex sampling decorrelates domain effects, enabling an interpretable linear model whose coefficients are the domain attributions. The optimal mix given by LinMix provably keeps only domains with positive attribution values and requires no search over candidate mixes. We further establish the optimality of vertex sampling, and prove that Dirichlet sampling inflates the attribution error when its mixes concentrate around the prior data mix. Pretraining experiments with 1B-parameter OLMo2 and 4B Qwen3.5 language models show that LinMix outperforms Dirichlet-based data mixing across reasoning, code, and math benchmarks, with relative gains of 46% on MBPP and 14% on GSM8K. At 288 fine-grained domains, only vertex sampling supports a linear fit, and LinMix improves benchmark accuracy by 11% on average over the best nonlinear baseline.

new Displacement Moments and Path-Terminal Separation in Brownian Interpolant Sampling

Authors: Zhong Han

Abstract: Brownian stochastic interpolants connect paired endpoint samples, yet their sampling error can depend on displacement moments even when neither endpoint has a first moment. For arbitrary Borel couplings, we prove early-stopped, target-to-output KL guarantees for standard Euler sampling from the true source. A fourth displacement moment gives an explicit $O(N^{-1})$ bound; a second moment suffices for fixed-law convergence as drift error vanishes. For one fixed coupling with no displacement moment above two, original bridge-path KL stays of order one, comparison-path KL is of order $1/\log N$, and terminal KL is at most $C/N^2$ on the same grid. The path decomposition identifies the information about the source discarded by Markov projection. When the original bridge is Markov, current-state Euler sampling instead has a second-moment $O((M_2+d)/N)$ bound; finite-entropy Schroedinger bridges for the probability Brownian reference are included. We also construct a current-state learner with expected output KL consistency from iid endpoint pairs. Numerical diagnostics illustrate the distinct errors. Conditional diffusion identities specify a restoration interface, while image controls find no iterative advantage over cheap probabilistic predictors. Uniform terminal rates for general second-moment couplings remain open.

new Bayes-Adaptive MDPs for Rollout Allocation to Enhance GRPO Training

Authors: Faeze Moradi Kalarde, Armin Toroghi, Nam Ho Koh, Doo Hwan Hwang, Seungjin Kim, Kee-Eung Kim, Scott Sanner

Abstract: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) has demonstrated strong performance in LLM post-training. However, it allocates a uniform rollout budget to every training question, ignoring variations in question difficulty. As a result, highly difficult questions may receive too few rollouts to generate informative positive reward signals, limiting their contribution to training, while moderately difficult questions often receive more rollouts than necessary. In this work, we formulate the problem of difficulty-aware rollout budget allocation to maximize training utility under the same total rollout budget as standard GRPO. We propose AdaRoll-GRPO, which reformulates rollout allocation as a sequential decision-making problem and leverages a Bayes-adaptive Markov decision process (MDP) to learn an adaptive allocation policy. The learned policy is computed offline and applied during GRPO training without introducing additional online optimization overhead. Extensive experiments on multiple reasoning datasets and LLM architectures demonstrate that AdaRoll-GRPO consistently improves GRPO training performance under a fixed rollout budget, highlighting the effectiveness of adaptive rollout allocation for GRPO in LLM post-training.

new When Visual Evidence Is Insufficient: How Instructions, Post-Training, and Internal Activations Shape Refusal in Multimodal Language Models

Authors: Ananth K Suresh, Aveen Dayal, Gouthaman KV, Aditya Pola, Vineeth N Balasubramanian

Abstract: For reliable deployment, multimodal large language models (MLLMs) should recognize when an image provides insufficient evidence to answer a question and withhold unsupported answers. Yet refusal alone is not necessarily evidence of such recognition: models may refuse because of unrelated safety constraints or without identifying the missing evidence. We study how refusal on visually unanswerable questions is shaped by three sources of control: inference-time instructions, post-training, and internal activation interventions. Across three MLLMs on the MoHoBench benchmark, we systematically manipulate the meaning and placement of refusal instructions, compare supervised and preference-based post-training, and intervene on a refusal-related activation direction derived from naturally answered and refused examples. We find that refusal is highly sensitive to both the meaning and placement of instructions, with model-specific changes at the sample level that are obscured by aggregate refusal rates. Evidence-aware and general refusal instructions have stronger effects than an intentionally mismatched safety instruction. Post-training produces larger increases in refusal, but can also induce responses that combine refusal language with attempted answers. In contrast, activation intervention produces smaller yet transferable changes across prompt conditions, without evidence of indiscriminate refusal on tested visually answerable samples. These findings show that refusal is jointly shaped by external instructions, post-training, and internal representations, highlighting the need to disentangle evidence recognition from a model's learned propensity to refuse.

new CRISP: Co-designing Pruning and Scheduling for Efficient MoE Inference on Edge Servers

Authors: Congwei Zhang, Youming Tao, Yifei Zou, Yu Yang, Ruirui Zhang, Xiaoyu Zhang, Falko Dressler, Xiuzhen Cheng, Dongxiao Yu

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) inference on memory-constrained edge platforms is often dominated by expert-weight movement rather than computation. Although each token activates only a small subset of experts, routing is input-dependent, so all experts must remain accessible and memory movement dominates latency. Existing approaches such as uniform pruning and expert dropping overlook two properties of MoE execution: expert combinations are highly skewed in frequency, and experts contribute asymmetrically within each combination. We present CRISP, a combination-aware framework that co-designs pruning and runtime scheduling for efficient MoE serving on memory-constrained edge platforms. CRISP reorders neurons by importance so one stored expert can support multiple execution widths, assigns differentiated execution to hot, warm, and cold combinations based on frequency and expert role, and exploits heterogeneous widths to overlap expert loading with computation. Across Mixtral-8x7B and DeepSeek-MoE-16B, CRISP improves throughput by up to $2.1\times$ at the same memory budget while limiting accuracy loss to 3.6\% relative to full-width inference. Our results highlight that efficient edge MoE serving requires jointly optimizing which expert capacity is preserved and how expert execution is scheduled.

new Interpretable Meta-Policies for Neural Decision Making

Authors: Raban Emunds, Jannis Bl\"uml, Quentin Delfosse, Kristian Kersting

Abstract: Deep Reinforcement Learning remains difficult to interpret and align due to the black-box nature of neural networks. While (neuro-)symbolic approaches improve transparency by incorporating symbolic reasoning, applying them directly to low-level actions results in overly complex, unreadable policies. We introduce NEXUS, a hierarchical RL framework that resolves this tension by combining neural skills with symbolic meta-policies. By shifting transparent reasoning to high-level skills rather than atomic actions, NEXUS drastically reduces symbolic complexity without sacrificing environment performance. Object-centric observations enable high-level reasoning and the design of reward functions and meta-policies by interaction with LLMs. We demonstrate that NEXUS agents learn disentangled skills, are interpretable regarding high-level goals, are better aligned to goals described in the game manual, less prone to reward hacking and more robust to game simplifications than deep RL. Finally, we isolate and evaluate the impact of different meta-policy architectures (neural, symbolic, neuro-symbolic) on environmental return and aligned task performance.

new An Input Skip Can Reparameterize a VAE Without Improving the Generator

Authors: Kevin Lee

Abstract: An input skip can reduce reconstruction error while leaving a model's distribution of new samples unchanged. For a Gaussian variational autoencoder (VAE) with constant input skip $0\leq\alpha<1$, a model without a skip can be written in skip form by multiplying its decoder output by $b=1-\alpha$ and its noise variance by $b^2$. The distribution of observations and latent variables and its exact generative scores stay the same, while reconstruction mean squared error (MSE) is multiplied by $b^2$. Separate training can produce different generators because optimization depends on the parameter coordinates. In a Gaussian experiment with known density, changing skip and observation variance together reduces MSE but worsens energy score and the estimate of negative log density from importance sampling. On seven annual Deep-ocean Assessment and Reporting of Tsunamis (DART) records, energy score differences at the same observation variance have both signs on four original records and favor the skip on three records from 2018. Probabilistic principal component analysis (PPCA) has lower mean energy scores than every neural DART fit. With the encoder and decoder kept the same, estimating observation variance from training reconstruction error can also worsen energy score. Assessing generation requires more than reconstruction error.

new Follow the Gradient: Tracing Safety Degradation Back to Training Data

Authors: Saket Reddy, Amro Abbas, Anshuman Suri, Ari Morcos, David Schwab, Pratyush Maini

Abstract: Fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs) on instruction data can quietly erode their safety, even when no training example looks harmful. Because this safety-degrading data looks benign, standard content filters struggle to catch it. We tackle this problem by developing SimpleLoRIF, a scalable gradient embedding method that identifies fine-tuning examples whose gradients align with unsafe behavior. By simplifying a low-rank attribution method and restricting backpropagation to the final four transformer blocks, SimpleLoRIF can embed datasets on the order of a million examples, boosting gradient attribution from a small-scale analysis tool into a practical filter for full post-training datasets. We first validate it on emergent misalignment, where we find that gradients can connect insecure-code training data to the broadly harmful behaviors that appear after fine-tuning, with AUC as high as 0.865 in the gradient space of misaligned models. We then use SimpleLoRIF to build SafeTulu, a version of Tulu 3 with the 20% of examples most gradient-aligned with unsafe responses removed. Training on SafeTulu reduces GCG attack success rate by up to 22% across Qwen, Llama, and Olmo, outperforming both LLM-as-a-judge and prior gradient-based filtering, while largely preserving or even improving capability. Finally, we analyzed the removed data and identify possibly under-studied sources of safety degradation, such as tool-execution failures and narrative arithmetic. We release SafeTulu along with a Hugging Face Space that lets researchers build their own customized versions of SafeTulu to fine-tune on, such as by changing the removal fraction or filtering only certain subsets.

new Orthogonal and Parametrized Multi-Attribute Steering of LLMs

Authors: Remy Ogasawara, Cullen Anderson, Narmeen Fatimah Oozeer, Foad Namjoo, Amir Abdullah, Jens Kristian Refsgaard Schou, Jeff M. Phillips

Abstract: Activation steering offers an effective and efficient way to modify an LLM's behavior at inference time. However, it remains unclear how to apply steering such that the signal is consistently strong and the steering strength has a universally interpretable meaning. We address this first by identifying the most steerable interval for each steering vector and rescaling the steering range accordingly. Building on this, we introduce a more interpretable method for steering, called parameterized steering which instead of pushing activations in a direction, it sets them at the desired trait strength. Next, with multi-trait steering, we find that naively combining vectors causes them to interfere with one another. In response, we develop a method to orthogonalize the vectors; it decouples these traits, allowing a model to exhibit one behavior without inducing the others, thus enabling multiple traits to be steered simultaneously.

new The Structure Gap: When More Data Cannot Replace Structured Reasoning

Authors: Kheer Sagar Patel, Mojtaba Soltanalian

Abstract: A persistent belief in modern AI is that sufficiently large learners trained on sufficiently much data can make explicit domain knowledge optional. This paper examines a limitation of that belief. We define structure-encoded problems: tasks whose input-output map is determined, or sharply constrained, by known structure such as a solver, symmetry group, graph, physical law, measurement operator, grammar, combinatorial search space, or optimization objective. For such tasks, the central question is not expressivity--a dense neural network may contain the desired map--but finite-data identification, extrapolation, and certification. We introduce the structure gap: the finite-resource gap between a method that receives the governing structure and a learner that receives only examples. The strongest useful form of the claim is asymptotic rather than absolute: structure-agnostic learning may approach a structured solver as coverage grows, but every finite dataset can leave residual uncertainty that the structure resolves by construction. We prove this exactly for a Gaussian inverse problem: the known-operator Wiener solver attains Bayes risk, while the dense empirical learner in the correct linear class has persistent expected excess risk. We also prove a cell-coverage lower bound and an unobserved-orbit non-identifiability result. Controlled experiments in known-operator inversion, sparse recovery, and graph reachability with size extrapolation instantiate the mechanisms. When validated structure is available, it can constrain the hypothesis space before learning begins, leaving learning to address components the structure does not specify.

new GTFormer: A Syntax-Free Graph Network Alternative to Transformers for 8192$\times$ Length Extrapolation in Linear Time

Authors: Elisabeth Fink

Abstract: Self-attention mechanisms have established the Transformer as the dominant backbone across natural language processing, source code intelligence, and automated theorem proving. However, all-to-all pairwise token comparison enforces a quadratic $\mathcal{O}(N^2)$ compute and memory barrier alongside severe softmax attention dilution as sequence length $N$ scales beyond the training horizon. Inspired by recent advances in algebraic graph learning (\textbf{WPNet}), this work introduces \textbf{GTFormer}, a syntax-free hierarchical Graph Neural Network (GNN) that replaces flat self-attention for general long-context sequence modeling. GTFormer organizes raw token streams into a bounded-degree hierarchy combining a balanced binary reduction tree ($\lceil \log_2 N \rceil$ depth), horizontal boundary-bridging shift edges, random expander shortcuts, multi-channel convex highway gating, and a top-down broadcast pass. Across eight long-context code, log, and multi-hop retrieval benchmarks trained exclusively on sequences of length $N \le 512$, GTFormer extrapolates zero-shot to $N = 4{,}194{,}304$ ($8192\times$) on a single commodity GPU in linear $\mathcal{O}(N)$ memory, maintaining near-perfect accuracy where standard and RoPE Transformers suffer from out-of-distribution attention collapse and out-of-memory exhaustion.

new Collective Dynamics of Self-Reinforcement: How Cross-Task Coupling Drives Policy Collapse in Unsupervised RLVR

Authors: Peize Ding

Abstract: Label-free reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) rewards a language model for agreeing with its own majority vote. It improves reasoning at first but eventually collapses: the self-assessed reward goes to one while accuracy falls, often until the model gives the same answer to every problem. The collapse appears only when training uses many problems. We explain this dataset-size dependence as a collective instability. In a solvable many-problem model in which problems share parameters, a population whose problems prefer different answers amplifies any shared perturbation for as long as the population-averaged slope $\langle F'\rangle$ of the single-problem self-reinforcement drift $F$ is positive. This holds while problems are still uncertain about their answers. Per-problem sharpening, slowed by $1/N$ for $N$ training problems, races against the shared mode, which grows at a rate $\propto\lambda^2$ independent of $N$, where $\lambda$ is the strength of the shared coupling. This yields a critical number of problems $N_c\simeq\ln(1/\delta)/(\lambda^2G)$, where $\delta$ seeds the shared mode and $G$ decreases with the initial confidence. Simulations of the model confirm the predictions: a sharp transition, $N_c\propto\lambda^{-2}$ up to a logarithmic correction, a single critical amplification $\lambda^2NG$ across couplings, confidence profiles and training variants, a logarithmic dependence on batch size, and no collapse for confident initial models. A kernel version of the theory identifies a protective coupling (generalization of sharpening) and a marginal-entropy bonus that stabilizes the uniform collective mode. In label-free RL of a small Transformer, removing the shared direction prevents collapse entirely, restoring it causes collapse, and collapse grows with training-set size and base-model entropy.

new When uncertainty quantification fails: structural vulnerabilities and corrections for selective prediction under clinical class imbalance

Authors: Walter Endrizzi, Flavio Ragni, Raffaele Marchesi, Stefano Bovo, Monica Moroni, Giuseppe Jurman, Venet Osmani

Abstract: Background. Selective prediction systems improve the safety of clinical artificial intelligence by deferring ambiguous cases to human experts while automating high-confidence decisions. However, this safety mechanism relies entirely on the premise that uncertainty scores accurately reflect clinical ambiguity. In prognostic tasks characterized by severe class imbalance, where the clinical outcome of interest is a minority, this assumption breaks down. Standard uncertainty measures cease to capture clinically aligned ambiguity; instead, they become a proxy for class membership, systematically penalizing minority patients regardless of their individual predictive clarity. Results. Across 56 synthetic conditions and three neurodegenerative cohorts spanning severe class imbalance to near-balanced conditions (multiple sclerosis, Parkinson's Disease, Alzheimer's Disease), our framework consistently maintained bounded, balanced risk across trajectories. In contrast, global metrics either collapsed minority Sensitivity or protected the minority at the expense of majority patients. Applying CCR with standard entropy fails to protect the minority because it breaks within-class monotonicity when tau=0.5. Combining CCR with threshold-aware metrics restores strict monotonicity, correctly identifies ambiguous patients, and safely mitigates minority exclusion. Conclusions. Under clinical class imbalance, standard uncertainty quantification measures dataset sparsity rather than clinical ambiguity, creating a systemic illusion of safety. This bias is a correctable structural flaw. Clinical pipelines deploying selective prediction under imbalance should adopt threshold-aligned uncertainty measures and class-stratified evaluation. This reduces class-specific risk imbalance, supporting safer selective prediction under the evaluated conditions without silently excluding the most vulnerable patients.

new Pneuma: A Large-Scale Real-World Time Series Benchmark for Commercial Aircraft Predictive Maintenance

Authors: Jiafeng Lin, Yutian Zhang, Huan Zhang, Yuxuan Wang, Jinyan Feng, Yuhui Liu, Zhongyi Pei, Jianmin Wang

Abstract: Predictive maintenance is vital for commercial aviation, yet progress remains hindered by the lack of public, large-scale real-world datasets. As a result, most methods are developed and evaluated on proprietary data, limiting reproducibility. We introduce Pneuma, the first publicly available large-scale benchmark for predictive maintenance using onboard time series from in-service commercial aircraft. Built around bleed air systems, Pneuma contains over 920 million multivariate time steps across four aircraft types and 9 verified fault scenarios, directly aligned with real maintenance logs. The dataset exhibits key real-world challenges, including substantial class imbalance, subtle fault evolution patterns, and significant variance across aircraft configurations. We structure the benchmark around three tasks: multivariate forecasting, supervised fault classification, and unsupervised anomaly detection, with labels derived from actual maintenance records. Under unified settings, we evaluate over 25 models from four methodological families on 9 carefully curated subsets. The results reveal that even strong contemporary models struggle with subtle fault signatures. Further analyses show consistent gains from additional in-context examples and multivariate inference, while forecasting quality is strongly associated with anomaly detection performance. Together, these findings provide practical insights into effectively modeling complex real-world aviation time series. We hope Pneuma will serve as a rigorous and reproducible foundation for advancing data-driven predictive maintenance toward real-world deployment in commercial aviation.

new Gym-Anything-RT: Scaling Real-Time Environments for Vision-Language Agents

Authors: Pranjal Aggarwal, Sean Welleck

Abstract: AI agents have made substantial progress in domains where they have a non-trivial amount of time to deliberate between actions, such as mathematics, software engineering, and performing tasks on desktop applications. It is less clear how such models fare in settings that demand real-time interaction, such as playing a video game or operating a physical device. In these settings, the environment state may evolve as the agent is reasoning, or even as the underlying model is performing a forward pass. To gain insight into the real-time interaction capabilities of vision-language agents, we introduce Gym-Anything-RT. Our key idea is to construct visual minigames that demand a portfolio of capabilities required for interactive real-time settings. Gym-Anything-RT uses an agentic pipeline to automatically generate such environments based on a taxonomy of six capabilities required for real-time interaction, including the ability to perceive the environment and act under an evolving environment state. A key feature of Gym-Anything-RT is that it allows for controlled comparison of real-time interaction capabilities: we can run the same minigame in real time, or pause or slow down the environment to relax the requirement for real-time actions. We use Gym-Anything-RT to construct 100 real-time minigames and evaluate AI agents on them. We find large capability gaps in real-time interaction. For example, GPT-6 Astra (xHigh) achieves a pass rate of only 9.0% on the real-time minigames, which rises to 75.0% when the environment is paused. We also identify intriguing behaviors by the most powerful frontier agents: for instance, when given the ability to write code, these agents write code that bypasses the need to perceive and act in real time. We publicly release Gym-Anything-RT and the generated minigames to spur further research on AI agents in real-time interactive settings.

new LCM-Promo: An LLM-Based Uplift Model for Promotion Allocation

new Understanding the limits of latent world models

Authors: Rodrigo Gonzalez Laiz, Steffen Schneider

Abstract: World models are widely proposed as core components for enabling agents to reason over long temporal and spatial horizons. Yet current evaluations often emphasize task performance while providing limited insight into whether learned world models acquire predictive latent dynamics that reflect the underlying structure of the environment. new GAS-PFN: Graph-Aware Spatiotemporal Prior-Data Fitted Networks for Zero-Shot Traffic Forecasting

Authors: Joseph Natter, Long Vu, Yifan Zhang, Bobin Deng, Kun Suo, Rui Wu

Abstract: Traffic forecasting requires modeling complex temporal dynamics and the spatial dependencies imposed by road topology. Supervised graph-based models achieve strong performance, but are often trained for a particular dataset and sensor graph, making deployment to new or evolving networks costly. Prior-Data Fitted Networks (PFNs) offer a zero-shot alternative by learning in-context inference from synthetic task priors, which provide an explicit mechanism for encoding domain-specific assumptions. Existing forecasting PFNs, however, do not explicitly represent the graph-structured dependencies governing traffic dynamics. To combine graph-aware traffic modeling with zero-shot adaptation, we introduce the Graph-Aware Spatiotemporal Prior-Data Fitted Network (GAS-PFN). GAS-PFN incorporates sampled road topology into its synthetic task prior through random-walk diffusion, teaching the model to learn graph-coupled dynamics during pretraining. Its co-designed inference architecture uses two mask-conditioned passes through a shared Transformer backbone to separately model sensor-specific temporal dynamics and spatiotemporal interactions among neighboring sensors before integrating their representations for prediction. This design enables GAS-PFN to use target-network structure and context without parameter updates. Across six benchmarks at the 90-minute horizon, GAS-PFN reduces MSE by 34.0% on average relative to TimePFN, its closest PFN-based predecessor. It also reduces MSE by 27.7% on average relative to four recent industry-developed general-purpose zero-shot forecasters. In this comparison, all models receive 90 minutes of contiguous recent history, and any additional context is drawn from each dataset's chronological training split. These results indicate that aligning PFN priors and inference architectures with graph structure improves in-context learning for zero-shot traffic forecasting.

new EnvSched: Environment-Aware Scheduling for Rollout-Efficient Agent Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Sihan Yang, Weichen Bai, Ruichuan An, Yiming Cheng, Zhiyuan Feng, Tianyi Jiang, Renye Yan, Ning Ding, Yifan Zhang, Renrui Zhang, Yehui Tang, Wentao Zhang

Abstract: As executable environments for language agents scale, generating and verifying their rollouts becomes increasingly expensive. Under finite rollout budgets, this raises an important question: can data management at the environment level make agentic reinforcement learning (RL) more efficient by organizing, evaluating, and scheduling task instances together with the environments that determine their tools, states, transitions, and verification? To address this question, we introduce EnvSched (Environment-aware Scheduling), an online scheduler that learns from rollout history. EnvSched assigns scheduling value through two complementary modeling choices: exploitation prioritizes tasks likely to provide informative reward differences, while coverage spreads each batch across underobserved environments and interaction patterns. EnvSched organizes environment--task pairs as nodes in a dual-relation graph, with within-environment edges capturing task semantics and shared tool, state, and verifier structure, and cross-environment edges capturing abstract execution and reward topology. By propagating historical rollout statistics along both edge types, it predicts within-task reward variance. These predictions guide a scheduler that allocates each batch to tasks with high predicted reward variance and underobserved environments and tasks. Environment quotas and a similarity penalty further limit concentration and redundancy, yielding informative and diverse batches without additional pilot rollouts. In RL on Qwen3-8B, EnvSched achieves an average score of 48.81 across BFCL-v3, tau-Bench, and ACEBench-Agent after 40 training steps, exceeding the highest score attained by random task selection after 120 steps. It also improves downstream performance across model sizes at the same training budget.

new Scaling Laws for Tool-Use Agents via Controllable Environment Synthesis

Authors: Andrew Szot, Samira Abnar, Omar Attia, Abbas Kazerouni, Michael Kirchhof, Cheng-Yu Hsieh, Raviteja Vemulapalli, Alexander Toshev

Abstract: We explore how to build performant large language model (LLM) tool-use agents that complete complex, multi-step tasks. Specifically, we study how data composition in synthetically generated tool-use environments affects the training of such agents. To do so, we propose the SynthAPI pipeline, which explicitly controls the diversity of generated environments along the axes of tools, tasks, and the databases on which the tools operate. By systematically varying SynthAPI's generation controls in 458,752 tasks across 849 trained models of four different sizes, we derive a scaling law that predicts downstream tool-use agent generalization based on the scale and composition of the synthesized environments. These predictable relationships reveal several lessons for scaling tool-use agent data. As data budgets grow, scaling tool and database diversity becomes more effective than increasing the number of tasks, and increasing data complexity yields larger gains. Scaling according to these findings yields agents that outperform models trained on data from prior work in large-scale tool-use agent and synthetic environment generation across four evaluation benchmarks.

new Attention as Lattice Gauge Theory: Relations Interact Only Through Wilson Loops

Authors: Aakash Sarkar, Ananya Mukherjee, Motoshige Sato

Abstract: Relations compose through the entity they share - Alice is Bob's mother and Bob is Carol's father, so Alice is Carol's grandmother - and the order matters, since a father's mother is not a mother's father; verifying a composed relation means closing the loop. We show that if each entity's coordinate frame may be rotated independently (per-entity frame freedom), three things follow. In the pure-gauge sector, relations that share no cycle cannot interact at all. The only gauge-invariant score of a triangle is a class function of its holonomy $H = T_{ij} T_{jk} T_{ki}$ - a Wilson loop. And the only equivariant update of an edge is $U = \Psi(H)\, T_{ij}$ with $\Psi$ conjugation-equivariant. Edge attention by Wilson loops is therefore not one design among many but the unique form the symmetry permits. Three communities hold pieces of this form: the commuting-operator embeddings of knowledge graphs are its abelian (commuting) sector; AlphaFold's triangle update and the Edge Transformer have its shape - an edge updated through the third vertex - without its constraints; and lattice QCD builds it equivariantly on lattices. We instantiate the equivariant sub-family on general relational graphs as holonomy attention. With no training and composition alone, it infers unobserved $S_4$ relations on sparse graphs at 0.88 with a fifth of the edges corrupted, at or slightly below specialist synchronization algorithms, where every commutative architecture is capped at 0.083; its holonomy score flags corrupted triangles at AUC 0.99 where commuting scores are capped at 0.76 by a second selection rule, and it separates clusters without labels.

new Heads of Security: Learned Attention Gates Improve Prompt Injection Robustness

Authors: Igor Shilov, Xiaoxue Yang, Alexander Panfilov, Evgenii Kortukov, Matthieu Meeus, Peter Romov, Wojciech Samek, Jonas Geiping, Yves-Alexandre de Montjoye

Abstract: We introduce Heads of Security (HoS), a prompt injection defense that enforces instruction-data separation via a small set of learned per-head attention gates. HoS learns how each head should redistribute attention between trusted and untrusted tokens, enabling models to reason over untrusted content without following injected instructions. We evaluate HoS on Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct and GPT-OSS-20B. We compare it against SecAlign++, a state-of-the-art finetuning-based defense, and ASIDE, a defense that, like ours, builds instruction-data separation into the model architecture. We show that HoS offers greater robustness to adaptive attacks, better performance on standard utility benchmarks, and matches top performance on standard agentic prompt injection benchmarks. More specifically, the black-box RL-Hammer attack reaches 97% ASR against GPT-OSS-20B and 85% against SecAlign, but only 10% against HoS. Claudini, a state-of-the-art white-box attack, strengthened against HoS through autoresearch, requires 3 times more FLOPs to reach the same ASR on HoS as it does on GPT-OSS-20B or SecAlign. HoS preserves base-model utility across standard multiple-choice benchmarks and prompting setups, whereas SecAlign and ASIDE degrade in few-shot settings. On agentic benchmarks with static attacks, HoS and SecAlign perform similarly and substantially improve the security-utility trade-off over the base model. ASIDE struggles with tool use, achieving only 10% utility. These results show that prompt injection robustness can be achieved and improved through targeted attention interventions.

new When Should We Intervene at Test Time?

Authors: Jiahao Zhang, Xinling Wen, Liangliang Li, Yu Chen

Abstract: Test-time adaptation helps a deployed model respond to changing inputs, but the same intervention can rescue one batch and damage the next. Updating normalization statistics, for example, can improve corrupted images yet fail when a batch contains mostly one class. Average accuracy hides these failures and cannot tell a model when to use an intervention. Target labels can guide this decision, but they are scarce and some choices are already clear. We introduce PORTAL, a controller that chooses when to deploy an intervention, which inputs to label, and when to stop querying. Each available prediction rule is a candidate, and its predictions are fixed before current feedback arrives. Every acquired label therefore evaluates all candidates on the same input. PORTAL combines these shared comparisons and directs queries toward candidates that can still overtake the current leader. It stops when the remaining labels cannot change the choice or when the possible accuracy gain no longer justifies another query. Our analysis links the required feedback to accuracy gaps and shared mistakes, explaining why some choices need much less evidence. Experiments show that this control turns an intervention that loses accuracy overall into a useful choice on selected batches. It prevents severe failures caused by changes in arrival order and preserves gains across complete corruption streams. When a fixed rule already works well, PORTAL uses little feedback to retain its performance. Periodic label checks reveal when past evidence needs refreshing and help the controller recover as conditions change. The acquired labels also train stronger candidates for future batches, and selection brings nearly all of their accuracy gains into deployment. The same feedback therefore improves both the choice made now and the predictions available to later batches.

cross Learning to Exploit Passive Dynamics for Energy-Efficient Target Hopping of a Spring-Legged Quadcopter

Authors: Ruigang Chen, Qi Zhang, Zhicheng Zhong, Zhuorui Yun, Yizhar Or, Mingyi Liu

Abstract: Combining aerial thrust with spring-loaded hopping makes monopedal quadcopters promising for locomotion over complex terrain, but heuristic proportional-integral-derivative (PID) tuning limits coordination between active thrust and passive contact dynamics. We present a direct estimated-state-to-motor Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) policy that commands four motors without an explicit hopping state machine or low-level attitude PID. Its reward combines Energy-Manifold Shaping for mass-normalized vertical-energy tracking and apex-state anchoring with Efficiency Shaping, which uses a history-aware power estimator to penalize general power use, impose an additional airborne-power cost, and penalize airborne near-stationarity. In representative hardware runs, the PPO-based control stack reduced cycle-averaged measured electrical power by 30.7% and mean total normalized thrust by 49.8% relative to the tuned PID-based control stack, while retaining repeatable commanded-height hopping and more concentrated landings. These observations are consistent with improved use of passive dynamics and reduced measured electrical demand.

cross Reconstructing the Human Development Index at the Individual Level from Survey Microdata: Development and Validation of a Methodology

Authors: Tcharlies Bachmann Schmitz (PX Center, Brazil), Natalia Veronez da Cunha (Universidade do Planalto Catarinense, UNIPLAC), Isabelle Ferreira (Universidade do Planalto Catarinense, UNIPLAC)

Abstract: This study proposes and validates a methodology for reconstructing the Human Development Index (HDI) from individual-level survey microdata, enabling disaggregated estimates for demographic subpopulations while maintaining coherence with the official HDI. cross PROBE: Frontier Coding Agents Find Different Bugs Than Maintainers Fix

Authors: Marco Paina, Mark Niklas M\"uller, Niels M\"undler-Sasahara, Martin Vechev

Abstract: Proactive bug discovery is an important software-engineering problem. Even though coding agents are already widely deployed in this domain, their capabilities remain understudied. Authors: Thanh Dang, Matthew X. Burns, Jiaming Liang

Abstract: We introduce a Stein variational framework for sampling from a target density $p\propto e^{-U}$ when the target potential $U$ is possibly non-smooth. The key idea is to introduce a smoother reference potential $U_0$ and the anchor weight $a=e^{U-U_0}$. The resulting anchored Stein variational gradient descent (SVGD) algorithm is able to target $p\propto e^{-U}$ without computing the gradient of $U$ and include standard SVGD as a special case. Abstract: Action-conditioned world models offer a scalable alternative to costly real-world interaction for improving robot policies. Authors: No\'e Blassel

Abstract: Accelerated dynamics methods, such as parallel replica dynamics, require estimates of the decorrelation time, after which a process trapped in one of its metastable states is distributed according to the quasi-stationary distribution (QSD). In a method proposed by Binder, Leli\`evre and Simpson, decorrelation times are defined by coupling a particle approximation of the non-linear conditioned probability flow of the process with MCMC-inspired diagnostics. cross Symmetry Breaking Is the Price of Finite Capacity

Authors: Maggie Chlon, Leon Chlon

Abstract: If rotating a problem never changes its answer, one might expect its best model to be rotation-invariant; we show that this fails, predictably and by an exact amount, when capacity is limited. A symmetry groups a model's directions into blocks of equally strong directions, a symmetric model must use each block whole, and when the budget cuts a block every optimal model breaks the symmetry. For a Gaussian problem with a $k$-unit bilinear predictor, the log-loss price of symmetry is exactly $(U_k-I_k)/(2\sigma^2)$, the signal of the best $k$ directions minus that of the best whole blocks. Because an attention head compares tokens through a similarity of rank at most its head dimension, whatever computation precedes it, the same price governs heads fitted to a symmetric similarity by squared error, and heads trained this way on three-dimensional directions break symmetry at exactly the predicted head dimensions, with the predicted rotation sensitivity (RMSE 0.004). Under softmax attention, flatter attention restores the predicted symmetric head where tested, while with peaked attention broken heads beat every whole-shell symmetric head at 16 of 20 head dimensions. We give a procedure that separates capacity-driven breaking from other causes.

cross Anytime Valid Confidence Bands for Distribution Functions

Authors: Bror Hultberg, Dave Zachariah, Ant\^onio H. Ribeiro

Abstract: Nonparametric inference often requires characterizing an unknown function, such as a cumulative distribution function (CDF), across its entire domain rather than a specific point. We develop confidence bands for distribution functions of a data stream that are valid simultaneously over all times and all points, even when each observation may depend arbitrarily on the past. We begin by deriving such bands for the time-averaged conditional CDF and show how their widths are determined by the depth of a binary search tree. We then show that this result is an instance of a framework through which one can construct valid confidence bands uniformly over time and over an arbitrary index set for any indexed family of time-averaged distribution functions. We illustrate the framework on importance-weighted and right-censored CDFs, as well as multivariate CDFs.

cross LLM Hallucination Detection via Low-rank Multilinear Decomposition

Authors: Devansh Sharma, Faizul Rakib Sayem, Shahana Ibrahim

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) frequently generate fluent yet factually incorrect responses, raising concerns about their reliability. Recent hallucination detectors increasingly exploit internal representations, but extracting informative signals from high-dimensional hidden states remains challenging. Existing approaches often rely on selected activations or pooling-based compression, potentially overlooking discriminative information distributed across Transformer layers, feature modes, and tokens. We propose LOTUS (Low-rank Order-statistic TUcker-based Subspace learning), a multilinear low-rank decomposition framework for hallucination detection that extracts compact representations from LLM hidden states while reducing dependence on labeled data. Our approach first summarizes variable-length token representations using complementary statistical descriptors that capture peak activations, activation distributions, and cross-layer changes. We then leverage low-rank multilinear decomposition to extract compact representations that capture the underlying structure of LLM hidden states, which are subsequently used to train a lightweight hallucination classifier. Experiments across multiple LLMs and question-answering benchmarks demonstrate improved hallucination detection performance over recent state-of-the-art baselines.

cross Approximation Families and the Statistical Efficiency of Denoising Score Matching

Authors: Brian Miyatake

Abstract: We study score matching over finite-dimensional approximation spaces generated by gradients of eigenfunctions of the infinitesimal generator of a forward diffusion. We compare polynomial approximation under one-dimensional Ornstein-Uhlenbeck flow with trigonometric-polynomial approximation under heat flow on the torus. In the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck setting, we show that for every non-Gaussian initial distribution uniformly comparable to the standard Gaussian, the asymptotic score-estimation variance grows exponentially along a subsequence of polynomial degrees. Averaging the score-matching loss under the diffusion semigroup, which we call operator-informed score matching (OISM), reduces the variance at each fixed degree but does not eliminate this exponential instability at sufficiently small times. A local minimax lower bound shows that the same high-degree dependence arises when estimating the polynomial projection of the score, rather than being an artifact of OISM. In contrast, under heat flow on the torus, OISM over trigonometric polynomials admits finite-sample control of the score error along the forward diffusion and yields the classical minimax rate for estimating sufficiently smooth densities under expected Kullback-Leibler loss.

cross Structure, Not Sequence: Tracking and Validating Folding Mechanisms in ESMFold's Trunk with Sparse Autoencoders

Authors: Bohan Liu, Xiaohan Fan, Wenqian Ye, Aidong Zhang

Abstract: Structure predictors like ESMFold fold proteins using neural network computation and have transformed structural biology. However, the driving mechanism from amino-acid (AA) sequence to 3D structure is insufficiently understood, and biological meaning remains locked in the dense network. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) have shown promising results in obtaining interpretable concept features over an overcomplete basis of intermediate protein sequence-model representations. Yet folding models, which predict physical shape from AA sequence semantics, have no such account. We hypothesize that the features carrying the folding computation evolve in the 3D structural regression process. In this paper, we interpret the folding trunk in three steps. First, a pilot analysis on the representation distribution across modules and recycles locates distinct spatial computation in the trunk. Second, the dictionaries expose a division of labor: the single representation carries inherited semantics, and the pair representation computes the pairwise geometry. Third, we interpret the dictionary at scale with a reasoning language model, trace how the concepts compose across blocks by gradient-based attribution, and follow the interpreted code to the model's folding output, verifying where the computation aligns with, or departs from, known biochemistry. Together, the sparse dictionaries we train capture the relational geometry the trunk computes beyond the sequence model, and their concept activations forecast the model's own fold quality, turning the black-box folding trunk into a sparse, interpretable, and scientifically verifiable circuit.

cross Cross-modal Steering Vectors Exist Across Both Image and Text Generation

Authors: Donghao Zeng (Mila, McGill University), Nishant Subramani (Carnegie Mellon University), Tamar Rott Shaham (MIT CSAIL), Benno Krojer (Mila, McGill University, Northeastern University)

Abstract: Are concepts and mechanisms unified across modalities in current AI models, or instead implemented separately for each one? We operationalize this broad question of modality unification by studying cross-modal steering: 1) finding vectors that successfully steer generation on tokens of one modality, which then 2) transfer zero-shot to steer generation when added to tokens of another modality. Our study goes beyond simple correlational alignment (e.g. representational similarity) because steering is fundamentally causal. We study cross-modal steering in several unified multimodal models (UMMs), since they possess shared parameters for language and image generation, focusing on these two modalities. Across diverse steered concepts such as emotion and object size, we show that steering vectors transfer zero-shot in both directions (text-to-image & image-to-text). Even for concepts that are highly unique to one modality (e.g. formal vs. colloquial language), steering vectors often induce a consistent, interpretable effect in the other modality, such as formal clothing and design. This transfer has a geometric signature too: despite a clear modality gap separating text and image representations, steering vectors with strong cross-modal transferability tend to point in similar directions for the same concept. Our findings shed light on how vision and language interact inside unified multimodal models and point towards more general, modality-agnostic approaches for controlling their behavior.

cross Privately Clipping Heavy-Tailed Data

Authors: Matthew Joseph, Alex Kulesza

Abstract: Summing vectors is a basic task in differentially private data analysis. Standard private sum algorithms clip vectors to some bound, sum them, and add noise scaled to the bound. The differential privacy literature has developed a deep library of noise addition mechanisms, but it offers few tools for privately choosing a clipping bound based on the data. We introduce a method that privately chooses a clipping bound by optimizing residual coherence, the directional alignment of vectors exceeding the bound, against the variance cost of additional noise. We prove a close relationship between residual coherence and bias for a general class of heavy-tailed data regimes and show empirically that, across several datasets, our method outperforms existing baselines.

cross BUSTA: Teaching Small Language Models to Reason About Binary Execution

Authors: Ritika Poddar, Aidan Fennelly, Deirdre Murphy, Eric Wolos, Corre L. Steele, Michael Doyle

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLM) are increasingly applied to Reverse Engineering (RE) workflows. RE, however, differs from common LLM coding tasks as it requires models to reason about low-level program behavior. Furthermore, the deployment of tooling in resource-constrained sandboxed RE environments limits the ability to use frontier-scale solutions. We examine whether a targeted LLM training approach can transfer distinct RE skills to open models via inverse basic-block execution: given an x86-64 basic block and a desired output register state, the model predicts the input register values that produce it. Fine-tuning \texttt{Qwen3-1.7B} and \texttt{Nemotron-3-Nano} with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) raises knowable-register prediction accuracy from 73.2\% to 85.3\% and from 63.3\% to 95.2\%, respectively. cross FASST: A Compact Stateless Atmosphere Model for Long-Term Ocean Coupling

Authors: Pratham Aggarwal, Duncan Watson-Parris, William E. Chapman

Abstract: Coupled climate simulations are computationally expensive, largely because of the atmosphere. We introduce FASST, a coupled climate model that retains the prognostic POP2 ocean while replacing the atmosphere and sea ice with lightweight U-Net emulators. The atmospheric emulator is diagnostic, predicting surface fluxes from the evolving ocean-ice state every $6$ simulated hours, while the sea-ice emulator advances the ice state monthly. For $140$ years of unforced integration, FASST maintains a stable global-mean climate without SST restoring, with post-transient global-mean SST of $291.30 \pm 0.22\mathrm{K}$ compared with $291.25 \pm 0.10\mathrm{K}$ in CESM$2$ piControl. FASST also captures interannual Ni\~no-$3.4$ variability, although with weaker amplitude than CESM ($0.62\mathrm{K}$ versus $1.02\mathrm{K}$). These results demonstrate that a stateless learned atmosphere can sustain stable, free-running coupled climate simulations over century-scale integrations.

cross Balanced Active Subspaces for Optimal Control via Trajectory Optimization

Authors: Emmanuel S. Ameh, Samuel E. Otto

Abstract: The curse of dimensionality associated with solving the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equation for the value function remains a key obstacle for real-time optimal feedback control of high-dimensional nonlinear dynamical systems. This is especially severe in aerospace applications such as relaminarizing unsteady turbulent flows and recovering from aerodynamic stall where nonlinear dynamics play a significant role. We introduce Balanced Active Subspaces for Optimal Control (BASOC), a model reduction framework which seeks to find directions in state space along which the value function is most sensitive, and the distribution of states exhibits maximum variance along optimal trajectories. BASOC leverages trajectory optimization and the Pontryagin minimum principle to obtain state and co-state (value function gradient) data along locally optimal trajectories for constructing state and gradient covariance matrices. Balancing these matrices provides an oblique projection for approximating the value function, which can also be used to construct reduced-order models and surrogate feedback control laws. We show that for linear-quadratic optimal control problems, BASOC becomes equivalent to a snapshot method for initial condition balancing of the optimally controlled closed-loop realization. The framework is demonstrated on a nonlinear three-dimensional system where POD and balanced truncation are known to perform poorly, and on the nonlinear complex Ginzburg-Landau equation to demonstrate feasibility for larger-scale systems.

cross Enhancing Clinical Classifier Robustness with Deep Representation Learning Based OOD Detection on DNA Methylation Data

Authors: Md Zahangir Alom, Quynh T. Tran, Breuer Alexandar, Brent A. Orr

Abstract: DNA methylation (DNAM) array-based tumor classification is a powerful clinical diagnostic tool, but models trained on fixed reference cohorts can produce faulty predictions for tumor types not represented in the training distribution. Deep neural network (DNN) classifiers were trained on public sarcoma (SARC) (GSE140686) and brain tumor (BT) (GSE90496) cohorts, and feature vectors were extracted from bottleneck layers. Spectral discrepancy was used to discriminate in-distribution (IND) from OOD samples. Performance was measured via cross-validation, holdout samples, and an independent clinical dataset. 1D-SSA was the most effective approach. In the sarcoma-IND/brain tumor-OOD setting, the framework achieved an AUROC of 0.975 and True Negative Rate (TNR)@95TPR of 0.930, and an AUROC of 0.9569 and TNR@95TPR of 0.8660 for the brain tumor-IND/sarcoma-OOD setting.
