cross HELICS: Biobank-scale Conditional Synthetic Genome Generation via Latent Flow Matching

Authors: Ahmad Abdel-Azim, Xihong Lin

Abstract: Individual-level genotype data underpin genetic discovery, disease-risk modeling, and therapeutic development, yet many critical settings remain data-limited, including underrepresented ancestries, rare or imbalanced disease cohorts, and restricted-access biobanks. Conditional synthetic genome generation would support privacy-aware benchmarking, simulation, and data augmentation, but requires learning an ultra-high-dimensional discrete distribution over hundreds of thousands to tens of millions of correlated single-nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs), or sites of DNA variation. We introduce HELICS: High-fidelity gEneration via Latent Interpolant Conditional Synthesis, a phasing-free latent flow matching framework for genome-wide SNP-level generation from biobank-scale genotype data. cross IOSage: Detecting HPC I/O Bottleneck Patterns to Guide LLM Recommendations

Authors: Mahdi Banisharif, Tabassum Mahmud, Ahmad Tarraf, Suren Byna, Ali Jannesari

Abstract: HPC applications often leave much of their parallel file system's bandwidth unused, and turning their Darshan logs into concrete I/O fixes still requires a scarce I/O expert. Rule-based tools flag counters against thresholds set by experts, and in tools built on large language models (LLMs), such as ION and IOAgent, the LLM decides which bottlenecks a log contains. We present IOSage, in which a classifier trained on many labeled logs, not the LLM, makes that decision, since a single log gives an LLM no baseline for what is abnormal. For 5 of the 6 application scenarios with a helpful change, IOSage with Claude Sonnet 4 names it, including the change for 10-minute WRF restarts (up to 1.93x faster); with GPT-4o and Llama 3.1 as well, the classifier and cards name more helpful changes than the LLM alone. On 78 benchmark test runs without a pattern, the LLM deciding alone proposes changes for 64; with the classifier, for 9. The classifier reaches 0.929 Micro-F1 on 488 labeled benchmark test runs (0.916 as the gate; 0.832 on the 158 from configurations and jobs outside training), above Drishti's rules, WisIO, and IOAgent's pipeline without its literature retrieval. On TraceBench's nine expert-labeled traces from production systems, it has the highest precision of the tools compared (0.82), with 2 false positives against 12 for the keyword-mapped LLM diagnoses shipped with TraceBench and 5 for Drishti, on the five issue labels that match registered patterns.

cross Evaluating Federated Vision-Language and Vision-Only Models for Privacy-Aware Breast Cancer Imaging

Authors: Malithi Mithsara Wanniarachchi Kankanamge, Ning Yang, Sree Bash Debnath, Md Zobaer Islam, Abdur R. Shahid

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) shows promise for breast imaging, but limited labeled data, institutional data fragmentation, and privacy concerns hinder centralized training. cross Whole-Body Humanoid Control via Stochastic Motion Tokenization

Authors: Michael Drolet, Oleg Arenz, Jan Peters

Abstract: While velocity-commanded RL policies are effective for controlling quadrupeds, they are a limited interface for humanoids that need to perform complex whole-body motions. Furthermore, RL tracking policies for whole-body motions typically have limited information about the desired future motion, preventing them from performing controlled deviations to achieve long-term task success; such information is particularly relevant when a controller must prepare for transitions between behaviors. We address these challenges using concise motion representations that can be generated by a high-level controller, given a behavior label or text command. cross A Simple Presentation of Robust Membership Inference Attacks

Authors: Renaud Gaucher, Ephraim Linder, Adam Smith, Jonathan Ullman, Lydia Zakynthinou

Abstract: In a membership inference attack, an attacker is given the data of a particular individual, and tries to determine if that individual was included in a particular dataset, given some kind of statistics derived from the dataset or a model trained on the dataset. Roughly, a membership inference attack is robust if it succeeds even when the statistics are highly noisy, regardless of how that noise is introduced. We give a simple presentation of robust membership inference attacks for mean estimation, covering both multivariate Gaussian distributions and binary product distributions. We also give a summary of the literature on robust membership inference, covering history, extensions, and connections to other research areas.

cross Second-order Sinkhorn Geometry and $\sqrt{n}$-Inference

Authors: Tristan Luca Saidi, Florian Gunsilius, Gonzalo Mena

Abstract: The Sinkhorn divergence, a debiased form of entropic optimal transport, has been widely used in various scientific disciplines due to its computational and statistical tractability. Unlike the Wasserstein distance, however, the Sinkhorn divergence is not a metric and it does not naturally admit a formal Riemannian structure. These properties are central to many applications of optimal transport methods that utilize interpolation, barycentric averaging, parallel transport or other geometric operations. Recent work addresses these shortcomings by constructing a formal Riemannian tangent space and a metric tensor on the space of probability measures that quadratically approximates the Sinkhorn divergence. In this work, we extend this framework with two major threads of contributions. For a suitable class of finite-smoothness cost functions, we prove Hadamard differentiability of the Sinkhorn metric tensor and we derive the Levi-Civita covariant derivative in this geometry; we then use the covariant derivative to obtain the geodesic and parallel transport equations, each of which amount to an ODE on a fixed Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space (RKHS) with smooth coefficients. Our second contribution is to linearize the solutions to the geodesic and parallel transport equations using standard tools from Banach space ODE theory. We show that under standard regularity conditions these linearizations imply $\sqrt{n}$-limiting Gaussian processes centered at population values for the plugin estimators of the exponential map, the logarithmic map, parallel transport and the induced Sinkhorn distance. Moreover, we show that the nonparametric bootstrap is consistent for all objects mentioned. To the best of our knowledge, these are the first $\sqrt{n}$--limit theorems and bootstrap consistency results for canonical geometric operations in a transport-based geometry on the space of probability measures.

cross Algorithmics for Graphical Separation Criteria

Authors: Mois\'es Chavira-Flores, Sebastian Weichwald, Leonard Henckel

Abstract: Statistical graphical models are representations of the dependences between the components of a random vector. They encode these dependences via graphical separation criteria. We consider the algorithmic problem of checking separation statements for the various graph classes and separation criteria that have been developed by the community. We establish that all criteria we study can be checked by modular variations of reachability. For all separation criteria but $\sigma$-separation, this yields a linear-time algorithm. This is effectively the optimal attainable time since loading the graph itself is linear-time in the number of edges in the graph. Using the CIfly framework for deploying modular variations of reachability, we also provide R and Python code implementing our algorithms. We evaluate empirical runtime and scaling behavior in a simulation study.

cross Causal Localization of Evidence Effects in Transformers

Authors: Jipeng Li, Pramod Khargonekar, Yanning Shen

Abstract: Can controlled symbolic inputs reveal attention heads that influence a language model's decisions from natural language evidence? We introduce controlled evidence aggregation (CEA), which varies the counts and order of two symbols in otherwise fixed prompts and measures changes in a frozen Transformer's preference between two answer tokens. The preference is measured by their logit difference; the prompts prescribe no correct answer. Balanced utility causal selection (BUC) identifies compact head sets by jointly evaluating how much ablating their outputs removes the count effect and how closely patching mean outputs between count conditions recovers the effect in both directions. We derive conditional bounds linking head attribution to intervention effects and specifying when the attribution shortlist can be recovered from finite samples. In Pythia-410M, ablating sets of 1-12 heads selected separately for four ordering regimes removes 65.5-91.6% of the count effect. The sets retain their influence on count comparisons not used for selection. To test relevance beyond symbolic inputs, we evaluate a natural language task asking which state is supported by more reports. Ablating eight heads selected only on symbolic yes/no prompts lowers mean accuracy by 8.2 percentage points, versus 0.8 for random sets matched by size and layer. Our results demonstrate that controlled symbolic probes can identify compact attention head sets whose causal influence extends to natural language decisions.

cross RoboAuto: Autonomous Robot Program Search through Multi-Level Physical Understanding

Authors: Buzhao Liu, Jianing Ye, Xiu Yuan, Yangtong Feng, Chengsong Huang, Tyler Dinh, Yevgeniy Vorobeychik, Ning Zhang, Chongjie Zhang

Abstract: Recently, LLM-based autonomous agents have shown strong capabilities in iteratively generating, evaluating, and refining executable solutions. cross God Does Not Play Dice: Enforcing Batch Invariance in Agentic Kernel Generation

Authors: Youwei Bao, Chengpeng Wang, Simin Chen

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) serving systems dynamically batch user requests for GPU execution, so the same request may be processed alongside different requests across runs. If the GPU kernels lack batch invariance (BI), this changing batch context can alter the numerical output for an unchanged input, undermining reproducibility. Existing kernel generation agents focus on correctness and efficiency but \emph{overlook BI}. We find 19 confirmed BI violations among 83 batch-applicable released artifacts. To address this limitation, we propose \tool, which combines contract-guided static dependency analysis with dynamic validation of bitwise consistency across batch contexts. \tool is designed as a plug-in component for general kernel agents, allowing their original optimization capabilities to be reused while adding BI enforcement. We integrate it as successive acceptance gates in the optimization loop, rejecting structurally risky or dynamically non-invariant candidates before performance benchmarking and returning feedback to the agent. Experiments across three agent frameworks under baseline and BI-aware generation on real world LLM serving tasks show that \tool improves the BI pass rate from 67.6\% to 91.9\% while retaining 91.8\% of the baseline geometric mean speedup. These results demonstrate that \tool can be incorporated into kernel optimization by agents as an additional reliability objective alongside correctness and speed.

cross Impossibility of No-Regret in Multi-Agent Bidding

Authors: Zongjun Yang, Rachitesh Kumar, Christian Kroer

Abstract: We consider $T$ repeated second-price auctions across $n$ pacing bidders with i.i.d. item arrivals, and show that it is computationally impossible for all bidders to achieve no-regret simultaneously while satisfying their respective budget or return-on-investment constraints. Therefore, in the prominent internet auction systems where buyers simultaneously deploy pacing algorithms or automated bidding agents, we cannot hope to achieve joint no-regret guarantees in general. We establish a deterministic relationship that high variance in online bidding systems implies high regret. By showing that one can extract a pacing equilibrium from a low-variance system, we prove PPAD-hardness of attaining joint no-regret. For several widely used bidding algorithms, we also show evidence from dynamical systems and numerical simulations that they can indeed display high-variance behavior through cycling, even on simple market instances.

cross Look Only When Needed: Certificate-Guided Visual Routing for Efficient Diffusion VLMs

Authors: Donghyeon Cho, Joowon Kim, Seungho Shin, Eunho Yang

Abstract: Diffusion vision-language models (DVLMs) process their entire input at every denoising step. Since visual tokens make up 89-97% of this input on the benchmarks we study, most of the decoding computation is spent repeatedly processing the same image. Visual token pruning methods partially relieve this burden by removing part of the visual tokens, but they lose the removed information and still process the remaining image at every step. To measure how often the image is actually needed, we compare LLaDA-V's predictions with and without the image at every decoding step and find that removing the image changes a token being unmasked at only 22.8% of steps on average and at as few as 7.9%. We therefore propose LOWN, a decoding method that decides at every step whether to process the image and how many tokens to unmask. LOWN predicts the masked tokens without the image and processes the image only when this prediction disagrees with the last image-conditioned prediction. Agreement certifies the earlier prediction for reuse, and the number of certified tokens sets how many are unmasked in that step. We further observe that the need for the image differs across benchmarks, so LOWN's adaptive decision is needed rather than a fixed schedule. Without training or a tuned threshold, LOWN retains 99.6% of LLaDA-V's score on ten benchmarks at 35.3% of the decoding FLOPs on average. LOWN also generalizes across LLaDA-based DVLMs, composes orthogonally with visual token pruning, and runs under key-value caching for further efficiency gains. Code and project page are available at https://hyeon9698.github.io/LOWN/

URLs: https://hyeon9698.github.io/LOWN/

cross Specialization, Diversity, and Scaling in Child-Scale Language Models

Authors: Steven Y. Feng, Michael C. Frank

Abstract: Modern language models are trained on far more language than children experience, but we know much less about learning from small, naturalistic datasets. We train small GPT-2 models on transcripts of speech with children from dense home recordings (BabyView) and on synthetic child-directed dialogue (TinyDialogues), varying the amount, source, and diversity of training data. Models trained on one child's language environment predict that environment substantially better than other children's environments. Much of this specialization is lexical and distributional: a simple 5-gram model reproduces the cross-child pattern (r=.91). Training on data from multiple children clearly improves prediction of pooled held-out language relative to training on a single child's input without a similarly clear improvement on grammatical benchmarks. Across data sizes, naturalistic and synthetic training inputs also produce different grammatical and lexical-semantic patterns, with important differences between grammatical phenomena. Together, these results show that small-data language learning depends not only on how much language a model sees, but on which environments it comes from and how well that input matches what the model is asked to predict.

cross From Biological Curves to Soft Modular Robots: Learning-Based Inverse Design with Uncertainty-Guided Refinement

Authors: Dixiang Wang, Minghao Zha, Arifur Rahman, Yinhong Qin, Yitao Jiang, Shiyang Lu, Zebing Mao, Muhao Chen, Luyang Zhao

Abstract: Animals exhibit diverse shape-changing behaviors, from the undulatory swimming of eels to the arching movements of inchworms. Reproducing these biological shapes with modular soft robots offers a promising approach to developing versatile bioinspired robotic systems. However, translating a desired shape into a feasible robotic configuration remains challenging. Existing cable-driven modular soft robots rely on computationally expensive physics-based modeling and combinatorial search for inverse design, making it difficult to efficiently reproduce diverse target shapes. To address this challenge, we present a learning-based real-to-sim-to-real framework that translates biological curves into robotic configurations through surrogate-assisted inverse design and uncertainty-guided refinement. The proposed method combines learned inverse prediction with targeted candidate exploration, using a fast forward surrogate to evaluate and select suitable designs. The learned forward surrogate reduces deformation prediction time from $1.53 \times 10^5$ ms to 0.0008 ms, achieving approximately a $1.9 \times 10^8$-fold speedup. At the same time, the proposed inverse design method reduces design time from $1.99 \times 10^6$ ms to 4.18 ms. On 5,000 test samples generated from previously unseen threading patterns, the method achieves a 93% shape reconstruction success rate under surrogate-based evaluation. Physical demonstrations with four bioinspired robots further illustrate the translation of biological curves into realizable robotic shapes.

cross Quantifying and Prioritizing Stealthy Source-Manipulation Threats Against Multi-Source Anomaly Detectors

Authors: Indraveni Chebolu, Seema Panghal, Arnab Mallick, Ch A S Murty

Abstract: Security pipelines often fuse several data sources and protect a downstream model with a cross-source anomaly detector. Such detectors are usually judged by their clean false-positive rate and by how well they catch random corruption. Neither metric captures an adversary who controls some sources and targets a specific downstream decision. We propose a threat-quantification score, the \emph{task-conditioned visibility margin}, defined as the least detector evidence a manipulation must reveal per unit of harm to the downstream task. The margin has a closed form, takes about $85\,\mu$s per certificate, yields a minimum-evidence attack, and identifies task-relevant directions the detector cannot see. On a 17-channel industrial telemetry benchmark, a detector that flags 82.8\% of random single-channel corruptions is first-order blind on 64.7\% of task/subset pairs in an audit of one- to four-channel subsets. Task-conditioned manipulations cause more than twice as many falsely reassuring errors as generic stealthy ones, and more of them evade the alarm on four of five splits, mainly at small budgets. Used to prioritize audits on held-out subsets, the margin finds a median of four verified failures in ten checks, compared with none for random order and two for alarm-based ranking, and performs comparably to the task-gradient baseline. It also ranks vulnerable configurations of the learned GDN and TranAD detectors and of a five-source crop-yield panel.

cross SMEBENCH: Benchmarking Neural Music Editing with Synthetized MIDI Edits

Authors: Matthieu Cervera, Francesco Paissan, Micael Antunes, Mirco Ravanelli, Ricard Marxer, Cem Subakan

Abstract: Neural music editing requires a system to perform a requested change while preserving the musical content that should remain unchanged. Existing evaluations often lack a paired target edit, making these requirements difficult to measure separately. We introduce SMEBench, a large-scale benchmark of controlled music edits constructed by modifying symbolic MIDI performances and rendering matched source and target audio. Each example contains a source, an instruction, a paired target, edit metadata, and complementary descriptions. The benchmark covers twelve instrumentation, articulation, dynamics, and pitch operations and includes a balanced 3,600-example test set together with a smaller real-audio complement derived from multitrack recordings. The paired targets allow source preservation, target agreement, and textual compatibility to be evaluated independently. We use these references to diagnose common audio-editing metrics and benchmark six representative editing systems, showing that the metric used to rank a system can materially change the conclusion. The dataset is available at our repository.

cross Neuronal Identity as an Organizational Basis for Analyzing Neural Population Dynamics

Authors: Seungjae Han, Joshua Yedam You, Soi Kim, Minho Eom, Jinho Park, Seunghee Park, Byeongwook Lee, Young-Gyu Yoon

Abstract: Individual neurons exhibit diverse molecular, morphological, and electrophysiological properties that shape their distinct roles in neural circuits. While these multimodal features are largely shared across individuals, they remain an underutilized source of information in analyzing neural population dynamics. We posit that these shared biological profiles provide a common reference for relating neural dynamics across subjects and recording sessions. Our framework leverages this structure by routing neurons into latent groups based on their biological profiles and analyzing population dynamics through group-level representations. Because these assignments are anchored in biological properties that are consistent across subjects, the learned organization supports transfer to unseen animals without retraining or neuron-wise correspondence. We evaluate our framework on whole-brain calcium imaging in larval zebrafish and large-scale electrophysiology in the mouse visual cortex. Across both species and recording modalities, the framework delivers state-of-the-art performance in both neural decoding and manifold analysis: it achieves superior decoding of behavior and stimulus labels while better preserving the temporal consistency and local geometric structure of latent trajectories on unseen data. Our experiments demonstrate that not only does providing biological features improve baseline performance, but our proposed framework achieves even stronger results by using these properties to explicitly structure the population rather than treating them as auxiliary inputs. Together, these results suggest that organizing neural populations by their multimodal biological features offers a promising framework for generalizing population dynamics across subjects and recording sessions.

cross Frequency-Uniform Log-Regret Reinforcement Learning for Diffusions

Authors: Jose Blanchet, Lutong Hao, Renyuan Xu

Abstract: High-frequency control problems are often studied through time discretization, but refining the timestep $\delta$ increases the discrete horizon $H=T/\delta$ and can make standard finite-horizon regret guarantees deteriorate even when the physical horizon $T$ is fixed. We study episodic value-based reinforcement learning for Euler discretizations of controlled diffusions. The performance is measured as the cumulative regret over $K$ episodes relative to the optimal policy of the corresponding Euler model. Under stability and regularity conditions on value function gradients, Bellman residual derivatives, and fitted critics, we prove an $\mathcal{O}(\log K)$ regret bound with constants uniform in $\delta$. For dynamics with drift linear in unknown parameters and unknown constant diffusion covariance, we verify localized versions of these conditions for a constrained, model-based controlled-diffusion fitted-$Q$ algorithm (CD-FQ) and obtain high-probability $\mathcal{O}(\log K\log\log K)$ regret for fixed and finite $T$, again with constants uniform in $\delta$.

cross Batching the Reflective Optimization Loop: Parallel Proposals Make GEPA Faster and Better

Authors: Jialin Zhang, Lakshya A Agrawal, Shangyin Tan, Donghyun Lee, Lutfi Eren Erdogan, Dan Klein, Koushik Sen, Alex Dimakis, Matei Zaharia

Abstract: Reflective text optimization makes agentic systems better, but the optimization loop is itself a resource-hungry workload whose sequential structure leaves parallel evaluation capacity idle. We extend the open-source GEPA optimizer to propose and evaluate a batch of candidates on each optimization step instead of one candidate at a time. In our sweep on two tasks, most batched runs finished in half the wall-clock time or less, and the fastest in about a quarter to a third. Batched settings also achieved higher held-out test scores, from 68.9% to 72.1% on LiveBench-Math (with $2\times2$) and from 49.0% to 60.0% on HoVer (with $8\times1$). A simple runtime model explains the measured speedups under a finite worker pool, and batched settings also spend their metric-call and dollar budgets more efficiently. We frame the axes along which a reflective optimization step can scale in parallel, a first look at allocating a fixed optimization budget across them.

cross Variable-Length Feedback Coding With Universal Decoding and Bandit Channel Selection

Authors: Recep Can Yavas

Abstract: We study variable-length feedback (VLF) coding over $K$ additive white Gaussian noise (AWGN) channels whose noise variances are unknown to both terminals but lie in a known interval. The receiver selects one channel at each time, conveys the selection over noiseless feedback, and must learn the channel qualities while decoding, to avoid overusing suboptimal channels. This setting, which we call the VLF-Bandit problem, combines VLF coding, universal decoding, and bandit channel selection. We develop two joint coding and channel-selection schemes. The VLSF-Bandit scheme is a single-phase variable-length stop-feedback (VLSF) scheme based on a Gaussian random codebook, a score formed from a ratio of likelihood mixtures, and the asymptotically optimal $\text{kl-UCB}^{++}$ bandit algorithm applied to the received power. The VLF-Bandit scheme sends the message in packets by Schalkwijk--Kailath transmission, uses its normalized outputs as noise samples to learn the variances, and lets its exploration packets carry message bits. We prove second-order achievability bounds for both schemes, which are first-order optimal. In the logarithm of the achievable codebook size, the magnitude of the $\log N$ coefficient, with $N$ the average decoding time, is a baseline of one plus a channel-exploration term for VLSF-Bandit. For VLF-Bandit, this magnitude is smaller, with a universal-decoding term replacing the baseline and a smaller channel-exploration term. The VLF-Bandit bound improves on the universal VLF bound of Yavas and Tan (2025) for $K=1$ and on the bound of an explore-then-commit strategy whenever $K\geq2$.

cross TAPESTRY: A Single Backbone for Video Generation and Robot Control

Authors: Jeremy A. Collins, Seungjae Lee, Rachanon Wachakorn, Krishnan Srinivasan, Animesh Garg, Vitor Campagnolo Guizilini, Paarth Shah

Abstract: Pretrained video generation models encode rich priors about physical interactions, but leveraging them for robot control typically requires either resource-intensive video generation at test time or action heads trained from scratch that do not fully utilize the pretrained backbone. We introduce Tokenizing Actions as Video Patches in a Shared Transformer Policy (TAPESTRY), a world action model that repurposes a pretrained video diffusion transformer into a robotic policy without requiring video generation at inference. Our key idea is to treat robot actions as single-patch tokens in the video model's latent space. A lightweight linear projection maps each action timestep to the patch dimensions of the pretrained VAE, and the resulting tokens are processed by the same patchifying layers and transformer backbone used for video. The shared backbone is optimized through three diffusion objectives sampled per minibatch element: Video diffusion uses VAE latents from robot and human videos to establish dynamics priors. Joint video+action diffusion trains on robot demonstrations using a coupled diffusion timestep and an asymmetric attention mask. Action-only diffusion denoises actions conditioned on clean image latents. At test time, the model generates a compact action token sequence in a single DDIM step from pure noise, producing smooth action chunks at 84 ms per action chunk on consumer hardware and avoiding the cost of full video generation. Across simulation benchmarks and real-robot experiments, TAPESTRY achieves strong performance with substantially lower inference cost than comparable joint video--action policies. More information can be found at https://tapestry-wam.github.io.

URLs: https://tapestry-wam.github.io.

cross Joint Distributional Copula Regression for Insurance Claim Frequency and Severity via Boosting

Authors: Banghee So, Na Zhang

Abstract: In this paper, we propose a model-based boosting approach for fitting structured additive distributional copula regression models with bivariate responses, focusing particularly on the joint modeling of claim frequency and claim severity. The proposed approach is based on generalized additive models for location, scale, and shape (GAMLSS), which extend the general idea of GAMs by allowing multiple parameters of a response distribution, rather than only its conditional mean, to depend on covariates. The model is therefore intrinsically interpretable at the level of its additive predictors, providing greater transparency and auditability than black-box alternatives. We evaluate the proposed framework using synthetic and real-world data. We demonstrate its ability to recover the underlying covariate effects and dependence structure, identify informative variables, and achieve competitive predictive performance relative to benchmark models. We also illustrate how the fitted model can be interpreted.

cross Boundary Geometry Determines Optimal Shallow ReLU Approximation under Weighted Coefficient Constraints

Authors: Jia-Qi Yang, Lei Shi

Abstract: We study how boundary geometry affects $L^q$ approximation by shallow ReLU neural networks on convex bodies $K\subset\mathbb R^d$. The target classes are unit balls of weighted variation spaces that can enlarge classical spaces by incorporating domain geometry into constraints on output coefficients. We establish upper and lower bounds for arbitrary convex bodies and determine the optimal polynomial approximation exponents for all planar convex bodies and their products with polytopes, including cubes and circular cylinders. For $C^2$ convex bodies, we determine the optimal exponents in a dimension-dependent range of $q$ extending beyond $2$. When all principal curvatures are positive, the result extends to every finite $q\ge2$. For these classes, the dependence of the optimal exponent on the domain is captured by a single geometric growth exponent associated with small boundary caps. We also identify sharp weight thresholds for retaining the classical approximation exponent and improve previous weights on Euclidean balls while preserving the classical $L^2$ approximation rate. When the target is given as a finite ReLU network, the upper bounds can be realized by a compressed network using only the original neurons, without increasing the weighted coefficient sum. These results show that boundary geometry can change the optimal exponent even when dimension, error norm, and weighting rule are fixed.

cross Evaluating AlphaEarth Embeddings for Crop Classification: Separability, Temporal Dynamics, and Cross-Regional Transferability

Authors: Priyanka Goyal, Maitreyee Sar, Adway Mitra, Manjira Sinha

Abstract: Earth Observation (EO)-based agricultural monitoring is shifting from pixel-level spectral analysis toward representation-driven modeling, yet systematic evaluation of learned representations across spatial, temporal, and domain-generalization dimensions remains limited. Geospatial foundation models generate high-dimensional embeddings encoding spatio-temporal land-surface properties, but their utility for agricultural applications remains insufficiently understood. This study presents a representation-agnostic evaluation framework integrating geometric separability, temporal dynamics, spatial robustness, and cross-regional generalisation. A 64-dimensional AlphaEarth embedding space is benchmarked against Sentinel-2 reflectance, NDVI-derived phenology, and harmonic features using field-scale data from the Conterminous United States (CONUS; 2017-2023) and Northern India (2021). Traditional features achieve strong linear separability (Fisher ratio), while embeddings provide superior distribution-aware separation -- Bhattacharyya and Mahalanobis distances up to an order of magnitude higher for spectrally similar pairs (e.g. Corn/Soybeans: 3.9 vs. 0.10). Embeddings uniquely capture multi-year crop rotation dynamics, reaching a separability ratio of 1.784 versus a maximum of 1.003 across all engineered features, and exhibit lower residual spatial autocorrelation of errors (mean Moran's $I \approx 0.17$). However, harmonic and NDVI features show stronger cross-continental transferability (US $\to$ India Macro F1: 0.493 vs. 0.326 for embeddings), reflecting better distributional alignment. The combined feature space yields balanced performance, supporting hybrid modeling. These findings reveal a fundamental trade-off between representational richness and cross-region transferability, providing a structured framework for feature selection in large-scale crop classification.

cross HorizON: A Multi-Horizon Deep Operator Network for Property Prediction of Organic Battery Stacks

Authors: Subhash V. S. Ganti, Lukas Woelfel, Arla Diengdoh, Tzvetan Mihaylov, Thomas Nevolianis, Jan Meyer, Christopher Kuenneth

Abstract: Transition-metal-based batteries rely on critical elements such as lithium, cobalt, and nickel. Their extraction creates environmental and supply-chain challenges. Organic electrode materials (OEMs) reduce this reliance and provide broad molecular tunability. However, OEM-based batteries still show limited long-term cycling stability. Their design also requires the joint optimization of coupled metrics such as voltage, specific capacity, capacity retention, and energy density. These metrics depend on the full battery stack and on the interactions among its components rather than on the active material alone. In our previous work, we developed a data-fusion multi-task framework for average discharge voltage and first-cycle specific capacity. Both targets are single-valued. Here, we extend that framework to multi-horizon prediction by predicting a property at every cycle of a discharge trajectory rather than at a single cycle. Our multi-fidelity and Multi-horizon Deep Operator Network learns DFT redox potentials and cycle-dependent specific-capacity trajectories within one model. We train it on complete battery-stack representations and usage conditions, so it learns battery-stack interactions rather than cathode-only effects. This framework supports long-term property prediction, inverse design, and process optimization for organic battery systems.

cross Intrinsic Information-Theoretic Analysis of ReLU Nets

Authors: Johan Mylius-Kroken, Elisabeth Wetzer, Ali Ramezani-Kebrya, Robert Jenssen, Kristoffer Knutsen Wickstr{\o}m

Abstract: Mutual information (MI) is fundamental to representation-learning analysis in deep networks, but in high dimensions MI estimators depend on hyperparameters whose bias often dominates the signal. We show that continuous piecewise-linear (CPWL) networks, including ReLU networks, admit an intrinsic information-theoretic analysis that avoids this pitfall: their inherent partition of the input space into convex linear regions assigns to every input a discrete region label $\Pi$ alongside the continuous representation $T$ and label $Y$. We introduce a decomposition of the mutual information $I(Y;T)$ into interpretable geometric terms. We show that the dominant term, the *routing information* $I(Y; \Pi)$, admits an estimable plug-in estimator, that in the well-sampled regime, approximates a population lower bound on the joint information $I(Y; \Pi, T)$. When the partition saturates, a functional-equivalence quotient collapses regions implementing the same linear operator and restores informativeness; this extension has a single bounded, dimensionless parameter, which is reported through the quotient's compression ratio. The routing estimator agrees strongly with three established MI baselines applied to the same networks and data. The functional quotient recovers informative estimates in the regime bordering the well-sampled and under-sampled regime. We provide a framework for analysing the internal information geometry of CPWL networks, grounded in their inherent piecewise-linear structure rather than in external discretisation.

cross Sample-and-Score: Surrogate-Guided Coordinated Spatial Reuse in IEEE 802.11 Networks

Authors: Maksymilian Wojnar

Abstract: Coordinated spatial reuse (Co-SR) in IEEE 802.11bn increases area throughput through simultaneous transmissions, yet the combinatorial configuration space makes real-time optimization challenging. We propose a sample-and-score approach: draw random Co-SR configurations, score each with a surrogate model, and select the best. The surrogate is trained offline and deployed without online fine-tuning or sequential exploration, yet performs close to multi-armed bandit and heuristic baselines that need multiple interaction steps or extensive measurements. On unseen topologies with 2-16 access points, it recovers 52-77% of a perfect surrogate's throughput. A perfect sample-and-score approach reaches 95% of the upper bound throughput with 8192 candidates.

cross RAG-CSI: Global Local Retrieval via Joint State and Dynamics Matching for CSI Prediction

Authors: Xinyue Jiang, Tong Li, Shiceng Zhang, Ke Chen, Zhu Xiao

Abstract: Channel state information (CSI) prediction forecasts future channel states from historical observations, supporting transceiver design and resource allocation with reduced measurement overhead. However, existing deep learning predictors mainly encode channel knowledge in model parameters, which can limit their ability to represent diverse patterns of channel dynamics across scenes. In this paper, we propose RAG-CSI, a retrieval-enhanced Transformer framework that augments prediction with relevant historical channel knowledge. Specifically, we construct a global--local CSI knowledge store containing representative observations across scenes and historical observations from the current serving scene. We further develop joint state--dynamics retrieval (JSDR), which jointly characterizes channel states, phase dynamics, and amplitude dynamics to retrieve relevant CSI sequences. A Transformer predictor then uses cross-attention to fuse the retrieved knowledge with the current CSI history and predict future CSI with limited additional model parameters. Experiments on a multi-scene CSI dataset show overall improvements exceeding 10\% across all five evaluation metrics over the best baseline for each metric, including a 15.42\% reduction in normalized mean squared error.

cross PREEN: Exemplar-Based Color Style Transfer with Smartphone Editor Presets

Authors: Beomjin Ahn

Abstract: We present PREEN (Preset Regression via an Exemplar-based Editor Network), which casts color style transfer as regression onto the 29 parameters of a deterministic image editor. Given a content image and a style reference, PREEN predicts a preset, a vector of editor slider values that is interpretable and editor-consumable, not a raster image. The key obstacle is supervision, since an arbitrary real reference lacks a preset ground truth. We therefore supervise with a style-transfer teacher, whose target looks we invert into the editor's parameter space as pseudo-presets, then pretrain a CLIP-conditioned regressor and refine it with LoRA under an image-level CIELAB loss. On the human-curated PST50 benchmark, PREEN applies the reference's color while preserving structural fidelity, leading the baselines on pixel- and perceptual-fidelity metrics. The preset is compact and predicted at sub-second on-device latency. The low-dimensional subspace also exposes a single inference-time strength knob that traces a continuous default-to-vivid trajectory in one forward pass, with every intermediate state a valid editor preset. A two-axis human study shows that raters prefer the vivid end for matching the reference but the default as a standalone photo, a trade-off the knob spans without retraining.

cross Quantum Evolution Kernel for Node-Attributed Graphs

Authors: Manuel Padovani, Alessandra Di Pierro, Claudia Daffara

Abstract: We present a novel approach to embed node-attributed graphs onto neutral-atom quantum computers, and propose an application to digit recognition. Our approach extends PASQAL's quantum evolution kernel (QEK) by introducing a node-level feature encoding based on three-level quantum states. To this purpose, we present a prototype showing how the resulting embedding can be leveraged to extract correlation-based information for classification tasks. The entire quantum process is simulated using Pasqal's Pulser library, which inevitably imposes constraints on the size of the experimental datasets. We compare our method with classical baselines to evaluate its potential. cross Online gas classification at the edge

Authors: Ton Juny Pina, Hugh Greatorex, Elisabetta Chicca

Abstract: Field applications in chemical sensing, from portable safety monitors to mobile robots, require models that are lightweight, fast to respond, and can be recalibrated from limited labelled data. Current gas classification pipelines assume controlled sampling cycles and offline feature extraction, and rely on computationally expensive models. With a few thousand neurons and sub-milliwatt power consumption, insects reliably classify odours in open environments. Inspired by the insect olfactory system, we present a neural network architecture that classifies raw sensor array readings using a sparse, high-dimensional binary representation. We evaluate it on random sequences of single gases and binary mixtures, introducing new samples dynamically and classifying gas identity from the transient sensor response. cross From Ethernet to Wi-Fi: Flow Feature Selection and Construction for Traffic Classification

Authors: Francisco Rau, Petia Georgieva, Carlos Herranz, I\~naki Val, Joaquin Perez

Abstract: Flow-based traffic classifiers trained on Ethernet data may lose accuracy when applied to Wi-Fi traffic. This paper proposes a five-stage procedure for selecting and constructing features that enable Wi-Fi flow classification using models trained only on Ethernet data. The procedure removes the transport protocol identifier, examines rate dependence, compares feature distributions and the information each feature provides for the application classes, reduces redundancy, and constructs normalized packet-size and temporal features. cross Zooming without Zooming v2: From Seeing Details to Connecting Evidence for Generalizable Multimodal Perception

Authors: Lai Wei, Yatai Li, Liangbo He, Jun Lan, Renjie Gu, Linhan Cao, Junkai Lin, Huijia Zhu, Weiqiang Wang, Linghe Kong, Yue Wang, Zhuosheng Zhang, Weiran Huang

Abstract: Multimodal perception forms the foundation of multimodal intelligence. In particular, spatial perception and multimodal long-context understanding are two pivotal tasks that underpin embodied AI and multimodal agents. However, synthesizing targeted, high-quality training data for these tasks remains difficult and costly. Our key insight is that these tasks share an underlying capability, which we call connecting evidence: identifying relevant visual facts, binding them to the correct entities, recovering their relations, and composing them into an answer. Crucially, targeted training data are far easier to synthesize for this capability than for these tasks themselves. To strengthen this capability, we propose EvidenceConnect, an easy-to-implement data synthesis framework that uses zoomed views of related regions within a single image to generate questions requiring comparison, aggregation, or chaining of visual evidence. To ensure quality, a reference answer is retained only when independent VLMs agree on it, and region-ablation tests discard questions whose answer survives the removal of selected regions. We then train base VLMs on the resulting data with RLVR, obtaining Zooming without Zooming v2 (ZwZ2). Although the synthesized data are not tailored to any downstream benchmark, ZwZ2 improves fine-grained and spatial perception, with further gains on embodied spatial reasoning, multimodal long-context understanding, multimodal agent tasks, and general visual reasoning. These results establish EvidenceConnect as an effective framework for supervising evidence connection and suggest that strengthening this shared capability leads to more generalizable multimodal perception.

cross Blindfold: Keeping the Audit Coin Secret from LLM Agents

Authors: Wei Zhu, Meiyun Zuo, Huiping Sun

Abstract: Audit-based certification bounds the violation rate of an LLM agent by auditing a random fraction $\varepsilon$ of actions and reweighting the audited labels; its validity is meant to rest only on the defender's audit coin. We show that it also rests on a confidentiality property: the coin must not interfere with the agent's view before the action commits, at a time the agent chooses. We call this pre-commit noninterference (PNI) and measure its violation by $D_{\mathrm{A}}$, the total-variation distance between the agent's views under the two coin values. PNI is necessary and sufficient for an unbiased certificate against adaptive agents, the worst-case bias is $(1-\varepsilon)D_{\mathrm{A}}$, and no procedure that sees only audited labels can pay less. Leakage composes across steps, a resource shared across episodes opens a channel of computable size, and raising the audit rate cannot restore validity. On a monitor we built, a silent audit changes no output but adds latency that tool timestamps expose, and the agent can enlarge it. A real LLM agent follows an injection that names a visible audit notice (generic, tool, and timing clauses have no effect); over that channel the plain certificate fails on measured labels and, by a post-hoc analysis, in a live run on a pre-registered schedule, while a silent audit restores it. A fresh coin per audited call closes the timing channel; padding only narrows it.

cross Quantized Edge Inference of a Self-Supervised SAR-Optical Encoder on Commodity x86 Hardware

Authors: Christian Sanchez-Leon, Dongming Chen

Abstract: Self-supervised vision transformers are increasingly used as reusable encoders for Earth observation, but their inference cost on the hardware available in the field is rarely characterized. We deploy a pretrained SAR-optical masked-autoencoder encoder on three commodity x86 nodes, from a low-power CPU without AVX2 to an AVX2+VNNI reference, at FP32, FP16 and INT8 on the CPU and, where present, the integrated GPU, and report latency, energy (RAPL and wall plug) and linear-probe accuracy. Quantization is accuracy-neutral: INT8 changes micro-mAP by only +0.0006, within the seed spread, so precision becomes a pure cost decision. INT8 lowers CPU latency on every node, by 1.45x even on the CPU without AVX2, and CPU energy wherever it is measured, and the VNNI reference (1.53x) suggests a further gain beyond AVX2. The Pareto-optimal device shifts from the integrated GPU, which is 4.38x faster than the CPU on the weakest node, to the CPU as the CPU class improves.

cross Systematic Morphometric Brain Autoencoding for Representation Learning in Neurodegenerative Diseases

Authors: Nuria Balb\'as-Esteban, Albert Belenguer-Llorens, Elaheh Moradi, Jussi Tohka, Vanessa G\'omez-Verdejo, Carlos Sevilla-Salcedo

Abstract: Early identification of neurodegenerative disorders using structural T1-weighted MRI is essential for timely clinical intervention, yet supervised deep learning models often struggle to generalize due to limited annotations and scanner-induced distribution shift. Self-supervised learning offers a promising alternative; however, existing approaches are largely adapted from natural image paradigms, relying on contrastive objectives or generic masked reconstruction with voxel-wise losses, which may not fully capture the distributed morphometric patterns associated with neurodegeneration. We introduce SYMBA (SYstematic Morphometric Brain Autoencoding), a self-supervised pretraining framework specifically designed for structural brain MRI. cross Prospector: learning a compact graph traversal policy for scientific reasoning

Authors: Haiqian Yang, Markus J. Buehler

Abstract: Scientific reasoning in specialized engineering fields depends on knowledge scattered across the literature, often proprietary and absent from frontier LLM training data. In such domains, frontier models tend to give generic answers. Knowledge graphs (KGs) can ground LLM reasoning. Because a graph is too large to provide in full, typical LLM+KG systems preselect a portion, deciding relevance before reasoning begins. Here we train a compact model, Prospector, to reason over a domain knowledge graph by traversing it step by step, seeking evidence in light of what it has already found. Prospector calls a small set of tools one at a time to progressively build its context, and answers with a chain of evidence that can be checked edge by edge against what its tools retrieved from the graph. On a knowledge graph built from the semiconductor-manufacturing literature, Prospector approaches the strongest off-the-shelf models given the same graph and tools, and it far exceeds off-the-shelf models answering closed-book or from a pre-selected portion of the graph. Although trained only on the semiconductor graph, Prospector transfers to unseen knowledge graphs from three other distinct fields: bio-inspired materials, per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) and materials properties. Served on a single GPU, Prospector answers faster and at a fraction of the cost of a hosted service, while keeping proprietary data on the owner's hardware. We find that the policy's latent state is indicative of its next move on both seen and unseen graphs, and modulating the latent state can steer the policy from one behavior mode to another. These results suggest a practical LLM + KG paradigm for scientific and engineering reasoning over private data, in which a compact model trained on a graph from published literature transfers its traversal to unseen graphs that stay with their owners.

cross How Much Can Best-of-N Help a World Action Model? Measuring Selectable Headroom and Realizing It in Closed Loop

Authors: Haojia Gao, Yue Cao, Yumin Liu, Haohua Que, Mingkai Liu, Guanqiu Guo, Zhuo Zou, Bokui Chen, Xueqian Wang

Abstract: World action models (WAMs) predict the visual consequences of the actions they generate, which makes test-time selection a natural way to improve a fixed policy: imagine the futures of candidate actions and execute the best one. The benefit of selection is bounded by the selectable headroom, how much better the best candidate truly is, but the standard estimate, which executes each candidate once and keeps the best outcome, conflates candidate quality with the randomness of everything that follows. We introduce a denoised counterfactual protocol, based on bitwise-reproducible branching, repeated continuations, held-out oracles, and common random numbers, that measures the headroom a selector can realize, and use its measurements to build a fail-gated selector. Across four WAMs, two benchmarks, over 100,000 branched rollouts, and pre-registered tests, we find that (i) the standard estimate overstates the selectable headroom of native samples: bounds from 17 continuations per candidate place its selectable share between 24% and 66% for three WAMs; (ii) structured action perturbations create up to 16 points of selectable headroom; and (iii) in both WAMs with action-conditioned imagination, imagined futures carry candidate-outcome information with no detectable loss relative to real futures, information that the value head of Cosmos Policy leaves unused. Deviating from the policy chunk only when a readout of the WAM predicts failure, the selector raises closed-loop task success from 76.9% to 81.6% on held-out episodes of training tasks, with the largest gain where the policy fails most often, and from 62.6% to 69.8% on unseen tasks.

cross Retrospective macromolecular pool fraction mapping from conventional clinical MRI: a physics-guided self-supervised deep learning framework

Authors: Jelmer van Lune, Miha Fuderer, Oscar van der Heide, Matteo Maspero, Martin B. Schilder, Jan Willem Dankbaar, Stefano Mandija, Alessandro Sbrizzi

Abstract: Macromolecular pool fraction (MPF) is a quantitative imaging biomarker sensitive to myelin integrity and other changes in tissue macromolecular content, but specialized acquisitions and reconstruction methods limit its use in large-scale studies. We present a physics-guided, self-supervised deep learning framework for retrospectively estimating MPF directly from conventional clinical brain MRI ($T_1$-weighted, $T_2$-weighted, and fluid-attenuated inversion recovery [FLAIR]) without paired quantitative reference scans. The framework embeds an analytical two-pool magnetization transfer signal model that related sequence-averaged radiofrequency transmit power to apparent longitudinal relaxation. To accommodate heterogeneous clinical protocols, the network conditions intermediate features on acquisition parameters via adaptive instance normalization. It was trained and evaluated on a multisite, multivendor, multiprotocol dataset of 7,705 MRI sessions (3 T). Across 1,237 test sessions, mean MPF was $0.182 \pm 0.007$ in white matter and $0.144 \pm 0.009$ in gray matter, with limited variation across vendor-by-protocol groups (between-group coefficient of variation: 2.65% and 5.63%, respectively). These results suggest that magnetization transfer-informed self-supervised learning enables stable MPF mapping from conventional clinical MRI, providing a scalable approach to investigate demyelinating pathology in both existing imaging archives and prospectively acquired conventional clinical scans without dedicated qMT acquisitions.

cross Doubly Robust Deep Kernel Tests for Structured Outcomes

Authors: Robin Leman, Bariscan Bozkurt, Sofiane Tanji, Arthur Gretton, Houssam Zenati

Abstract: We propose a test of whether two interventions induce the same outcome distribution when outcomes are structured and high-dimensional, such as images, text and graphs. We represent the interventional distributions as mean embeddings on learned deep features, such that the treatment effect is a doubly robust representation of the difference in mean embeddings. Our test, DR-KTE-Deep, optimises the learned features so as to maximise an estimated power criterion on this treatment effect representation. To motivate our learning objective, we show that the power criterion characterises the asymptotic signal-to-noise ratio of the test under fixed alternatives, and we derive a uniform finite-sample bound for its regularised estimate. We show that, with the learned kernel fixed, a held-out test is asymptotically valid under the null given suitable nuisance-estimation rates. cross Watermark Basins: Black-Box Detection of Distilled LLMs

Authors: Hongyan Chang, Hady Elsahar, Tom Sander, Pierre Fernandez, Saeed Mahloujifar, Sylvestre-Alvise Rebuffi, Tom\'a\v{s} Sou\v{c}ek, Tuan Tran, Valeriu Lacatusu, Surya Parimi, Rashel Moritz, Vanessa Stark, Alexandre Mourachko

Abstract: Proprietary language models and their reasoning traces are vulnerable to API-based distillation, where an adversary trains a student model on the teacher model's outputs. To obtain evidence of distillation, model providers can watermark the teacher's outputs, aiming to leave a radioactive signal in the student's outputs. Existing black-box detectors search for such radioactivity using token-level statistics of the suspect model's outputs. However, token-level signals are not always detectable, for instance when the distillation traces are sampled at a low temperature. In this work, we discover the watermark basin, a persistent signal that enables more reliable watermark detection: during fine-tuning, watermarked teacher traces embed a distinct, key-specific geometric signal in parameter space that lies orthogonal to the ordinary task-specific fine-tuning subspace. Building on this, we introduce Watermark Basin Alignment (WM-Basin), a black-box watermark detector that queries the suspect model through its API, without access to its weights or logits. WM-Basin maps generated text to gradient representations using a fixed clean probe model, projects out the ordinary fine-tuning task directions, and tests whether the residual gradient aligns with the watermark basin induced by the watermark key. We evaluate WM-Basin extensively across diverse teacher models and student architectures (from 135M to 8.2B parameters) distilled on mathematical reasoning. At a strict 0.1% false-positive rate, WM-Basin detects 79% of distilled models at n-gram=1 and 45% at n-gram=3, substantially improving over the state-of-the-art token-level baseline (29% and 1.3%, respectively). Because the watermark signal resides in a persistent basin, it survives post-distillation modifications including quantization, pruning, and reinforcement-learning post-training.

cross Low-degree polynomials lower bounds for clustering in small dimension

Authors: Alexandra Carpentier (MISTEA), Nicolas Verzelen (MISTEA)

Abstract: We study the computational limits of clustering $n$ observations drawn from a mixture of $K$ isotropic distributions in $\mathbb R^d$, in the regime where the number $K$ of groups is much larger than the dimension $d$. In this regime, tensor-based procedures recover the partition with a polylogarithmic separation between the centers, but they require a sample size that is a large and non-explicit polynomial of $dK$, whereas the existing low-degree lower bounds only show that $n$ must be larger than $dK^2$. We introduce a mixture of $2K$ product Bernoulli distributions on $\{0,1\}^d$, with $K=(d+1)^m$, whose centers are built from a dual BCH code. This construction ensures that the moment tensors of the observations coincide with those of the uniform distribution on $\{-1,1\}^d$ up to the order $2m+1$. For any fixed $m$, we establish a low-degree polynomial lower bound showing that clustering is hard as long as the separation $\Delta$ satisfies $\Delta^2\ll(K^2d^{3/2}/n)^{1/(2m+2)}\wedge\sqrt d$, up to polylogarithmic factors. In particular, with a polylogarithmic separation, low-degree polynomials require $n\gtrsim K^2d^{3/2}$ observations, up to polylogarithmic factors. Through a Gaussianization argument, this hardness carries over, in the sense of reductions, to isotropic Gaussian mixtures. Our proof relies on a family of polynomials of the observations, indexed by bipartite graphs, which is almost orthonormal. The moments of these polynomials are governed by the number of solutions of polynomial systems over the finite field $\mathbb F\_{d+1}$ that are induced by the BCH code, and the crux of our analysis is to connect the combinatorial structure of the bipartite graphs with the structure of the solution sets of these systems.

cross AnalogScout: Simulation-Efficient PVT-Aware Analog Sizing with LLM Agents

Authors: Yichen Long, Keren Zhu

Abstract: Analog device sizing must satisfy all specifications across process, voltage, and temperature (PVT) corners to ensure tape-out success, but simulating every candidate at all corners dominates the sizing time and slows design iteration. Conventional surrogate-based optimizers search the sizing space directly, while recent large language model (LLM) agents add circuit knowledge to designs but still delegate the sizes to global optimizers. Both learn the circuit response from repeated multi-corner SPICE simulations and optimize on top of it, which becomes expensive under a limited simulation budget. Providing an agent with reusable verified designs and sizing sub-blocks analytically could save most of this budget. We propose AnalogScout, a simulation-efficient PVT-aware sizing framework driven by an LLM agent. Combining elite-design reuse, analytic gm/Id local sizing and global refinement under full-corner verification, AnalogScout reduces power by 43.3% relative to the evolution strategy CMA-ES at an equal budget of 16k simulations on three SKY130 OTAs of increasing complexity, and reaches the final CMA-ES power after 7.0% of that budget. cross Online Interval Selection with Binary Predictions: Optimal Consistency and a Continuous Robustness Tradeoff

Authors: Zhong Han

Abstract: We study online interval selection with binary predictions of membership in a final optimal solution. Requests arrive in arbitrary order; accepted requests may be revoked, but rejected or revoked requests cannot be recovered. With rewards proportional to interval length, the best consistency under finite strict robustness is $d=(3+\sqrt5)/2$ when both guarantees hold almost surely on each fixed input, including for randomized algorithms. A continuous lower bound forces consistency strictly above $d$ at every robustness budget below $4\varphi+3\approx9.472$, whereas our deterministic algorithm attains $d$ with exact robustness $\varphi^5\approx11.090$, where $\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2$. A tunable replacement cost gives a continuous family with exact consistency throughout and exact robustness on two parameter intervals, including $(C,R)=(25/9,10)$ and $(4,7)$. cross The Geometry of Manifold Learning: Singular foliations via Deep Learning models

Authors: Gabriele Novelli, Eliot Tron, Emanuele Latini, Rita Fioresi

Abstract: Deep learning algorithms have become widely used and successful across a wide range of tasks. A key challenge is determining whether these algorithms truly capture the intrinsic geometry of their training data in order to better understand what they learn from data. In this theoretical work, we explore a new geometric structure defined on the data space by a trained neural network through the Data Information Matrix (DIM), a variation of the Fisher Information Matrix. This new geometric structure is a foliation and the main focus of this work is to explore the non-smooth case of ReLU activation functions, where traditional geometric tools often fail. The foliation is defined through a distribution spanned by the columns of the DIM. We first prove that this distribution is non-smooth in the sense of distributions, rendering the existence of an associated foliation much more complex. To circumvent this issue, we introduce a smoothed version of this distribution that differs from the original only on a set of measure zero. Finally, we prove that this smoothed distribution induces a singular foliation on the data space, which is based on the data the network has been trained with. Our theoretical contributions provide a geometric framework to better understand how neural networks organize their input space in non-smooth settings, bridging differential geometry and deep learning theory.

Authors: Sinjae Kang, Dong Kyu Shin, Jaehong Kim, Kamin Lee, Hyoeun Kim, Kaixin Wang, Li Zhao, Seonghyeon Ye, Kimin Lee

Abstract: Robot policy evaluation often relies on a binary success signal for task completion. cross An end-to-end framework for uncertainty-aware validation of generative models

Authors: Giada Badaracco, Sean Benevedes, Christina Reissel, Philip Harris, Gaia Grosso

Abstract: Density estimation with generative models is a common task in the physical sciences, for example in background estimation in particle physics. Existing machine learning techniques, however, lack methods to quantify uncertainties that can be propagated into a statistical test. That limits the validation of generative models to standard goodness-of-fit tests, in which the generative model is treated as exact (point-null hypothesis). But a model fitted to a finite training set is never exact, so if its uncertainties are neglected, any sufficiently powerful goodness-of-fit test will eventually reject it once the test set is large enough to resolve this estimation error. We propose SIGMA, an end-to-end framework combining density estimation with principled uncertainty quantification. We use Gaussian mixture models, for which uncertainties are tractable, and further construct a new goodness-of-fit test in which these uncertainties are profiled as nuisance parameters to account for the statistical error of the fit, promoting the conventional point-null test to a composite one. A rejection then signals genuine mismodeling rather than a test set large enough to resolve the error of the fit, provided the propagated uncertainties faithfully cover that error. We demonstrate the framework on a controlled two-dimensional toy model and on a collider physics benchmark modeling the QCD background process. With uncertainties included, the same model can be trusted on test sets up to an order of magnitude larger than the standard test allows. Our work thus provides a statistically grounded basis for a robust use of generative models in the physical sciences.

Abstract: Quickest detection problems (QDPs) arise in a wide range of applications. cross Regression Functions as Fields on Covariate Space: Evolution Equations, the Hessian, and Identification

Authors: Nobuaki Tanaka

Abstract: For each time $t$, the regression function $m_t(x)=E[Y_t\mid X_t=x]$ of an outcome on contemporaneous covariates can be regarded as a field on covariate space. We study how this field evolves when the covariates of individual units move as a diffusion and the outcome attached to each unit changes according to its own dynamics. Under mild regularity we show that $m_t$ satisfies a second-order evolution equation whose diffusion term is $\frac12\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma\nabla^2 m_t)$, the trace of the Hessian of the regression function weighted by the instantaneous covariance $\Sigma$ of the covariate increments, whose transport term is the drift of the time-reversed covariate process, and whose source term is the conditional mean rate of change of the outcome. The equation is a regression-theoretic reading of a classical transport law, and it yields a Feynman-Kac representation of $m_t$ along reversed covariate paths. We connect the Hessian appearing in the equation to the Hessian estimated by quadratic regression (under Gaussian design the best quadratic approximation has Hessian $E[\nabla^2 m(X)]$ exactly) and to the leading bias of kernel smoothers. For binary outcomes governed by bistable replicator dynamics with imitation among units with similar covariates, the closed equation is of Nagumo type, its planar travelling fronts are exactly logistic in $(x,t)$, and a single logistic regression of the outcome on covariates and time identifies the ratio of diffusion to reaction and the direction of invasion. We discuss identification under panel versus repeated cross-sectional designs and a two-stage estimation strategy, and we illustrate the results with simulations.

cross Spectral Braids: A Topological Descriptor for Dynamic Directed Networks

Authors: Mehmet Emin Aktas, Esra Akbas

Abstract: Eigenvalues are a workhorse of graph machine learning: spectral clustering, spectral graph neural networks, and graph kernels all summarize a network by the eigenvalues of a matrix. These eigenvalues are almost always treated as a static list of numbers. When a graph changes, however, its eigenvalues move, and when the matrix is not symmetric, they move in the complex plane, where they can wind around each other. The pattern of this winding is a braid, a classical object from topology. We propose spectral braids, a methodology for dynamic directed networks: each snapshot is aggregated into a community-level transition matrix, its complex eigenvalues are tracked over time, and the braid they trace is read off as a word. The word is then mapped to a matrix by the Burau representation, which gives a fixed-size feature vector for any classifier. The braid has a concrete meaning: each eigenvalue describes how a group of communities behaves, and each crossing records one group overtaking another. We evaluate the method on dynamic network classification, using synthetic networks of online communities whose labels describe how the network evolved between the same starting and ending states. Our experimental results show that the proposed features reach 97.1% accuracy on four classes, against 44.5% for the best feature set that does not track eigenvalues, and that the Burau features also separate networks containing the same events in a different order, which simple counts of crossings cannot do. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first study that defines braids and braid monodromy on graph data and uses them as features for learning on dynamic networks.

On top of the task policy, we add a learned residual safety filter with adjustable conservativeness, letting us tune the balance between performance and safety and greatly reduce object drops. In 13.3 hours of real-robot evaluation over 4,800 unseen goals across 4 objects and diverse wrist orientations, the state-based policy reaches 95.2% of goals within 2 cm and 0.4 rad, and the filter cuts its drops by 75% while still reaching 91.5% of goals. The policies can also spin a lightbulb held in an arbitrary pose, reach out-of-distribution goals, withstand fast arm motion and perturbations, and, needing only a wrist camera, serve as portable skills such as screwing in a lightbulb. We release the full recipe as a platform for studying performance, reliability, and safety in high-dimensional dexterous systems. Videos and more are available at https://ian-chuang.github.io/dexsafe/.

URLs: https://ian-chuang.github.io/dexsafe/.

cross Honeycomb-Surfels: Compact Hexagonal Primitives for Efficient Scene Rendering and Reconstruction

Authors: Prakhar Kaushik, Soumava Paul, Alan Yuille

Abstract: Novel view synthesis and 3D reconstruction have advanced rapidly with radiance fields and splatting, yet their memory and computational demands limit their use in resource-constrained settings. We introduce Hexels: novel planar hexagonal surface elements with a first-order spatial color field for efficient 3D scene and 2D image representation. Hexels exploit locally planar regions with smoothly varying appearance using fewer, larger primitives. Their hexagonal support provides an area- and boundary-efficient domain, while the local color field captures appearance variation within each primitive. Across 34 scenes, Hexels improve PSNR over memory-matched 2DGS by 4.53-7.38 dB across datasets while requiring lower training VRAM. We further ablate Hexels by showing that per primitive, Hexels outperform 2DGS by upto 4.74 dB while using lower training VRAM and having 2.31 times faster FPS. Even in unconstrained settings, Hexels offers complementary quality-memory trade-offs. For efficient 3D surface reconstruction, Hexels reduce error by 28.9% compared with memory-matched 2DGS across 15 DTU scans. On 6 Tanks and Temples surface scenes, Hexels achieves the highest mean F1 among compared methods (34.38%, versus 31.72% for 2DGS). We further also show that Hexels, as a general primitive, improves 2D image representation over Image-GS. Hexels push the Pareto frontier on the quality-efficiency trade-off of surface-aligned primitives.

replace Adaptive Momentum Benefits Score-based Generative Models

Authors: Ziqing Wen, Ping Luo, Jiahuan Wang, Xiaoge Deng, Tao Sun, Dongsheng Li

Abstract: Score-based Generative Models (SGMs) have achieved remarkable success in generation tasks by establishing a diffusion process that gradually perturbs real data into Gaussian noise and then learning a reverse process to reconstruct the data from noise. However, conventional denoising methods, such as Langevin dynamics and numerical stochastic differential equation solvers, rely on randomness but suffer from slow generation speeds due to the high number of score function evaluations. To address this, we propose a plug-and-play Markov adaptive momentum sampling method, inspired by the strong connection between the SGMs' sampling process and stochastic gradient descent optimization. Our approach accelerates the transformation process without introducing additional hyperparameters. Theoretically, we establish a rigorous foundation by analyzing the relationship between the distribution of samples generated by our method and the original data distribution. This provides a novel perspective on the analysis of sampling distributions. Empirically, our method demonstrates superior efficiency, generating high-quality images/graphs in significantly fewer sampling steps, achieving a speedup of 2x to 5x while maintaining or surpassing baseline performance on various image and graph generation benchmarks. These results underscore the effectiveness of adaptive momentum in enhancing the efficiency of SGMs.

replace A Comparative Study of Convolutional and Recurrent Neural Networks for Storm Surge Prediction in Tampa Bay

Authors: Mandana Farhang Ghahfarokhi, Anahita Farhang Ghahfarokhi, Mahta Zamanizadeh, Seyed Hossein Sonbolestan

Abstract: In this paper, we compare the performance of three popular deep learning architectures: Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM), Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN), and CNN-LSTM in water level prediction over different time window sizes. We used wind, pressure, and tide data from National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) stations. Our results show that the LSTM model generally outperformed the other models in forecasting water level trends. By applying LSTM, we achieved a mean squared error (MSE) of 0.006, making it the most reliable option for real-world use. We predicted water levels using wind, pressure, and tides. Wind, pressure, and tide data are time-based features that lack spatial patterns. Consequently, LSTM performed better than spatial methods, such as CNN. We also investigated Hurricane Ian, which occurred in Tampa Bay in September 2022 and caused a significant storm surge. Our findings show that CNN and CNN-LSTM performed slightly better than LSTMs during powerful storms, such as hurricanes. Hurricanes are seldom observed in training data, and CNN-based models are more reliable when predicting data that were not included in the training. To explain how environmental factors affect the predicted water level, we used the perturbation method. The perturbation shows that the tide is more effective at predicting water levels than wind and pressure.
