replace Where Small Mistakes Matter: Quantifying State Criticality for Imitation Learning

Authors: Yusuf Umut Ciftci, Joonwon Kang, Ajay Suresha Sathya, Somil Bansal

Abstract: Imitation learning typically treats every demonstration state as equally important, optimizing a behavior-cloning objective uniformly across the dataset. Yet not all states are created equal: in a small number of critical states, even a slight deviation from the expert action can lead to a failure, while in many other states such errors are recoverable and inconsequential. We formalize state criticality in imitation learning as the worst-case degradation in downstream value induced by a bounded perturbation of the expert action at a given state. We then propose SCRIM, a practical State CRIticality Measure that estimates this quantity by training an adversarial disturbance policy and a value critic around a fixed imitation policy. SCRIM assigns each demonstration state a score reflecting how vulnerable the task outcome is to small, policy-relevant action errors. We show that these scores can be used to reweight behavior-cloning losses, improving imitation at consequential states and increasing overall task performance. We further show that SCRIM enables data curation by selecting compact, high-value subsets of demonstrations that preserve performance while substantially reducing the amount of training data. We evaluate SCRIM across various simulated and real-world robotic manipulation tasks, and demonstrate that SCRIM improves policy training, enables efficient data selection, and provides interpretable insights into which moments in a demonstration are most critical for task success. Project website: https://sia-lab-git.github.io/SCRIM/

URLs: https://sia-lab-git.github.io/SCRIM/

replace MemWM: Associative Memory-Enhanced Latent World Models for Long-Horizon Planning

Authors: Yuwei Sun, Siyu Zhu

Abstract: World models trained via latent dynamics offer a scalable path for embodied planning, yet existing methods suffer from representational collapse, computational overhead, and limited long-horizon capability. We introduce MemWM, a memory-enhanced latent world model that augments JEPA-style prediction with a content-addressable memory bank. MemWM learns high-level representations directly from observations and uses them to condition predictions via adaptive layer normalization, complementing low-level action-conditioned dynamics within a shared latent space. To prevent memory slots from collapsing into identical representations, we propose two complementary losses that encourage structural diversity across slots and balanced utilization across time steps. On PushT, TwoRoom, Reacher, and OGBCube, MemWM attains an average success rate of 89.9%, matching DINO-WM (89.3%) while using 4$\times$ fewer trainable parameters and no pretrained encoder, and improving over LeWM by 4.1%.

replace Memorilla: Latent Semantic Memory for LLMs

Authors: Adibvafa Fallahpour, Parsa Idehpour, Vignesh Kothapalli, Nikita Mounier, Shirley Wu, Shayan Pardis, Jure Leskovec

Abstract: Memory enables large language models (LLMs) to use information beyond their parameters, such as past user conversations, long documents, or an agent's earlier actions. For a frozen LLM, access to this accumulated knowledge is limited to what a memory system places in its context window. However, gathering and combining relevant information needed for each query while keeping the model's context short remains challenging for existing systems. Here we introduce Memorilla, a memory system that gathers information from an entire document collection into a fixed number of query-dependent 'memory tokens' for a frozen LLM. Memorilla encodes documents (such as user chats, book chapters) once into reusable embeddings, then learns to retrieve and place query-specific information in the LLM's input embedding space. Using only 16 memory tokens, Memorilla achieves the best performance against baselines including Mem0 and MemoRAG on five of eight benchmarks spanning personalization, long-document and knowledge-intensive question answering. Notably, on FactKG, it reaches 84.33% accuracy versus 80.43% with full-context inference, while using 108x fewer tokens to represent the source documents. Furthermore, Memorilla can facilitate continual learning in agentic systems where the document count (the agent's past turns) increases throughout the trajectory. In a TextWorld environment, we show that Memorilla reduces the agent's tendency to wander between rooms it has already explored, and improves its average success rate from 18.6% with full-history in-context learning to 24.2%. Overall, Memorilla aids frozen LLMs in accumulating and using knowledge effectively across long-context interactions and takes a step towards building continually learning AI systems.

replace What Remains Without Leaking Test Labels? Exposing Evaluation Flaws in Graph-Level Anomaly Detection

Authors: Yujie Lu, Da Yan

Abstract: Graph-level anomaly detection (GLAD) identifies unusual graphs in applications such as molecular screening, often using only normal graphs for training. Yet the evaluations of some detectors select models with test labels, and high benchmark scores alone do not show whether detectors recognize structural changes. We audit released code and compare the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) for model states (checkpoints) chosen with and without test labels from the same training run. We separately compare test-tuned hyperparameters with repository defaults and test sensitivity to structural changes while controlling graph size. We evaluate 21 detectors on the UB-GOLD benchmark and on synthetic and molecular graphs with controlled structural changes. About half of the checkpoint-selection gain is linked to choosing a checkpoint and reporting its performance on the same labeled test set. The mean gap persists across training seeds and when normal and anomalous graphs come from different datasets. Most detectors miss many tested structural changes that simple counts detect. With tuned hyperparameters, the two leaders remain the top two by AUROC under every checkpoint rule. Across nine main datasets, test-label checkpoint selection raises the mean AUROC of UB-GOLD's 12 detectors by 5.12 points relative to the final checkpoint.

replace First-Order LoRA Training with Phone NPUs

Authors: Xu Zheng, Wei Cheng, Zhuomin Chen, Dongkuan Xu, Mo Sha, Jingchao Ni, Dongsheng Luo

Abstract: On-device fine-tuning lets a large language model (LLM) learn from its user's private data without uploading it. Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) reduces the trainable parameters, but each first-order training step still multiplies by the frozen base-model weights during the forward pass, activation recomputation, and input-gradient computation. A phone's neural processing unit (NPU) already holds these weights for inference; using it for training also requires computing input gradients, which its inference interface does not support. We present a system that runs all three frozen-weight products on the NPU while keeping the training graph, adapters, and optimizer on the CPU. Its input-gradient kernels reuse the resident weight tiles and the forward dequantizer, avoiding a transposed weight copy and ensuring forward and backward use the same effective weights. To preserve usable gradients, the system selects precision by gradient sensitivity and applies a per-row range guard for half-precision (FP16) outputs. Its runtime fuses the remaining CPU operators and overlaps NPU execution with adapter computation, while resumable execution and thermal pacing let training proceed as a background job. We evaluate it on two Qualcomm Snapdragon platforms using four LLMs from the Qwen and Llama families. Our workloads cover nightly personalization with labels supplied on the phone and the public LaMP-2 benchmark; comparisons include an optimized CPU trainer, a phone-GPU trainer, forward-only training, and GPU-trained references.

replace Maestro: Agentic Formal Proof Development by Coordination

Authors: Haoyu Li, Zeren Shui, Yuhao Zhang, Ferhat Erata, Benjamin Mikek, Murali Krishna Ramanathan, Hao Zhou

Abstract: Lean is increasingly used to verify mathematical proofs and software produced by LLM agents, but writing formal proofs remains hard. Existing prover systems take a divide-and-conquer approach that recursively decomposes hard proof goals into easier lemmas and splits again any lemma that resists proof. However, a lemma can resist proof for different reasons, including insufficient guidance, a false statement, or a flaw in the decomposition that produced it. Existing systems cannot tell these apart as a failed attempt reports little beyond its failure, and responses are confined to the failing node without considering the rest of the proof or revising the plan above it. The system may thus keep decomposing obligations along a flawed proof direction until the budget runs out, causing the whole proof attempt to fail. We introduce Maestro, a Lean prover harness that handles failures through global coordination. Provers report why they failed, not just that they failed, and a coordinator that sees the whole proof chooses an appropriate response, such as guiding a stuck prover, correcting a false lemma, or retracting a flawed subtree and replanning it. The same harness extends to verified code synthesis, where a checked counterexample sends the code back for repair. We evaluate Maestro with four frontier LLMs on seven mathematics and three software verification benchmarks. It proves more targets than each model's native coding agent (Claude Code or Codex) on every benchmark the agent has not already saturated, with the largest gains on research-level mathematics. With Claude Opus 5, it discovers its own proofs of 34 of the 48 theorems in the June 2026 release of arXivLean, against 19 for the native agent. It also outperforms all published prover systems on every benchmark they share, solving 669/672 PutnamBench, 59/60 Lean-IMO-Bench, and 6/6 IMO 2026.

replace Hilbert-Scale Stability of Posterior Diffusion Priors for Ill-Posed Signal Recovery

Authors: Bahman Moraffah, Raha Moraffah

Abstract: Indirect and noisy observations are difficult to invert when the forward operator smooths out the unknown signal. In this case, the unstable modes are precisely the modes that the data reveals least, thus the success of any reconstruction method depends on how prior information interacts with the geometry of the operator across regularity scales. Diffusion priors have become ubiquitous for such problems; however, the current theory still does not determine when a learned score remains informative under ill-posed forward operators. In this paper, we study posterior diffusion for linear inverse problems on Hilbert scales and derive a stability theory under an explicit score-amplification condition. Under a smoothing condition on the forward map, a source condition on the truth, and weak-norm control of score error, we derive non-asymptotic recovery bounds in \(\Hs^s\) that separate observation noise, score approximation, and reverse-time discretization. These bounds give a recoverable regularity threshold determined jointly by operator smoothing, truth regularity, score regularity, and the posterior correction. The threshold specifies the Hilbert-scale norms \(\Hs^s\) in which weak-norm score error remains controlled after propagation through the posterior reverse dynamics. For larger regularity indices \(s\), the inverse geometry magnifies the same error, and the Hilbert-scale recovery estimate no longer gives uniform stability. We also prove that a posterior correction adapted to the forward operator improves stability relative to isotropic guidance in the regularity ranges where the smoothing action of the forward map matters most. The recovery estimates give a regularity-sensitive theory of posterior diffusion and show how learned score error, operator smoothing, and reconstruction norm jointly determine the stable range predicted by the analysis.

replace The Shape of the DPO Hyperparameter Landscape: What to Measure, How It Moves with Scale, and When Not to Transfer It

Authors: Hannan Mahadik, David Salinas, Jacek Golebiowski, Aaron Klein

Abstract: Direct preference optimisation (DPO) is widely used for language model post-training, but its performance depends strongly on the choice of its hyperparameters, such as the learning rate and the reference-policy regularisation parameter $\beta$. To better understand the DPO hyperparameter landscape, we conduct extensive experiments using a dense hyperparameter grid across different model scales from the same family. This allows us to analyse how the optimal DPO hyperparameters evolve with model size and the number of update steps, with intermediate checkpoints providing an inexpensive signal to accelerate the hyperparameter optimisation process. Furthermore, by evaluating our models on both static and dynamic benchmarks, we identify which benchmarks provide the strongest signal for hyperparameter selection. Finally, we investigate how different state-of-the-art hyperparameter optimisation strategies perform in this setting. Our experiments show that the optimal learning rate varies with model size and that hyperparameters are not transferable across training datasets. We also find that validation loss is a poor proxy for hyperparameter optimisation, as hyperparameters that minimize validation loss only weakly correlate with optimal hyperparameters for downstream benchmarks. Therefore, DPO hyperparameters need to be tuned specifically for the target model, dataset, and evaluation objective. We also show that short candidate training runs can substantially reduce the cost of this selection. We release all our training configurations and evaluation results to support reproducible analysis of selection quality and training cost.

replace BRIDGE: An Acceptance Gate to Block False Improvements in Model Updates, with a Retrospective Clinical Evaluation

Authors: Dwi Sunaryono

Abstract: Models are updated repeatedly, and an update that looks better on validation data but not on new data is a false improvement that can misguide clinical decisions. The naive rule accepts every positive validation gain and is optimistic when the same data choose and judge the update. BRIDGE is a model-agnostic acceptance gate that decides each update from predictions and the available labels, with fixed settings. Each fold of the data judges the update chosen on the other folds (cross-fitting) to reduce selection bias; acceptance requires the gain's lower bootstrap bound to exceed a margin. The four settings are (A) 11 new datasets, (B) synthetic tasks, (C) 12 retrospective clinical tasks, and (D) 39 supporting datasets. The false-improvement rate falls from 19.1% (naive) to 1.1% (BRIDGE) in (A), 20.7% to 1.7% (B), 20.0% to 0% (C), and 12.5% to 0.5% (D); every 95% confidence interval of the reduction over datasets or tasks excludes zero (not over the clinical sources). An equal-size random draw from the naive acceptances contains more false improvements in (A)-(E) (post hoc, p<0.001). On the OpenML collection (E) of 14 new datasets, the false-improvement rate falls from 25.6% to 1.0%. BRIDGE removes 97% to 100% of the false improvements that the naive rule accepts, and its mean gain per deployed update is 1.8 to 3.1 times the naive rule's. The 95% upper bound on the false-improvement rate is below 5.5%: formal in (B) for independent tasks (3.56%), and conditional on the real data tested (0.33% to 5.40%).

replace Loopholing All the Way: Language Modeling with pure Self-Conditioning

Authors: Adrien Deschenaux, Nadezhda Ilieva, Justin Deschenaux, Manuel Madeira, Dorina Thanou, Aur\'elien Lucchi, Antonio Orvieto, Caglar Gulcehre, Pascal Frossard

Abstract: Recent Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) use Self-Conditioning (SC), or Loopholing, to improve their performance. While SC is traditionally seen as an auxiliary heuristic, its empirical success raises the question: can a continuous generative model be based purely on Self-Conditioning? We answer affirmatively with LAW (``Loopholing All the Way''), which propagates the Expected Embedding under the denoiser's prediction across steps, decoding the tokens once, at the end. On Sudoku, LAW outperforms previous Discrete and Continuous Diffusion Models, including Loopholing Discrete Diffusion. On Maze, LAW is the most accurate from 2 to 64 NFE. After training on TinyGSM and sampling at temperature 1 with 1024 NFE, LAW solves 42.2% of GSM8K problems against 34.5% for uniform Loopholing Discrete Diffusion. Additionally, fine-tuning a pre-trained Uniform Diffusion Model (Duo) with LAW increases its accuracy from 17.2% to 48.1%. Finally, on OpenWebText, LAW pushes the Pareto frontier and approaches Autoregressive Models (ARMs) with 64 NFE. Find our code at https://github.com/ilievanadezhda/law.

replace Exact Spectral Uncertainty Decomposition for Linear-Gaussian Bayesian M/EEG Inverse Problems

Authors: Ismail Huseynov, Stefan Haufe

Abstract: Distributed magneto- and electroencephalographic (M/EEG) source reconstruction is a severely underdetermined linear inverse problem. Bayesian source imaging regularizes inversion through source-prior and sensor-noise covariances, but the posterior covariance does not directly distinguish uncertainty constrained by the measurements from uncertainty unobservable under the sensing geometry. We study this distinction for linear-Gaussian M/EEG models with diagonal source-prior and sensor-noise covariances, covering Type-I inference and Type-II inference conditional on learned covariance hyperparameters. After restricting the analysis to the prior-supported source space, standardizing the prior, and whitening the sensor noise, the complete singular spectrum of the forward operator separates likelihood-visible and likelihood-null directions. This yields an additive uncertainty decomposition of the conditional posterior covariance: uncertainty in likelihood-visible directions is reduced according to spectral information content, whereas likelihood-null uncertainty remains unchanged from the prior. The spectral representation also yields a prior-minus-reduction identity, showing that covariance reduction is confined to the likelihood-visible subspace. Spectral markers quantify likelihood information and prior-variance retention and reduction. Fixed-orientation MEG simulations with adaptive noise learning illustrate the framework. Dense Bayesian minimum-norm inference retains a large likelihood-null component, whereas sparse $\gamma$-MAP strongly reduces the fitted prior support. Within that support, the markers distinguish strongly informed directions from a weakly constrained direction. Thus, prior-support selection determines which source coordinates remain in the fitted model, while the spectral decomposition quantifies how strongly the retained directions are constrained by the measurements.

replace Benchmarking Model Compression Techniques Across Heterogeneous Edge Platforms: A Predictive Maintenance Use Case for Rotating Machinery

Authors: Matheus Calheira Guimar\~aes de Oliveira, Marco Aur\'elio da Silva Cruz, Regis Cardoso, Lucas Cambuim, Rafael Macieira, Arthur Paim, Helaine Neves, Alex Santos, Lilian Guarieiro, Jean Schmith, Prashant Kumar, Erick G. Sperandio Nascimento

Abstract: To enable Industry 5.0, where autonomous systems are widely adopted and integrated with people to promote sustainable development in industries, it is imperative to adapt complex deep learning models to operate efficiently on resource-constrained hardware for edge inference. However, adapting these models remains challenging due to the hardware heterogeneity of edge computing in industrial settings, performance degradation caused by model compression, and the lack of clear guidance on which compression techniques are practical and effective for applications such as predictive maintenance. To address these challenges, this paper presents a comprehensive comparative analysis of four state-of-the-art model compression techniques applied to a high-performance convolutional neural network for predictive maintenance using a real-world dataset of rotating machinery, equipment typically used in industry. The compressed models were evaluated across a heterogeneous set of nine computing platforms, ranging from highly constrained edge computing devices to general purpose and high-performance processors, reflecting the diversity and fragmentation typical of industrial edge environments. By jointly prioritizing classification performance, inference speed, and compression ratio, we found that compression using knowledge distillation emerged as the most effective approach. The model had more than 90% speedup across all devices while maintaining a 91.22% balanced F-score, with a very small drop in predictive performance relative to the uncompressed model, along with a 98.9% size reduction. Through successful deployment across multiple edge platforms and a systematic comparison of compression strategies, we demonstrated a clear path to achieving Industry 5.0 targets, drastically cutting computational costs and, consequently, energy consumption without sacrificing predictive performance.

replace Same Luck, Different Choice: Twin-World Credit Assignment for Agentic Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Zeropy Surio, Tobias Engel, Mei-Lin Chou, Anika Raman, Samuel Okafor

Abstract: Agentic reinforcement learning rewards a complete episode, yet the policy needs credit for every decision inside it. Counterfactual branching estimates this credit by re-running the episode after an alternative decision, but the branch draws its sampling noise independently of the factual episode, so the outcome difference mixes the effect of the decision with the luck of everything that follows. We observe that in agentic search the retriever, the corpus, and the grader are deterministic, so an episode is a deterministic function of the policy's decisions and its sampling noise. Twin worlds are therefore realizable: the same noise, a different decision. We introduce TWICE (TWIn-world Credit Estimation), which keys the Gumbel noise of every token by turn and line, rolls out counterfactual twins that inherit the factual episode's downstream noise, and turns their paired outcome differences into turn-level advantages for policy-gradient updates on actor-sampled trajectories; only the actor is deployed. Twin credit is unbiased for any coupling quality, its variance is that of an independent counterfactual scaled by $1-\rho$, where $\rho$ is the twins' outcome correlation, and under coupling the number of rollouts needed to resolve an advantage of size $\Delta$ falls from $\Theta(\Delta^{-2})$ to $\Theta(\Delta^{-1})$. A square-root rule spends the twin budget where outcomes are predicted to disagree per unit cost. On seven question-answering benchmarks, TWICE improves the average exact match of GRPO by 5.3 and 4.5 points on Qwen2.5-3B and 7B, outperforms tree-search and counterfactual-branching baselines, and uses 68% fewer auxiliary tokens than VinePPO.

replace A Fix for Softmax and Cross-Entropy

Authors: Jonathan T. Barron, Dan Gnanapragasam, Michael Tschannen

Abstract: Standard implementations of softmax, log-softmax, and cross-entropy have two numerical bugs. The first bug, Softmax Collapse, rounds the target logit's gradient to zero if that one logit is much larger than the others, resulting in gradients that no longer sum to zero. The second bug, Softmax Cancellation, occurs when student logits are close to teacher logits, and produces non-zero gradients even when the student and teacher logits are equal. We present simple implementations of log-softmax, softmax, and cross-entropy that fix both bugs. Our fix eliminates the previously observed "slingshot" phenomenon, speeds up grokking with weight decay, prevents accuracy collapse without weight decay, stops student networks from drifting away from the teacher during distillation, and shows that after memorization, logit growth is bounded by an equilibrium between weight decay and Adam's $\epsilon$ hyperparameter.

replace CuraLoc: Repository-Level Fault Localization via Planned Code Inspection

Authors: Sangeeta Warrier, Anush Sankaran, Ryen W. White

Abstract: A bug report describes what went wrong, but rarely where the code should change. Existing repository-level localization methods often let language models iteratively decide what code to inspect next, requiring repeated model calls and substantial token usage. We investigate whether these inspection decisions can instead be planned before source retrieval. In this research, we introduce CuraLoc, which first selects candidate files and represents their symbols using compact behavioral and structural descriptions. In a single planning decision, the model jointly selects starting symbols, chooses code relationships to inspect from each, and forms hypotheses about likely edits. Deterministic retrieval collects the requested source for a final ranking informed by those hypotheses, concentrating model reasoning on choosing and comparing code rather than directing each retrieval step. We demonstrate the efficacy of our proposed approach on the SWE-Bench Lite and Verified datasets. On SWE Bench Verified with GPT-5.2, CuraLoc raises Func@1 from LocAgent's 53.8% to 59.3% while using 90.3% fewer recorded tokens, and also outperforms RepoSearcher (56.2%) and Agentless (39.6%) on Func@1. On SWE-bench Lite, CuraLoc achieves a high 83.8% Func@5 accuracy improving on Agentless by more than 15% in relative accuracy. Additionally, CuraLoc supports different model backbones, with strong localization accuracy demonstrated using both GPT-5.2 and GPT-6 Astra. These results provide evidence that repository-level code inspection can be planned effectively without iterative model-controlled navigation.

replace Rethinking Multi-View Fusion for Urban Representation Learning

Authors: Sean Bin Yang, Zongyi Xu, Jilin Hu, Ying Sun, Kristian Torp, Bin Yang, Torben Bach Pedersen, Christian S. Jensen

Abstract: Urban region representation learning encodes heterogeneous urban signals, such as mobility flows, points of interest, and land-use patterns, into unified embeddings for downstream tasks, including check-in prediction, crime forecasting, and service demand estimation. Existing approaches rely on globally shared fusion and joint optimization, implicitly assuming uniform modality importance and naturally balanced multi-objective training. However, urban data is inherently heterogeneous. Our empirical analysis reveals two structural issues: (i) modality utility varies markedly across regions, causing static fusion to overemphasize signals from certain modalities; and (ii) heterogeneous objectives exhibit persistent gradient imbalance, causing representational bias and unstable training. We present PRISM, a self-adjusting framework for adaptive multi-view learning. PRISM adapts sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) routing to learn region-specific view allocations, while a Proportional-Integral-Derivative (PID)-inspired regulation mechanism mitigates gradient conflicts during optimization. Experiments on three large-scale urban datasets show that PRISM achieves more competitive performance than strong multi-view baselines, while providing more stable optimization behavior and more balanced region representations.

replace Tree Depth and Sample-Quality-Associated Features: Two Hidden Factors in Boruta Selection for RNA-Seq Data Classification

Authors: Tareque Mohmud Chowdhury, Abu Raihan Mostofa Kamal

Abstract: Boruta is a widely used method for selecting gene panels from RNA sequencing data, and its most used implementation recommends Random Forest trees of depth 3 to 7, an untested setting. We tested whether this setting changes which genes are selected, using pan-cancer data from The Cancer Genome Atlas (10,469 tumors, 33 cancer types) and breast cancer PAM50 subtyping (981 tumors, 5 subtypes). A leakage-free protocol fitted every label-dependent step inside training folds, measured every effect against seed-to-seed variation, and held out a locked test set per task. In pan-cancer data, shallow trees changed the selection far more than a new seed did: the top-500 overlap with unlimited-depth selections was 0.37 at depth 5, against 0.84 to 0.93 between seeds at depth 5 or unlimited depth. Shallow trees confirmed 3.5 times fewer genes, missed markers of large classes and produced less accurate 50-gene panels (balanced accuracy 0.809 vs 0.889). The effect vanished once the cap reached about 60 to 75% of the depth that unrestricted trees grow to, set mainly by the number of classes, not sample size, and leaving depth uncapped cost no accuracy in either task. The effect replicated in a second cross-validation repeat and under permutation importance. About a third of pre-filtered candidates also tracked sample quality within class; a within-class filter removed them without loss of test accuracy (balanced accuracy 0.943 vs 0.942 with 1,000 genes). We recommend uncapped trees, or a cap chosen from the realized tree depth, and a within-class quality filter before Boruta.

replace The Cost of an Unknown Horizon for the Last Iterate of Clipped SGD under Heavy-Tailed Noise

Authors: Yuxing Peng, Zhiqing Tang, Weijia Jia

Abstract: Training is often stopped at a data-dependent time, so guarantees for the last iterate must hold at all times simultaneously. We determine the optimal time-uniform rate for the last iterate of clipped SGD on smooth convex objectives under heavy-tailed noise with a bounded central $p$th moment, $1\beta_p$, even with adaptive step sizes and thresholds. This rate lies between the optimal rate $O(T^{-\alpha_p})$ for a known horizon $T$, $\alpha_p=(p-1)/p$, and the previous time-uniform rate $\tilde O(t^{-2(p-1)/(3p)})$ (Chezhegov et al., ICLR 2026); under finite variance, the exponents are $1/2$, $2/5$, and $1/3$. Their conjecture that a better schedule reaches the optimal rate thus holds with a known horizon (Liu; Jiang et al.) but fails without one. Querying gradients at the running average attains $\tilde O(t^{-\alpha_p})$ at all times, whereas heavy-ball momentum with geometric forgetting and polynomially bounded steps cannot improve on $\beta_p$.

replace Climate downscaling with likelihood functional flow matching

Authors: Zhi-Song Liu, Helmi Toropainen, Ammar Kheder, Michael Boy

Abstract: Decisions about heat, frost and flooding are made locally, yet reanalyses and climate models resolve tens of kilometres. Generative downscalers add kilometre-scale detail with uncertainty, but are tied to a grid, costly to sample and lack a tractable likelihood. Here we introduce CD-FFM, a functional flow-matching model that treats the high-resolution field as a continuous random field that one model can query at any location and resolution. Its velocity acts pointwise, so spatial variability comes from a noise prior fitted to the observed spectrum and every observation has an exact likelihood. Downscaling ERA5 to 1 km daily temperature over Fennoscandia, CD-FFM lowers the continuous ranked probability score by 27-31% against regression and 12-18% against a forty-times larger neural operator in each of three test years, and outperforms a CorrDiff-style diffusion model while sampling about fifty times faster. replace INTRA: Interleaved Non-contiguous Token spaRse Attention

Authors: Yuxuan Xia, Wenbo Lu, Shaoyi Zheng, Shengjie Wang

Abstract: Transformers achieve strong performance across modalities but are bottlenecked by the quadratic cost of attention. Sparse attention can reduce this cost, but effective sparsity must preserve useful long-range communication and map well to GPU hardware. We introduce INTRA--Interleaved Non-contiguous Token spaRse Attention--a hardware-aware static sparse attention design based on a relay-communication view under Computational Query Set (CQS) constraints. Rather than directly covering all token-to-token interactions within each sparse layer, INTRA uses complementary sparse groups to provide short mediated communication paths across layers. Scatter forms relay groups that sample broadly across local neighborhoods, while Gather reconnects these groups in subsequent layers. Under a partition model, this structure guides CQS-compatible sparse patterns with global reachability. Our CQS-aware FlashAttention-style kernel computes structured indices at runtime, loads selected non-contiguous tokens, and packs them densely for computation. On LLaMA-3.1-8B, it improves long-context prefill efficiency with strong teacher-forced retrieval performance under matched LoRA adaptation. replace SEEK: Skill Extraction with Evolving Knowledge against Frontier Science Agents

Authors: Cheng Gui, Xiang Zheng, Yutao Wu, Hanxun Huang, Yige Li, Kaidi Xu, Cong Wang, Wei-Ying Ma

Abstract: Science agents increasingly package domain expertise as reusable skills that plan analyses, select tools, and execute workflows. This modularity creates a confidentiality risk: the interactions that deliver a skill's capability can also reveal the skill itself. We build an extensible benchmark of 40 protected skills across eight open- and closed-source science-agent platforms, comparing direct, fixed-strategy, and bandit-guided iterative probing under reference-based and reference-free evaluation. Leakage is pervasive: all eight platforms (100%) leak at least one protected skill, and four reach 100% attack success under a single direct request. To stress-test platforms that resist simple probing, we propose SEEK (Skill Extraction with Evolving Knowledge), a black-box, self-evolving red-teaming agent that consolidates response-derived evidence in persistent memory and evolves reusable attack skills, using only user-visible responses. On 12 skills from SpatialAgent and Biomni, SEEK achieves 100% attack success while reducing the mean first-success round by 31.8% and 38.2% relative to bandit-guided probing. Against a confidentiality policy that blocks all three benchmark protocols, SEEK still extracts one of six protected skills. These findings show that current science agents do not reliably protect their skills and that defenses must be evaluated against adaptive, multi-turn attackers.

replace STeP-TS: Shared Temporal Filters with Pruning for Time Series

Authors: Hanne Raum, Jasper Hoffmann, Joschka Boedecker

Abstract: Multivariate time series forecasting requires capturing temporal dependencies across multiple scales while accounting for both shared and variable-specific dynamics. Compact forecasting models have emerged as an attractive alternative to increasingly large architectures, but preserving strong predictive performance within tight computational and memory budgets remains challenging. We propose a compact one-layer convolutional framework that learns a shared bank of multi-scale temporal filters of each variable. Lightweight variable-specific scalar weights determine the contribution of each temporal component, enabling the model to capture shared temporal structure while adapting to variable-specific dynamics. The learned weights and filter-output energy further provide a natural signal for structured pruning of convolutional filters, reducing computational complexity with minimal degradation in forecasting performance. We additionally introduce an amplitude-adaptive normalization mechanism that preserves variable-dependent temporal amplitudes. Experiments on multiple datasets demonstrate forecasting performance competitive with state-of-the-art methods while requiring substantially fewer parameters and inference FLOPs and achieving low per-sample latency.

replace Shared Context, Separate Engines: Streaming Vision--Language Inference on Edge SoCs

Authors: Yilong Li, Chengpo Yan, Yijing Zeng, Hanlei Wu, Mingjun Song, Suman Banerjee

Abstract: Edge SoCs pack CPUs, GPUs, and NPUs, yet vision--language model (VLM) runtimes keep generation on one engine and leave the others idle. Streaming VLMs expose further parallelism: new frames arrive while earlier queries are still being answered. Engines differ in compute and memory access and contend when they co-run, so no single placement of stages wins across SoCs and workloads. Backend-local KV state keeps other engines from continuing the work. \name coordinates heterogeneous accelerators to execute stateful VLM inference concurrently. It separates a stream's versioned context from each engine's KV state, so any engine can continue the stream by reusing, importing, or reconstructing that state. Stateful Streaming Generation ingests new frames on one engine while another answers, and Concurrent Sequence Generation serves different ready requests in parallel. State-aware dispatch sends each request to the engine expected to finish it first. Each answer reads a fixed, published version of its history and matches its serial reference. \name beats the strongest single-engine baselines on Apple M4 Max, Rockchip RK3588, and Qualcomm QCS6490, even when they reuse context. On two RK3588 video streams, it delivers $19.6\%$ more throughput than the best single engine with context reuse and $27.2\%$ more than parallel engines without shared state. Cross-engine KV import cuts first-token latency by a factor of $4.6$ over prefix recomputation. Stateful Streaming Generation delivers up to $2.48\times$ the throughput of serial execution, and state-aware dispatch cuts query completion time by up to $72.7\%$.

replace CoPE-CGL: Community-aware Prototype Extraction for Replay-Free Continual Graph Learning

Authors: Bo-Young Lim, Seokhwan Kim, Kisung Lee, Hyuk-Yoon Kwon

Abstract: Continual graph learning (CGL) maintains models as graphs evolve without retraining from scratch, while mitigating catastrophic forgetting. Existing methods typically target either transductive or inductive settings and often store past subgraphs or require task identifiers at inference. We propose Community-aware Prototype Extraction for Continual Graph Learning (CoPE-CGL), a replay-free framework that uses a parameter-free structural prior, precomputed by multi-hop Laplacian smoothing, to anchor learning, memory, and inference. CoPE-CGL combines topology-infused bottleneck adaptation, community-level prototypes, and dynamic routing by majority vote over node-to-prototype matches. The default protocol assumes each test graph comes from one learned task; a node-level variant handles mixed-task graphs. Each task adds at most a constant amount of storage, with no past graph data retained. At equal storage and without task identifiers, CoPE-CGL matches the strongest replay baseline even when it receives true task identifiers: average performance (AP) is 95.17% and 95.72% versus 94.28% and 95.82% on Arxiv and CoraFull, respectively, with 5.9-14.0 times faster runtime and zero forgetting. Across four transductive and three inductive benchmarks and mixed streams, CoPE-CGL exceeds the second-best of up to eleven baselines by up to 1.41, 6.32, and 9.33 AP percentage points, respectively. It runs 1.3-7.9 times faster end-to-end than the most accurate competitor on each benchmark and scales to the largest benchmark graph. On graphs mixing two tasks, its node-level variant achieves the best AP among CGL methods. The design targets homophilic graphs with community structure.

replace Multi-Fidelity Training Reshapes Uncertainty Decomposition in Atomistic Models

Authors: Matteo Tolloso, Davide Bacciu, Luigi Bonati

Abstract: Multi-fidelity training, which combines abundant low-fidelity data with scarce high-fidelity labels, is increasingly common in atomistic modeling, but its effect on predictive uncertainty remains largely unexplored. We investigate this question using deep ensembles of mean-variance MACE models trained on ANI-1x, contrasting high-fidelity training from scratch with low-fidelity pretraining followed by fine-tuning. We characterize aleatoric, epistemic, and total uncertainty throughout training and under two distribution shifts: unseen molecular systems and higher-energy configurations. We find that pretraining reduces prediction error and improves uncertainty-based ranking across all test sets, but does not yield well-calibrated predictive variances; calibration deteriorates more severely under energy extrapolation. Training-dynamics analysis localizes most of this calibration degradation to high-fidelity optimization, during which the learned aleatoric variance becomes overconfident on held-out data. Moreover, multi-fidelity fine-tuning produces a more effective uncertainty-based acquisition function than high-fidelity training from scratch. These findings show that uncertainty ranking and calibration capture distinct properties and must be assessed separately in multi-fidelity atomistic models, especially under extrapolation.

replace Quantitative Generalization Bounds for Multi-Layer Transformers: A Few Long Strings can Beat Many Short Ones

Authors: Marius Henry, Michael Rizvi-Martel, Michael Hahn

Abstract: Transformer-based language models exhibit an astonishing capacity to generalize to longer and more difficult problem instances than those seen in training. However, theoretical understanding of this phenomenon still lags behind. Unlike most machine learning models, the generalisation abilities of transformers inherently require considering two axes. On one hand, one must examine in-distribution statistical generalisation, which concerns the ability of a model to broaden a function from training samples to novel samples of the same distribution. On the other hand, given the sequential nature of language data, one must also consider length generalisation, which reflects whether a model trained on inputs of a given length can extrapolate to longer ones. These two notions have mostly been studied separately, leaving open how they interact. In this work, we give a joint treatment of both axes for fixed-precision transformers. We give generalisation guarantees in terms of the parameter sizes of the involved models, as well as the quantization space and the alphabet size that supply the sequences. Finally, we illustrate how the requirements in the sample count and training length trade off. In particular, we find that increasing training length can be exponentially more efficient than increasing the sample size. Experiments support predictions of the theory.

Authors: Xi Chen, Chongjian Ge, Yiran Xu, Yiqun Mei, Henrik I. Christensen, Hao Tan, Ziwen Chen, Hanwen Jiang

Abstract: As autonomous research systems become more capable, benchmarks must both evaluate their performance and support meaningful research progress. We introduce Auto-Research Arena, a multi-target benchmark for evaluating autonomous research systems and discovering ideas in language modeling. Arena supports diverse research objectives within a shared experimental environment by separating admissibility from improvement: model quality and resource constraints determine whether a result qualifies, while the selected target measures progress among admissible results. We instantiate Arena in language model pretraining with nine targets spanning model, data, training, and inference efficiency, and evaluate seven research harnesses using two foundation models. Arena clearly differentiates existing harnesses across research objectives, with several sophisticated methods outperformed by our simple sequential search baseline, indicating substantial headroom in current auto-research systems. Beyond evaluation, Arena reveals a substantially broader frontier of model and system ideas aligned with active research directions. Reusing these discoveries in a new quality-driven search yields lower training loss under matched data and FLOPs and lower held-out BPB at the same model size. Together, Arena provides a common framework for benchmarking autonomous research and expanding its scope in language modeling. The code is available at https://github.com/Auto-Research-Arena/auto-research-arena

replace-cross NAG-GS: A Semi-Implicit Stochastic Optimizer with Large-Step Stability

Authors: Valentin Leplat, Daniil Merkulov, Aleksandr Katrutsa, Daniel Bershatsky, Olga Tsymboi, Ivan Oseledets

Abstract: Stochastic first-order methods are widely used for large-scale optimization and machine learning, where their performance often depends on the learning rate. We study NAG-GS, a stochastic momentum method based on a semi-implicit Gauss--Seidel discretization of a Nesterov-type flow. For strongly convex quadratics, we determine the exact range of stable step sizes for fixed damping and the step size that minimizes the worst-case spectral radius. When the damping equals the strong-convexity parameter, the method recovers the accelerated asymptotic factor $(\sqrt{\kappa}-1)/(\sqrt{\kappa}+1)$, where $\kappa$ is the condition number. We also prove that the quadratic iteration converges linearly with dynamic damping when its limiting fixed-damping iteration is stable. For general strongly convex, possibly nonsmooth objectives, we establish an $O(1/k)$ bound on the expected objective gap of the last iterate, using a conditionally unbiased stochastic first-order oracle with bounded second moment. The result holds with dynamic damping from any $\gamma_0>0$. We show that NAG-GS can also be written as a Heavy-Ball iteration with explicitly determined step sizes and momentum coefficients. We test the stochastic result on nonsmooth hinge-loss classification problems, and further evaluate NAG-GS on quadratic problems, logistic regression, convolutional neural networks, RoBERTa, and Vision Transformers. The experiments show that NAG-GS can remain effective at learning rates where standard optimizers become unstable or lose accuracy.

replace-cross Nash Incentive-compatible Online Mechanism Learning via Weakly Differentially Private Online Learning

Authors: Joon Suk Huh, Kirthevasan Kandasamy

Abstract: We study a multi-round mechanism design problem, where we interact with a set of agents over a sequence of rounds. We wish to design an incentive-compatible (IC) online learning scheme to maximize an application-specific objective within a given class of mechanisms, without prior knowledge of the agents' type distributions. Even if each mechanism in this class is IC in a single round, if an algorithm naively chooses from this class on each round, the entire learning process may not be IC against non-myopic buyers who appear over multiple rounds. On each round, our method randomly chooses between the recommendation of a weakly differentially private online learning algorithm (e.g., Hedge), and a commitment mechanism which penalizes non-truthful behavior. Our method is IC and achieves $O(T^{\frac{1+h}{2}})$ regret for the application-specific objective in an adversarial setting, where $h$ quantifies the long-sightedness of the agents. When compared to prior work, our approach is conceptually simpler,it applies to general mechanism design problems (beyond auctions), and its regret scales gracefully with the size of the mechanism class.

Abstract: Language Models (LMs) have demonstrated exceptional performance across various Natural Language Processing (NLP) tasks. replace-cross A Deep Learning Framework for TTV Characterization: A Case Study of the Kepler-88 System

Authors: Chen Chen, Jiapeng Gao, Gongjie Li, Lingkai Kong, Molei Tao

Abstract: Transit timing variation (TTV) provides rich information about the mass and orbital properties of exoplanets, which are often obtained by solving an inverse problem via Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC). In this paper, we design a new data-driven approach, which potentially can be applied to problems that are hard to traditional MCMC methods, such as the case with only one planet transiting. Specifically, we use a deep learning approach to predict the parameters of non-transit companion for the single transit system with transit information (i.e., TTV, and Transit Duration Variation (TDV)) as input. This approach is constructed using the Transformer-based architecture that can extract long-range interactions from TTV sequential data. When applied to the test set, our model achieves low fractional errors of less than a few percent for systems with TTV semi-amplitudes ranging from $\sim 0.3$ s to $\sim 5$ min. Extending our approach to a broader range of planetary architectures, including systems with substantially weaker or stronger TTV signals and those containing additional planets or stellar companions, remains an important direction for future work.

replace-cross Non-asymptotic Estimation of Sparse Partial Correlation via Joint Partial Regression

Authors: Samuel Erickson, Tobias Ryd\'en

Abstract: Partial correlation analysis is widely used in modern multivariate analysis. However, prior methods lack non-asymptotic guarantees and have slow asymptotic estimation rates. We present a method for simultaneously estimating sparse high-dimensional partial correlation matrices and inverse covariance with non-asymptotic rates. The method is a two-stage estimation which exploits the connection between the inverse covariance matrix and linear regression estimation method while enforcing positive semi-definiteness simultaneously. replace-cross A Mechanistic Interpretation of Prescribed-Parameter Convolutional Kernels

Authors: Jorge Marco Blanco, Rub\'en Cuevas Rum\'in

Abstract: Prescribed-parameter convolutional transforms achieve strong time series classification performance using fixed kernels, simple pooling operations, and a linear classifier. Their success raises a mechanistic question: which properties of the representation make this construction effective? We study the proportion of positive values (PPV), a pooling operator shared by ROCKET, MiniROCKET, and MultiROCKET. We establish exact invariance to circular translations under periodic boundaries and quantify departures from invariance under finite boundaries. A complementary analysis bounds the sensitivity of a smooth PPV surrogate. We then define the population pairwise geometry induced by a distribution of convolutional parameters and show how a finite random kernel bank concentrates around that geometry. This separates the role of the parameter distribution from the number of sampled kernels. Experiments on the UCR classification collection examine effective dimensionality, distance correlation with the input, and classification accuracy using PPV-only, unit-energy ROCKET kernels. Increasing kernel count produces diminishing representational gains. Increasing kernel length generally raises effective dimensionality and distance correlation, while the best test performance occurs at intermediate lengths and training accuracy remains close to perfect. Together, these results support an interpretation in which nonlinear pooling creates a richer but structured representation that can favour linear separation, while further representational richness need not improve generalisation.

replace-cross Foreclassing: From Forecasts to Decisions

Authors: Daniel Andrew Coulson, Martin T. Wells

Abstract: Time series forecasts are widely used to inform decisions under uncertainty. In many applications, these decisions involve classifying a time series based on outcomes that have yet to be observed. In this paper, we introduce Foreclassing, a new machine learning problem in which the class label of a time series is defined as a function of its future observations. We provide a mathematical formulation of Foreclassing and establish several theoretical results characterizing the problem. These results describe how additional future information affects Bayes classification risk, provide bounds on the classification error of learned Foreclassing models, and demonstrate how future observations used during training can yield finite-sample improvements over conventional classifiers. To provide a benchmark for future research, we propose ForeClassNet, a family of neural network models that jointly perform forecasting and classification while accounting for uncertainty in future outcomes. We investigate a variety of model architectures and evaluate their performance against conventional time series classifiers and forecasting-then-classify approaches. Experiments on datasets spanning weather, energy, and finance demonstrate the potential of Foreclassing to improve classification performance, probabilistic prediction, and downstream decision-making. Together, these theoretical and empirical contributions establish a foundation for future research on Foreclassing.

replace-cross An Embarrassingly Simple Data Selection Strategy for Million-Scale Multi-Modal Instruction-Tuning

Authors: Mengyao Lyu, Yan Li, Huasong Zhong, Hui Chen, Jungong Han, Guiguang Ding, Zhenheng Yang

Abstract: Recent research demonstrates that pretrained large language models (LLMs) require minimal but carefully curated data for instruction-tuning. However, existing methods rely on deeply extracted embeddings, gradients or narrow quality scores that are time-consuming to compute and limited in expressiveness, frequently failing to surpass a simple random-sampling baseline. To overcome these limitations, we step back from complex embedding spaces and target the fundamental objective of instruction tuning: how and what knowledge should be elicited from the pretrained model and exposed to users. We leverage interaction \underline{S}tyles as a proxy for structural diversity, and decompose data quality into capability-specific \underline{S}cores, yielding an explicit yet \underline{R}ich (SSR) profile for each sample. To our knowledge, SSR is the first data selection method validated at million-scale, achieving 99.1% of full-data performance using only 30% of a 2.6M pool. replace-cross When is Fine-Tuning Scalable? Certified Recovery from Noisy Data and Linear-Cost Fine-Tuning on Structured Task Shifts

Authors: Anastasis Kratsios, Tin Sum Cheng, Bum Jun Kim, Daniel Roy

Abstract: Transformers often transfer across related tasks with little fine-tuning; we study this question in noisy nonparametric regression for continuous maps $f,g:[0,1]^d\to\mathbb{R}^D$, representing the source and shifted tasks, using a single-query, single-head transformer $\mathcal{T}(\cdot)=V\operatorname{Softmax}(-\alpha(\cdot,K))$ with $\ell^{\infty}$-based alignment scores $\alpha$. Rather than assume that gradient-based training reaches an empirical risk minimizer, we give a model-specific two-stage procedure \textbf{certifying} source-task recovery and out-of-distribution (OOD) adaptation. With high probability, the resulting attention head recovers $f$ to arbitrary accuracy $\varepsilon>0$ in every fixed $L^p([0,1]^d)$ norm, $1\le p<\infty$, from noisy observations. For Lipschitz targets, its rate is minimax-optimal up to logarithmic factors without assuming oracle access to an asymptotic ERM. If $D=1$ and $f$ is Pfaffian than the values matrix factors into $AC$ with $A$ scaling at-most linearly $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-1})$ in the reciprocal approximation error $\varepsilon>0$. Importantly, our training procedure encodes all our identified (potentially) shared geometric structure from $f$ only into the values matrix factor $C$ and the keys matrix $K$ allowing for rapid access, and all $f$-specific structure only into $A$. Our main result concerns the OOD phase; where we identify geometric structures, which if shared by the source $f$ and OOD $g$ task; then only only the values matrix factor $A$ needs to be fine-tuned to guarantee an $L^p$-recovery of $g$. Critically, fine-tuning only $A$ is a convex problem which involving an \textit{at-most linear} number of parameters $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-1})$; dramatically cutting down on the worst-case scaling of $\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-\mathbf{d}})$ unavoidable in the source-training phase.

replace-cross LIME: Link-based User-item Interaction Modeling with Decoupled XOR Attention for Efficient Test Time Scaling

Authors: Yang Liu, Yunjiang Jiang, Ayush Agarwal, Yihan Wu, Haoran Liu, Bi Xue

Abstract: Scaling large recommendation systems requires advancing three major frontiers: processing longer user histories, expanding candidate sets, and increasing model capacity. While promising, transformers' computational cost scales quadratically with the user sequence length and linearly with the number of candidates. This trade-off makes it prohibitively expensive to expand candidate sets or increase sequence length at inference, despite the significant performance improvements. We introduce \textbf{LIME}, a novel architecture that resolves this trade-off. Through two key innovations, LIME fundamentally reduces computational complexity. First, low-rank ``link embeddings" enable pre-computation of attention weights by decoupling user and candidate interactions, making the inference cost nearly independent of candidate set size. Second, a linear attention mechanism, \textbf{LIME-XOR}, reduces the complexity with respect to user sequence length from quadratic ($O(N^2)$) to linear ($O(N)$). Experiments on public and industrial datasets show LIME achieves near-parity with state-of-the-art transformers but with a 10$\times$ inference speedup on large candidate sets or long sequence lengths. When tested on a major recommendation platform, LIME improved user engagement while maintaining minimal inference costs with respect to candidate set size and user history length, establishing a new paradigm for efficient and expressive recommendation systems.

replace-cross Uncertainty-Guided Multimodal Consensus for Multimodal Sentiment Analysis

Authors: Guowei Zhong, Junjie Li, Huaiyu Zhu, Ruohong Huan, Yun Pan

Abstract: Multimodal sentiment analysis (MSA) has made considerable progress in recent years. Existing MSA methods mainly learn shared multimodal representations or train unimodal predictors with a common multimodal label. However, assigning the shared label to every modality can obscure modality-specific sentiment, while direct fusion may overemphasize unreliable predictions when modalities disagree. To address these issues, we propose Uncertainty-Guided Multimodal Consensus (UMC), which first recovers continuous unimodal pseudo-labels and then forms a reliability-aware consensus across modalities. Our method consists of two key components: i) Continuous Prototype-Guided Label Recovery (CPLR), which combines heteroscedastic predictions with ordered sentiment prototypes and uses confidence and label conflict to refine continuous unimodal pseudo-labels, and ii) Reliability and Precision Consensus (RPC), which estimates sample-dependent modality reliability from predictive statistics and cross-modal disagreement and combines it with predictive precision through a closed-form consensus solver. Extensive experiments conducted on four publicly available MSA datasets demonstrate the effectiveness and superiority of our proposed model.

replace-cross Seeing is believing: The case for hardware-centric inefficiency detection and remediation

Authors: Ziji Chen, Steven W. D. Chien, Peng Qian, Noa Zilberman

Abstract: Large-scale machine learning workloads on the cloud suffer from silent inefficiencies, which do not trigger failures or obvious anomalies, making them hard to detect and diagnose. Existing diagnosis systems rely on guest-side instrumentation or dedicated healthy baselines. These assumptions do not hold in modern production cloud environments, where operators cannot observe inside the guest, and a healthy baseline for each setup is impractical because of system diversity. We present Unveil, a hardware-centric observability framework for detecting, identifying, and remediating silent inefficiencies using only host-visible telemetry. Unveil requires neither guest instrumentation nor application modification. It uses unsupervised learning models to detect inefficiencies and identifies known ones through a novel fingerprinting algorithm. An extensible knowledge base containing curated remediation scripts is used to correct problems without interrupting workloads when possible. We evaluate Unveil on distributed machine learning workloads, such as large language model serving, introducing less than 2% monitoring overhead. It successfully recovered from a 7% throughput loss and reduced inference latency by up to 43.48%. These results demonstrate that host-visible telemetry is sufficient for practical and continuous diagnosis of production machine learning systems.

replace-cross SELF: A Robust Singular Value and Eigenvalue Approach for LLM Fingerprinting

Authors: Hanxiu Zhang, Qiang Xu, Yue Zheng

Abstract: Safeguarding the Intellectual Property (IP) of Large Language Models (LLMs) against model theft represents a critical challenge in contemporary AI research. Although fingerprinting is essential for establishing forensic evidence for model derivation, existing methods are susceptible to false claim attacks, weight manipulations, or high false positive rates, undermining their utility. replace-cross Latent Nonlinear Denoising Score Matching for Enhanced Learning of Structured Distributions

Authors: Kaichen Shen, Wei Zhu

Abstract: Score-based generative models commonly employ linear forward diffusions with Gaussian reference distributions, limiting their ability to directly incorporate structural information into the noising dynamics. We introduce latent nonlinear denoising score matching (LNDSM), which combines nonlinear, structure-informed latent diffusions with the variational autoencoder (VAE) training framework. In our construction, the forward diffusion is designed to admit a prescribed reference distribution, such as a Gaussian mixture model, that reflects qualitative features of the latent data distribution. We derive a computable approximation to a cross-entropy upper bound in the VAE objective using Euler-Maruyama discretization and the resulting Gaussian conditional transitions. To stabilize training, we remove two zero-mean control variates whose variances become large for small time steps. The resulting objective enables joint training of the encoder, decoder, and latent score network. Numerical experiments on benchmark image datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of LNDSM in learning structured distributions, outperforming conventional latent score-based generative modeling in sample quality and mode balance across several settings, while achieving improved training efficiency. These results highlight the potential of nonlinear, structure-informed latent diffusions for generative modeling.

replace-cross Counting Clues: A Lightweight Probabilistic Baseline Can Match an LLM

Authors: Furong Jia, Yuan Pu, Finn Guo, Monica Agrawal

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) excel on multiple-choice clinical diagnosis benchmarks, yet it is unclear how much of this performance reflects underlying probabilistic reasoning. We study this through questions from MedQA that ask for the most likely diagnosis. We introduce Frequency-Based Probabilistic Ranker (FBPR), a lightweight method that scores options with a smoothed Naive Bayes over concept-diagnosis co-occurrence statistics from a large corpus. When co-occurrence statistics are sourced from Dolma, the pretraining corpus for OLMo, and RedPajama, an open reproduction of the LLaMA pretraining-data recipe, FBPR achieves comparable performance to the corresponding LLMs. Furthermore, direct LLM inference and FBPR exhibit complementary strengths, correctly answering largely different subsets of questions with overlap barely above chance. Combining them with a simple hybrid method yields a notable gain over either method alone. These findings show that explicit probabilistic baselines can account for a substantial portion of the benchmark performance on diagnosis questions, while serving as historically grounded reference points and complementary signals for hybridization.

replace-cross R2CC: Reliable and Resilient Collective Communication for Distributed Learning

Authors: Wei Wang, Nengneng Yu, Sixian Xiong, Zaoxing Liu

Abstract: Modern ML training and inference now span hundreds to tens of thousands of GPUs, where failures can waste 10-15% of GPU hours due to slow recovery. Common network errors and link fluctuations can cause up to 50% of job interruptions, triggering timeouts that often terminate entire jobs, forcing expensive checkpoint rollback during training and request reprocessing during inference. We present R2CC, a fault-tolerant communication library that provides lossless, low-overhead failover by exploiting multi-NIC hardware. R2CC performs rapid connection migration, bandwidth-aware load redistribution, and resilient collective algorithms to maintain progress under failures. Compared to existing fault-tolerant approaches, R2CC reduces failure-induced overhead by up to 92% for training and 98% for inference. R2CC is open-sourced at https://github.com/r2cc-project/R2CC.

replace-cross Semantic-Geometric Task Representations for Bimanual Manipulation from Human Demonstrations to Robot Action Planning

Authors: Franziska Herbert, Vignesh Prasad, Han Liu, Dorothea Koert, Georgia Chalvatzaki

Abstract: Learning structured task representations from human demonstrations is essential for bimanual manipulation, where action ordering, object involvement, and interaction geometry vary significantly across executions. A key challenge lies in jointly capturing the discrete semantic task structure and the temporal evolution of object-centric geometric relations in a form that supports reasoning over task progression. We introduce a semantic-geometric graph-based task representation that jointly encodes object identities, inter-object semantic relations, and per-object motion histories, via a Message Passing Neural Network (MPNN) encoder and a Transformer-based decoder. The encoder operates solely on the temporal scene graph, producing structured representations decoupled from action labels. The decoder then conditions on action-context to forecast future actions, associated objects, and object motions. This decoupling learns task-agnostic representations, enabling encoder reuse across embodiments through decoder-only finetuning on a small robot dataset. Across eleven bimanual tasks from two datasets, we find that the benefit of structured semantic--geometric representations over simpler sequence-based models grows with task variability in action ordering and object involvement. At deployment, a planner couples the action and motion predictions with learned Probabilistic Movement Primitives, achieving full task success on two real-robot bimanual tasks and outperforming graph ablations, Transformer, decoder-only, and finetuned vision-language model baselines.

replace-cross OneFlowSBI: One Model, Many Queries for Simulation-Based Inference

Authors: Mayank Nautiyal, Li Ju, Melker Ernfors, Klara Hagland, Ville Holma, Maximilian Werk\"o S\"oderholm, Andreas Hellander, Prashant Singh

Abstract: Simulation-based inference (SBI) methods typically amortize a single query, usually the posterior, so each further conditional or marginal needs its own model. Existing generalists amortize many queries in one transformer with one token per variable, so their attention cost grows quadratically with the number of variables. We introduce OneFlowSBI, a masked flow matching framework that trains one network on the joint distribution of parameters and observations. A ternary mask marks each coordinate as observed, generated or absent, so any conditional or marginal is sampled by changing the mask. The mask is an input, rather than a tokenization, so the backbone can follow the data's structure and scale to many variables. On high-dimensional image denoising and shallow water inference, OneFlowSBI achieves lower posterior error than the baselines, samples far faster than a transformer-based generalist, and handles missing regions and partial observation windows without retraining. On most of the ten SBIBM tasks at the largest simulation budget, it matches or exceeds neural posterior estimation and is comparable to transformer-based generalists, while also providing the likelihood and marginals.

replace-cross Learning Sequential Decisions from Multiple Sources via Group-Robust Markov Decision Processes

Authors: Mingyuan Xu, Zongqi Xia, Tianxi Cai, Doudou Zhou, Nian Si

Abstract: Learning a common policy from multiple source environments requires accounting for cross-source heterogeneity, and the offline setting introduces an additional challenge of limited data coverage. We formulate this problem as a distributionally robust Markov decision process under a shared linear MDP representation with source-specific transition and reward components. The uncertainty set is constructed through feature-wise mixtures of these components, yielding a $d$-rectangular structure that captures cross-source heterogeneity and admits a robust Bellman recursion. Building on this formulation, we develop Group-Robust Pessimistic Value Iteration (GR-PEVI), an offline policy-learning algorithm that combines feature-wise worst-case aggregation with a data-dependent pessimism penalty. Under a robust partial coverage condition, we establish finite-sample suboptimality guarantees for GR-PEVI and extend the analysis to clustered sources, characterizing the trade-off between the statistical gain from pooling similar sources and the approximation error induced by within-cluster heterogeneity. Simulation studies demonstrate the effectiveness of GR-PEVI, with empirical behavior consistent with the theoretical analysis. We apply GR-PEVI to a multi-source electronic health record study of multiple sclerosis treatment decisions to illustrate its use in a clinical setting.

replace-cross STRAND: Sequence-Conditioned Transport for Single-Cell Perturbations

Authors: Boyang Fu, Sameer Gabbita, George Dasoulas, Xiang Lin, Shanghua Gao, Xiaorui Su, Soumya Ghosh, Marinka Zitnik

Abstract: Prediction of the transcriptional response of a cell to a genetic perturbation is a central problem in the computational modeling of gene regulation. Perturbations of the same gene can produce different transcriptional responses depending on the targeted locus, such as an alternative transcription start site or a distal regulatory element, and gene-level perturbation models represent these distinct interventions with a single gene identifier. We introduce STRAND, a generative model that encodes the regulatory DNA sequence at the perturbation locus and uses this encoding to parameterize a conditional transport process from control to perturbed single-cell states. Sequence conditioning enables zero-shot prediction for perturbation targets absent from training and supports locus-resolved inputs at 128 bp model resolution within a 524 kb sequence window. On CRISPRi screens in K562, Jurkat, and RPE1 cells, STRAND achieves the best average rank on few-shot and unseen-gene benchmarks, with gains of up to 33% in perturbation discrimination, and improves transfer to held-out cell lines by up to 0.12 in $\Delta$Pearson. Ablation studies attribute these gains to sequence conditioning and to conditional transport. At the locus level, STRAND distinguishes alternative transcription start sites and validated regulatory elements that gene-identifier models represent as a single perturbation. STRAND is available at https://github.com/mims-harvard/STRAND.

URLs: https://github.com/mims-harvard/STRAND.

replace-cross Learning to Control: The iUzawa-Net for Nonsmooth Optimal Control of Linear PDEs

Authors: Yongcun Song, Xiaoming Yuan, Hangrui Yue, Tianyou Zeng

Abstract: We propose an optimization-informed deep neural network approach, named iUzawa-Net, aiming for the first solver that enables real-time solutions for a class of nonsmooth optimal control problems of linear partial differential equations (PDEs). The iUzawa-Net unrolls an inexact Uzawa method for saddle point problems, replacing classical preconditioners and PDE solvers with specifically designed learnable neural networks. We prove universal approximation properties and establish the asymptotic $\varepsilon$-optimality for the iUzawa-Net, and validate its promising numerical efficiency through nonsmooth elliptic and parabolic optimal control problems. Our techniques offer a versatile framework for designing and analyzing various optimization-informed deep learning approaches to optimal control and other PDE-constrained optimization problems. The proposed learning-to-control approach synergizes model-based optimization algorithms and data-driven deep learning techniques, inheriting the merits of both methodologies.

replace-cross Ensemble-size-dependence of deep-learning post-processing methods that minimize an (un)fair score: motivating examples and a proof-of-concept solution

Authors: Christopher David Roberts

Abstract: Fair scores reward ensemble forecast members that behave like samples from the same distribution as the verifying observations. They are therefore an attractive choice as loss functions to train data-driven ensemble forecasts or post-processing methods when large training ensembles are either unavailable or computationally prohibitive. The adjusted continuous ranked probability score (aCRPS) is a fair and ensemble-size-unbiased scoring rule when applied to ensembles with forecast members that are interpretable as conditionally independent draws from an underlying predictive distribution. However, distribution-aware post-processing methods that introduce structural dependency between members can violate this assumption, such that aCRPS no longer functions as a fair scoring rule. We demonstrate this effect using two approaches designed to minimize the expected aCRPS of a finite ensemble: (1) a linear member-by-member calibration, which couples members through a common dependency on the sample ensemble mean, and (2) a deep-learning method, which couples members via transformer self-attention across the ensemble dimension. In both cases, the results are sensitive to ensemble size and apparent gains in aCRPS can correspond to systematic unreliability characterized by over-dispersion. We introduce trajectory transformers as a proof-of-concept that ensemble-size independence can be achieved. This approach is an adaptation of the Post-processing Ensembles with Transformers (PoET) framework and applies self-attention over lead time while preserving the conditional independence required by aCRPS. When applied to weekly mean $T_{2m}$ forecasts from the ECMWF subseasonal forecasting system, this approach successfully reduces systematic model biases whilst also improving or maintaining forecast reliability regardless of the ensemble size used in training or real-time forecasts.

replace-cross HiAP: A Multi-Granular Stochastic Auto-Pruning Framework for Vision Transformers

Authors: Andy Li, Aiden Durrant, Milan Markovic, Georgios Leontidis

Abstract: Vision Transformers require significant computational resources and memory bandwidth, severely limiting their deployment on resource-constrained hardware. Most structured pruning methods reduce theoretical cost effectively, yet they typically operate at a single structural granularity and depend on multi-stage pipelines with importance ranking, auxiliary solvers or post-hoc magnitude thresholding, followed by a separate fine-tuning phase to recover accuracy. We propose Hierarchical Auto-Pruning (HiAP), which casts ViT pruning as a single budget-aware learning problem and jointly allocates sparsity across four granularities in one end-to-end phase. HiAP introduces stochastic Gumbel-Sigmoid gates at macro level (attention heads and FFN blocks) and micro level (intra-head dimensions and FFN neurons), and trains them against the task loss together with an analytical MAC cost term. Two penalty weights set how much compute is removed, while the gates gradually harden into a dense, smaller sub-network at convergence. It does not require importance heuristics, ranking metrics, auxiliary solvers or secondary fine-tuning. On ImageNet, HiAP compresses DeiT-Base to 7.4G MACs at 80.88% top-1 80.40% at 7.8G without distillation and DeiT-Small to 3.1G at 79.33%, competitive with substantially more complex pipelines at matched compute. The structurally pruned network can be accelerated natively on stock kernels, and more than 90% of the theoretical MAC reduction is realized as measured throughput at batch 256 on an A100.

Authors: Hannah P. Menke, Ahmed H. Elsheikh, Lingli Wei, Nanzhe Wang, Andreas Busch

Abstract: Geological CO2 storage, geothermal energy and underground hydrogen storage depend on irreversible or high-cost decisions made with sparse data and incomplete physical understanding. replace-cross Ctrl-A: Control-Driven Machine Learning using Adaptive Online Data Augmentation

Authors: Jesper B. Christensen, Ciaran Bench, Spencer A. Thomas, H\"usn\"u Aslan, David Balslev-Harder, Nadia A. S. Smith, Alessandra Manzin

Abstract: We introduce ControlAugment (Ctrl-A), an automated data augmentation algorithm for image-vision tasks, which incorporates principles from control theory for online adjustment of augmentation strength distributions during model training. Ctrl-A eliminates the need for initialization of individual augmentation strengths. Instead, augmentation strength distributions are dynamically, and individually, adapted during training based on a control-loop architecture and what we define as relative operation response curves. Using an operation-dependent update procedure provides Ctrl-A with the potential to reduce the use of augmentation operations that negatively impact model performance, alleviating the need to manually engineer augmentation policies for new image classification tasks. Experiments on the CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, SVHN-core, and Tiny ImageNet benchmark datasets using WideResNet-28-10 and ResNet architectures demonstrate that Ctrl-A provides competitive performance across a range of image classification benchmarks.

Authors: Shahar Cohen, David M. Steinberg, Yael Radzyner, Yochai Ben Horin

Abstract: We study a classification problem with three key challenges: pervasive informative missingness, the integration of partial prior expert knowledge into the learning process, and the need for interpretable decision rules. Abstract: Interpretable machine learning techniques are becoming essential tools for extracting physical insights from complex quantum data.
