replace-cross Compliance Moral Hazard and the Backfiring Mandate

Authors: Jian Ni, Lecheng Zheng, John R Birge

Abstract: Competing firms that share a population of risky customers face a decentralized risk detection problem in which each firm holds fragmentary information whose aggregation would generate social value, but private incentives impede truthful sharing. We develop a dynamic mechanism design framework for this setting and identify three strategic frictions that distinguish it from classical mechanism design with decentralized information: compliance moral hazard, adversarial adaptation, and information destruction through intervention. A temporal value assignment (TVA) mechanism credits firms using a strictly proper scoring rule applied to discounted verified outcomes; under stated assumptions, TVA implements truthful posterior reporting as a Bayes--Nash equilibrium (uniquely optimal at each edge in large federations, with $O(1/m)$ shading in finite systems). A network Shapley characterization shows that under edge-additive coalition value, each firm's marginal contribution is proportional to its weighted cross-firm interaction degree, yielding a sharp prescription for coalition design that prioritizes inter-firm volume over firm size. Embedding TVA in a model of competition among firms, we establish a welfare ordering across four regulatory regimes (autarky, voluntary federation, mandated full sharing, TVA) and identify conditions under which information-sharing mandates without compatible incentive design reduce welfare below autarky: a ``backfiring mandate.'' We illustrate the framework on a 1.4M-transaction synthetic anti-money-laundering benchmark; the same machinery extends to platform fraud, cybersecurity threat intelligence, and supply chain risk detection.

Abstract: Many news reports have claimed that machine-generated text (MGT) from large language models (LLMs) is taking over the web. replace-cross PGID: Progressive Guided Inversion and Denoising for Robust Watermark Detection

Authors: Minh Quoc Duong, Chun Tong Lei, Chun Pong Lau

Abstract: With the proliferation of AI-generated images, digital watermarking has become an essential safeguard for protecting intellectual property and mitigating malicious exploitation. Recent works on semantic watermarking have enabled efficient copyright protection for diffusion models. However, the dependence of semantic watermarking on diffusion inversion for watermark detection creates a critical vulnerability. Imprint removal and forgery attacks exploit this weakness to produce deceptive results. Our analysis reveals that these attacks succeed by displacing watermarked latents into the unwatermarked region, while guiding unwatermarked latents into the watermarked region. Based on that, we propose Progressive Guided Inversion and Denoising (PGID), the first plug-and-play, training-free noise extraction framework designed to defend against both attack strategies. PGID effectively defends by projecting perturbed latents back to the region where they originally belong. The projection is achieved by eliminating intermediate latent deflections and mitigating adversarial perturbations through progressive inversion-denoising cycles. Comprehensive evaluations across multiple schemes demonstrate that PGID successfully restores detection reliability by recovering removed watermarks and identifying forged instances.

replace-cross Phases of Muon: When Muon Eclipses SignSGD

Authors: Elliot Paquette, Noah Marshall, Lucas Benigni, Guangyuan Wang, Atish Agarwala, Courtney Paquette

Abstract: Muon and related spectral optimizers have recently demonstrated strong empirical performance as scalable stochastic methods, often outperforming Adam. Yet their behavior remains poorly understood. We analyze stochastic Muon, which orthogonalizes an exponential moving average of minibatch gradients, on a high-dimensional matrix-valued least squares problem, and we compare it with stochastic SignSGD, a simple baseline comparable to Adam. We derive explicit deterministic dynamics that provide a tractable framework for studying learning behavior. We show that Muon has non-linear sensitivity to the effective batch $B_{\rm eff}=B(1+\beta)/(1-\beta)$. With exact whitening and small $B_{\rm eff}$ Muon fails to resolve smaller eigenmodes, slowing down convergence. Momentum and finite Newton--Shulz iterations can act as a ridge which lessen this effect. This behavior is in contrast with SignSGD which has no transition, leading to different optimal learning rates, but with matching convergence rate up to a constant factor in the isotropic setting. For anisotropic data, large batch setting Muon performs a square-root preconditioning with respect to the data covariance spectrum while SignSGD provides no preconditioning for generic covariances. We analyze time to target loss in a power law covariance model and derive a phase diagram in the data exponent $\alpha$ and target exponent $\zeta$. Muon is favored for steep covariance spectra with rich targets ($\alpha>2$, $\zeta<\alpha+1$), SignSGD is favored for flat spectra ($\alpha<1$), and in other regions the winner depends on how the target loss scales with dimension. Our results shed light on why replacing Adam with Muon is not trivial even from a pure optimization perspective.

replace-cross PIVOT: Bridging Planning and Execution in LLM Agents via Trajectory Refinement

Authors: Tuo Zhang, Alin-Ionut Popa, Yan Xu, Rui Song, Dimitrios Dimitriadis

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based agents frequently generate seemingly coherent plans that fail upon execution due to infeasible actions, constraint violations, and compounding errors over extended horizons. PIVOT (Plan-Inspect-eVOlve Trajectories) addresses this plan-execution misalignment through a self-supervised framework that treats trajectories as optimizable objects iteratively refined via environment interaction. The framework comprises four stages: PLAN generates candidate trajectories; INSPECT executes them and computes structured losses with textual gradients encoding plan-execution discrepancies; EVOLVE applies these signals to produce improved trajectories; and VERIFY performs a final global check against task constraints. A monotonic acceptance process ensures a non-decreasing solution quality. Empirical evaluations on DeepPlanning and GAIA demonstrate state-of-the-art performance: with human-in-the-loop (HITL) feedback, PIVOT establishes a strong upper bound up to 94% relative improvement in constraint satisfaction, while its fully autonomous variant retains substantial gains, showing that the core trajectory-refinement mechanism remains effective without external supervision. At the same time, PIVOT remains computationally efficient, requiring up to 3x to 5x fewer tokens than competing refinement methods. These findings establish that (self- or human-supervised) feedback-based trajectory optimization is a principled methodology for mitigating plan-execution gaps in autonomous agent systems.

replace-cross Multi-Variable Conformal Prediction: Optimizing Prediction Sets using Multiple Calibration Variables

Authors: Laura L\"utzow, Simone Garatti, Marco C. Campi, Lars Lindemann, Matthias Althoff

Abstract: Split conformal prediction constructs prediction sets with finite-sample coverage guarantees, but its calibration stage is limited to adjusting a single threshold parameter. Hence, the shape of the prediction sets must be fixed beforehand, typically by fitting it on a reserved part of the calibration data. We introduce multi-variable conformal prediction (MCP), a framework that jointly optimizes and calibrates multiple prediction-set parameters in a single optimization problem, eliminating calibration data split without sacrificing finite-sample coverage guarantees. Building on scenario theory, a principled framework for certifying data-driven decisions, we propose two tractable variants: RemMCP, grounded in constrained optimization with constraint removal, which generalizes split conformal prediction; and RelMCP, based on penalized optimization with constraint relaxation, which also supports non-convex problems, albeit at a higher computational cost. In experiments with ellipsoidal and multi-modal prediction sets, RemMCP and a heuristic, adaptive version of RelMCP consistently meet the target coverage, with lower variance across calibration runs than baselines with calibration data split, and in most settings with smaller or comparable prediction sets.

replace-cross Parametric Rates for Nonparametric Conditional-Moment Bilevel Optimization

Authors: Fares El Khoury, Houssam Zenati, Nathan Kallus, Michael Arbel, Aur\'elien Bibaut

Abstract: We study functional bilevel optimization with conditional moments, where plug-in hypergradients can inherit first-order errors from nonparametrically learned conditional expectations. We introduce OBiGrad, an orthogonalized hypergradient estimator whose bias is a product of nuisance errors. Under our regression and complexity conditions, projected gradient descent with the direct plug-in hypergradient yields expected stationarity error $O(T^{-1/2}+n^{-1/2}+n^{-\nu/b})$ for the best iterate, whereas OBiGrad achieves $O(T^{-1/2}+n^{-1/2}+n^{-2\nu/b})$, where $b=\nu(2+p)+\gamma$ combines the approximation and complexity exponents. Consequently, when $\nu(2-p)\ge\gamma$, OBiGrad attains the parametric statistical rate $O(T^{-1/2}+n^{-1/2})$, improving the sufficient sample-size bound relative to the direct plug-in bound for a fixed iteration budget. We illustrate these gains on instrumental-variable and fitted-Q-regression problems, examining stationary-point estimation, returned population stationarity, and regularization sensitivity.

To address this, we introduce PlanAhead, a static planner-executor framework that evaluates the impact of plan representation on agent performance. We first automatically categorize WebArena tasks into 3 difficulty levels, enabling consistent difficulty grading without human annotation. Then we systematically evaluate 4 different plan representations on the tasks categorized as hard: sequential subgoals, narrative, pseudocode, and checklist; across different families of multimodal LLM-powered agents(OpenAI, Alibaba, and Google). To account for stochastic variability, we introduce a novel decomposition of agent Success Rate (SR) into 2 interpretable evaluation metrics: Achievement Rate (AR) and Solved-Task Consistency (STC). Our results show that both, the plan formulation and the underlying LLM generating the plan, significantly influence web-agent robustness and task success.

replace-cross An Asymptotic Theory of Chain-of-Thought in In-Context Learning

Authors: Kaito Takanami, Cengiz Pehlevan

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning has become a widely used mechanism for eliciting multi-step reasoning in large language models by generating intermediate reasoning steps at inference time. Yet the scaling behavior of generalization with CoT depth remains poorly understood. To address this question, we study a theoretically solvable model of CoT for in-context weight prediction in linear regression, where test-time reasoning is represented as an iterative refinement of the weight-parameter estimate. Using tools from random matrix theory under high-dimensional asymptotics, we derive an exact formula for the generalization error as a function of reasoning depth, pretraining data amount, and context length. Our analysis reveals a sharp phase transition separating exponential and polynomial improvement, saturation, and overthinking, and characterizes how the optimal reasoning depth scales. We further show that deeper reasoning is most effective with sufficiently rich pretraining and in-context information, whereas limited pretraining or context makes longer reasoning prone to error amplification or saturation. We also validate these predictions through experiments on fully learned linear attention and softmax attention models. Our results provide a unified theoretical account of how test-time CoT depth affects generalization.

replace-cross DRIFT: A Residual Flow Adapter for Decoding Continuous Outputs in Vision-Language Models

Authors: Zhuoming Liu, Jinhong Lin, Kwan Man Cheng, Lin Zhang, Shayok Bagchi, Yin Li

Abstract: Many modern vision-language models (VLMs) build on autoregressive decoding of discrete tokens. While text-based output interfaces enable scalable pretraining and strong zero-shot generalization across diverse tasks, they are poorly suited for problems that require precise continuous outputs, such as localizing temporal boundaries of events or generating robotic control actions. To address this challenge, we propose DRIFT, a general framework for adapting pretrained VLMs to continuous decoding tasks. DRIFT combines a base predictor, which provides a coarse estimate of the target output, with a generative refinement module based on flow matching that iteratively improves the prediction. This residual formulation transforms the generative modeling problem from learning a global output distribution to modeling a localized residual distribution around a strong prior, substantially simplifying optimization. We evaluate DRIFT on both perception and planning tasks, including visual grounding and robotic control. Across multiple tasks and architectures spanning MLLMs, VLAs, and WAMs, DRIFT consistently outperforms a strong set of regression- and generative-based solutions.

Authors: Qingyun Liu, Bo Yan, Yang Liu, Yuji Roh, Ekansh Sharma, Likang Yin, Emma Olowo, Yuxuan Li, Yikun Jiang, Diego Uribe, Saksham Aggarwal, Siqi Wu, Yuan Hao, Vikas Kedigehalli, Min-hsuan Tsai, Lukasz Heldt, Lichan Hong, Li Wei, Xinyang Yi

Abstract: User modeling in industrial recommender systems typically produces dense embeddings, which suffer from representational constraints due to fixed-dimensional vectors. Meanwhile, Semantic ID (SID) is effective for item representation, yet existing discrete user representations remain largely confined to quantizing already-pooled user vectors, clustering historical watches, or predicting coarse topic clusters solely for candidate retrieval. We propose TokenMinds, an industrial system that extends the PLUM framework from SID-based item retrieval to generative sequential user modeling. Built on an encoder-decoder architecture adapted from pretrained LLMs, TokenMinds introduces a dual-output design: the encoder outputs a dense user embedding capturing a holistic user state, while the decoder generates discrete SID-based user tokens representing multi-faceted future interests. Grounded in item semantics, these tokens preserve distinct interests that a single pooled vector would average away. Additionally, a shared SID vocabulary across long- and short-form videos unifies cross-scenario behaviors into a single model, cutting upstream training and serving compute by 50% and 31%. Furthermore, leveraging the text vocabulary inherited from the pretrained LLM, TokenMinds interleaves textual search queries directly with watch histories to capture explicit user intent. We validate TokenMinds through offline evaluations and live A/B experiments across YouTube's primary ranking surfaces, serving billions of users asynchronously: SID-based user tokens are effective on their own, yield additive gains alongside dense embeddings, and together outperform the previous-generation, non-LLM user model in production.

replace-cross Distributionally Faithful Imputation via Positive Semi-Definite Kernel Density Estimation

Authors: Andrea Basteri, Carlo Ciliberto, Alessandro Rudi

Abstract: Missing values undermine statistical inference and machine learning pipelines, yet most imputation methods rely on heuristics or restrictive parametric assumptions that ignore the joint data distribution. We recast imputation under missing completely at random (MCAR) as density estimation from masked observations: estimate a distribution whose observed marginals exactly match those in the data. Leveraging positive semidefinite (PSD) kernel densities we obtain a convex empirical risk problem with closed form marginals, solvable by a Newton interior point method. We further introduce a non convex extension that supports adaptive hyperparameter selection through alternating minimization. The resulting PSD-Impute model yields both single and multiple imputations from the same fitted density, enjoys statistical consistency, beating the curse of dimensionality for very regular probabilities. Experiments indicate competitive distributional accuracy compared with popular imputation baselines, suggesting strong practical promise.

ScalableRAG combines minimal ingestion, conventional-QA parity, exact corpus operations, and million-document viability; no evaluated alternative provides all four. Our code is available at https://github.com/cohesity/ScalableRAG .

replace-cross TextCloak: Safeguarding Data from Unauthorized LLM Fine-Tuning via RL-Driven Unlearnable Examples

Authors: Chengshuai Zhao, Pingchuan Ma, Dawei Li, Bohan Jiang, Zhiyuan Yu, Zhen Tan, Huan Liu

Abstract: The development of Large Language Models (LLMs) has led to significant advances across a wide range of language tasks, while simultaneously raising growing concerns about unauthorized online data exploitation and privacy leakage. Unlearnable examples (UEs) emerge as a promising defense by injecting crafted perturbations into data such that models trained on them exhibit degraded utility. However, existing methods for text protection are primarily designed for classification tasks and discriminative language models, which limits their broad applicability (e.g., instruction following or reasoning) in LLMs. In this work, we propose **TextCloak**, an RL-driven data synthesis framework that safeguards textual data against unauthorized LLM fine-tuning. TextCloak employs a *UE generative policy* that transforms clean text into unlearnable examples while preserving semantic fidelity and linguistic naturalness. To optimize the policy, we introduce **GRPO-UE**, which simulates the attacker's LLM fine-tuning and rewards downstream degradation via group-relative policy optimization. This overall formulates a bi-level optimization loop, allowing the generator to discover effective protective patterns. Comprehensive experiments on six publicly available datasets and nine state-of-the-art LLMs demonstrate that TextCloak consistently impairs unauthorized LLM fine-tuning while maintaining text utility for legitimate use. Further analyses confirm its transferability and robustness across model architectures, training configurations, and adaptive attacks, highlighting its broad applicability as a practical defense for online content.

replace-cross Hard Constraints, Smooth Gradients: Learning Feasible Inventory Policies via Differentiable Projection

Authors: Patrick Helm, Jan-Niklas Doerr, Joren Gijsbrechts, Stefan Minner

Abstract: Many operational problems are constrained sequential decision processes with combinatorial action spaces and interdependent constraints. Mixed-integer linear programs (MILPs) handle such constraints flexibly but scale poorly in stochastic environments. Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) promises scalable decision rules, but existing methods either penalize constraints rather than enforce them, or rely on feasibility mechanisms that break down once constraints interact. We bridge this gap by embedding a differentiable optimization module inside the policy: a neural network proposes continuous action targets, a quadratic program projects them onto the relaxed feasible set, and a dual-informed integer mapping restores integrality while preserving feasibility. Given a differentiable simulator, the policy trains end-to-end from sampled trajectories using pathwise gradients, while handling hard constraints with similar flexibility to MILPs. We show that our approach has bounded error relative to an exact integer projection and ensures the entire feasible action space is reachable. We apply the method to multi-echelon production-inventory planning under shared resource and material constraints. Our policy attains an average optimality gap of 1% on 3-item instances and consistently outperforms a rolling-horizon multi-stage stochastic program by at least 5.6% in 10-item networks. Compared to state-of-the-art echelon base-stock policies, savings reach 15.3% on 40-item instances. On an industry-scale case study with nonstationary demand from ASML, we reduce average cost by up to 1.8% relative to the best-known benchmark policy. The savings are largest where planning is hardest: in tightly capacitated systems with high demand variability. More broadly, we show that DRL can improve upon state-of-the-art baselines in sequential decision problems with hard constraints, which are widespread in practice.

replace-cross Computational and Statistical Guarantees of the c-Rectified flow

Authors: Leda Wang, Zhehao Xu, Qiang Liu, Harrison H. Zhou

Abstract: Recently, rectified flow has emerged as a fundamental framework for large-scale image generation, powering state-of-the-art systems such as FLUX.1 and Stable Diffusion 3. Despite its remarkable empirical success, the computational and statistical guarantees of iterative rectified flow have remained largely unexplored. We address this problem by studying c-rectified flow, a cost-aware class of rectified flow that projects velocity fields onto a gradient class while preserving endpoint marginals. The ordinary rectified flow can fail to recover the optimal transport coupling: in a Gaussian case study, the iteration converges to the optimal coupling if and only if the source and target covariance matrices commute. More generally, for a Gaussian to log-concave coupling, the ordinary rectified flow can reach a fixed point after one step from independent initialization. In contrast, under suitable regularity and uniform-integrability assumptions, iterative c-rectified flow always converges to the optimal transport coupling. We further establish quantitative one-step contraction and exponential convergence guarantees under projection-stability assumptions for both quadratic and strongly convex displacement costs. Finally, under a H\"older ball assumption, we develop new minimax-optimal score estimation rates and show that, when combined with iterative c-rectified flow, they yield a rate-optimal estimator of the optimal transport for the dimension \(d \ge 3\) and a nearly parametric rate for \(d=1,2\).

replace-cross Momentum in large-batch training: Polyak enlarges the critical batch size, Nesterov improves data efficiency

Authors: Jia-Nan Wang, Zixun Huang, Kairui Li, Zilin Wang, Lei Wu

Abstract: We study when and how momentum improves large-batch training in the one-pass regime, using power-law kernel regression as a tractable setting. We first characterize stochastic stability through the \emph{critical learning rate}, defined as the largest learning rate for stable training, and obtain $\eta_{\mathrm{SGD}}^{\mathrm{crit}}\eqsim 1$, $\eta_{\mathrm{Polyak}}^{\mathrm{crit}}\eqsim \min\{1,B(1-\beta)\}$, and $\eta_{\mathrm{Nesterov}}^{\mathrm{crit}}\eqsim \min\{1,B^q(1-\beta)\}$, where $B$ is the batch size, $\beta\in[0,1)$ is the momentum factor, and \(q>1\) is the capacity exponent governing spectral decay. Within the stable region, we derive scaling laws for the full risk dynamics, capturing the progression from an early transient through power-law decay to a noise floor. We then optimize the final-step risk under a fixed data budget, obtaining a three-regime phase diagram that reveals how the benefits of momentum change with batch size. Notably, Polyak enlarges the critical batch size, the largest batch size preserving the optimal small-batch data-scaling exponent, thereby allowing fewer sequential updates without sacrificing data efficiency. In contrast, Nesterov improves the optimal data-scaling exponent in the large-batch regime through look-ahead-induced suppression of noise amplification. Experiments on PLK regression quantitatively support the theoretical scaling predictions, while neural-network experiments provide qualitative evidence for the predicted batch-size dependence and stability behavior.

replace-cross A Tight 3-Approximation for Fair $k$-Means

Authors: Kangke Cheng, Guanlin Mo, Shihong Song, Hu Ding

Abstract: Fairness in clustering has attracted sustained research interest, motivated by the need to ensure equitable representation of protected groups in machine learning applications. We study fair $k$-means clustering in Euclidean space, where the proportion of each protected group in every cluster must lie within specified lower and upper bounds. These constraints make it challenging to determine both cluster centers and point assignments. Our algorithm first splits the input into many small fair clusters and then merges them into $k$ clusters with a weighted $k$-means algorithm. The merge is cheap only when the small clusters do not move points away from their optimal centers, so we add a displacement penalty to the split, which charges each point for how far the small clusters move it. Given a $\rho$-approximate algorithm for weighted $k$-means and any $\epsilon>0$, our algorithm returns a fractional solution whose cost is at most $3\rho+\epsilon$ times the optimal integral fair cost. With a PTAS as the subroutine, the approximation ratio becomes $3+O(\epsilon)$, improving the previous factor of $5+O(\epsilon)$. This ratio is the best possible: unless P=NP, no polynomial-time algorithm achieves the ratio $3-\delta$ for any fixed $\delta>0$, even in one dimension. The solution satisfies all fairness constraints exactly and can be rounded to an integral assignment with a bounded additive violation of fairness and no increase in cost. The same approximation guarantee extends to the $k$-sparse Wasserstein barycenter problem.

replace-cross Self-Supervised Learning for Robust Resonance Mass Regression in Cascade Decays

Authors: Ho Fung Tsoi, Alex Yang, Luis Felipe Gutierrez Zagazeta, Shion Chen, Dylan Rankin

Abstract: Reconstructing the mass of a heavy resonance from its decay products with missing energy is one of the central tasks that directly determine the sensitivity in new physics searches at collider experiments. Supervised learning approaches to this problem often struggle to generalize well due to the presence of various systematic uncertainties and distribution shifts. Exhausting all possible variations in the labeled data can be very compute-intensive, while a failure of the model to generalize can corrupt the reconstructed resonance widths that are critical in peak-hunting analyses. In this work, following the foundation model paradigm, we use a self-supervised approach to pre-train a transformer encoder with VICReg to learn an embedding invariant to various corruptions, then fine-tune it for mass regression on a heavy resonance with masses ranging from 2.5 to 6.5 TeV and a SUSY-like cascade decay into an eleven-body final state. We show that the pre-trained model reconstructs sharper resonance peaks and has a more stable performance under various realistic corruptions, compared to a supervised model of the same architecture trained on the same augmented data from scratch.

replace-cross Similarity Pairing with Energy Mover's Distance for Self-Supervised Pre-Training at the LHC

Authors: Ho Fung Tsoi, Dylan Rankin

Abstract: Many self-supervised methods for training foundation models at the Large Hadron Collider (LHC) rely on data augmentations to encourage the model to embed events into a representation space invariant to certain physical or detector symmetries. A common challenge arises from the large freedom in choosing a proper set of augmentations on which downstream performance depends. The implementation of augmentations involves either modifying existing events, potentially breaking the event fidelity, or simulating more event variants, which is computationally intensive. In this work, we present a data-driven method of pairing events by their similarity via the energy mover's distance (EMD), which measures how similar two events are in terms of the work required to transform one into the other. With this approach, distinct events are sampled and matched by their similarity to serve as views for learning invariance, keeping the physics content of each event intact without handcrafted distortions. We demonstrate this augmentation-free pairing method by pre-training on QCD jets via self-distillation and show that it can yield semantic jet embeddings with downstream discrimination power comparable to or better than an augmentation-based baseline.

replace-cross When Does Test-Time Physical Diagnosis Pay?

Authors: Zhengshu Zhang

Abstract: When a robot faces unfamiliar physical conditions, a common approach is to collect evidence about what changed and adapt. For such diagnosis to improve behavior, six ordered empirical conditions must hold: a meaningful reference, identifiability of the physical condition, use of the acquired evidence, decision value, selection value over a fixed alternative, and safe realization. We test this chain in controlled and public environments. In our controlled environments the frozen policy chooses correctly on every decision, but which evidence it buys is fixed by how the panels assign decisions to evidence strata. On three mechanisms absent from training, the frozen decoder fails its registered transfer gate. On all 96 decisions of the transfer panel, its full-trace input lies far outside its training range, and on such inputs it returns one answer whatever the trace contains. All 15 of its errors fall in the 30 decisions from which cheaper evidence was removed by design, a split hidden by aggregate accuracy. None of three primary public tasks passes its registered qualification gates. Before attributing a failure to how a frozen model uses evidence, evaluation should therefore check whether its deployment inputs lie inside its training range.

replace-cross Nuclear Norm-Regularized Bayesian Matrix Completion

Authors: Calvin Tolbert

Abstract: Nuclear-norm-regularized least squares is a standard approach to matrix completion, but it yields only a point estimate. Its Bayesian counterpart, a prior combining the nuclear norm with a Frobenius-norm term, gives a strongly log-concave though nonsmooth posterior when the noise precision is known. In practice the precision is unknown, and placing a truncated Jeffreys prior on it makes the joint posterior neither log-concave nor smooth. To our knowledge, we give the first sampler for this model with an explicit non-asymptotic guarantee: its output is within a prescribed total-variation or Wasserstein distance of the posterior on the matrix after a number of steps polynomial in the matrix dimensions and the reciprocal of the target accuracy. The sampler never targets a continuous distribution that is not strongly log-concave. It discretizes the precision onto a grid, estimates its posterior by thermodynamic integration along the tilted conditional posteriors of the matrix, and bounds the error this propagates to the posterior on the matrix. The approach applies whenever non-log-concavity is confined to a bounded scalar hyperparameter and the conditional distributions are strongly log-concave with smooth-plus-Lipschitz potentials. Our result establishes feasibility; the polynomial complexity is not intended as a practical algorithm at current problem scales.

replace-cross Plan-to-Synthesis: Cross-City Human Mobility Generation via Semantic Latent Flow Matching

Authors: Zhoufu Wang, Baoshen Guo, Zhiqing Hong, Junyi Li, Kailai Sun, Heye Huang, Alok Prakash, Shenhao Wang, Jinhua Zhao

Abstract: Human mobility generation aims to synthesize realistic point-of-interest (POI) visitation trajectories and has become an important tool for travel behavior modeling, transportation management, and urban planning. The generated latents are subsequently decoded and grounded to concrete POIs. Extensive experiments on large-scale multi-city datasets show that SeMoFlow achieves higher trajectory fidelity than existing baselines, preserves city-specific mobility motifs, and supports both joint multi-city generation and effective cross-city transfer. replace-cross CraftSPH: A composable framework for differentiable smoothed particle hydrodynamics implemented in PyTorch

Authors: Gen Matono, Shujiro Fujioka, Mayuko Nishio

Abstract: Smoothed particle hydrodynamics (SPH) is well suited to large-deformation fluid problems, and the growing use of automatic differentiation in engineering applications has motivated interest in differentiable SPH solvers. In this study, a composable differentiable SPH framework, CraftSPH, has been developed. In CraftSPH, major computational operations are implemented as independent modules that users can combine to construct solvers for different purposes. The results also showed that the choice of operations included in the computational graph affects the computed gradients. Together, these findings demonstrate that CraftSPH enables users to assemble problem-specific solvers for forward and inverse analyses within a common framework.

replace-cross Advantage of Sample Complexity in Quantum PAC Learning Requires Inverse Access to State-Preparation Unitaries

Authors: Natsuto Isogai, Satoshi Yoshida, Mio Murao

Abstract: Whether quantum computation can reduce the amount of data required for conventional machine learning is a central problem in quantum machine learning. In probably approximately correct (PAC) learning, a learner receives training examples sampled from an unknown probability distribution. Quantum PAC learning extends this framework by allowing access to quantum examples, which are quantum states that coherently encode this distribution, or to unitaries that prepare quantum examples, with or without inverse access. When only copies of quantum examples are available, the optimal worst-case sample complexity asymptotically matches that of classical learning. In contrast, access to both a preparation unitary and its inverse can improve the accuracy dependence of query complexity. However, it has remained open whether access to the preparation unitary alone suffices for this improvement. In this work, we resolve this question by determining the optimal query complexity with forward-only access, taking the worst case over compatible preparation unitaries and their dimensions. In both realizable and agnostic PAC learning, this complexity matches, up to constant factors, the optimal sample complexity achievable with classical examples or copies of quantum examples. To prove these results, we construct, for any algorithm with forward-only access, a simulator that approximates the algorithm's Haar-averaged output state using as many copies of the prepared state as the algorithm makes queries. These results show that inverse access is essential for the known improvement in accuracy dependence in this worst-case setting. Beyond PAC learning, our reduction provides a general tool for transferring lower bounds for state copies to query lower bounds for forward-only access to the corresponding preparation unitaries.

replace-cross SD-DPC: Sparse Dictionary Differentiable Predictive Control

Authors: Ali Reza Daneshvar Garmroodi, Jan Drgo\v{n}a

Abstract: We present sparse dictionary differentiable predictive control (SD-DPC), a framework for learning sparse, interpretable feedback policies for nonlinear systems from data. A prediction model is first identified by rollout-based sparse identification of nonlinear dynamics (SINDy), building on gradient-based and multistep formulations. The policy is then parameterized as a sparse combination of dictionary functions and trained by differentiating a constrained finite-horizon predictive-control objective through this model, so that its terms are selected by closed-loop performance rather than by imitating a previously trained controller. The result is an explicit feedback law with only a handful of terms. Across three benchmark control problems, SD-DPC satisfies the constraints in almost all test scenarios, outperforms a policy distilled onto the same terms by up to an order of magnitude, and requires orders of magnitude less memory and online computation than an optimization benchmark, while admitting explicit sensitivity bounds.

replace-cross The Score Is Not the Structure: Brain Alignment and Cross-Lingual Transfer

Authors: Saman Rahbar

Abstract: Similarity scores are often offered as evidence that a model shares structure with the brain or across languages. We ask what such a score reads when that structure is removed, or when the instrument measuring it does not work, in two settings. Across seventeen languages, a grammaticality probe transfers worse between more distant languages (r = -0.66). But the probe's own accuracy falls along the same axis and is at chance in four languages, four of the five most distant. Dropping them halves the explained variance, and because it also narrows the range of distances, the design cannot say how much of the gradient is the instrument. Counting the 272 language pairs as independent gives p = 0.0006 for a steering effect that is null when the seventeen languages are the unit (p = 0.155). In brain alignment, a training objective raises a language model's similarity to fMRI responses from 0.10 to 0.34 (reliability ceiling 0.54), yet targets with the brain correspondence destroyed still reach 0.31. A shuffled target scores about k/n against the real one, for rank k and n sentences, a baseline that can be computed before any model is trained. Both interventions work, yet we detect no distance-graded steering effect and no syntactic benefit from alignment. Before reading a correspondence score, measure how much of it survives without the correspondence.

Abstract: Explanations of LLM outputs are considered an important way to make model behavior legible, helping human users make good decisions on downstream tasks. Abstract: We present Agaram 2.5B, a Tamil-first language model trained from scratch that converses in Tamil script, romanized Tanglish and English, and calls tools. Our first model, Agaram 1.5B, guided the new base, tokenizer and fine-tuning recipe: we pretrained a new 2.53B-parameter base on 50 billion tokens, 58% of them Tamil, with a tokenizer that needs 1.55 tokens per Tamil word where Qwen2.5's needs 9.44. A four-day public preview of our first 2.5B chat model showed where it fell short with real users, and we rebuilt supervised fine-tuning around what we saw, on 222,662 records. The released model scores above Sarvam-1 in all six translation directions we measured (FLORES chrF 56.9 into Tamil and 55.4 into English), chooses the expected tool on all 25 prompts of a tool set that was used during checkpoint selection and whose schemas appear in training, with exact arguments on 16, and was preferred over the preview model by an LLM judge on 100 sealed human-written prompts (net preference +30 points, 95% interval +14.5 to +45.0). On FLORES it also scores above Google's Gemma 4 E4B-it and our fine-tuned variant of it in both directions, with 2.53B parameters against E4B's 4.5B effective parameters. Factual knowledge and reliable refusal of harmful requests remain open problems, which we set out as future work. The base and chat weights and GGUF files are released under Apache-2.0, and this report gives a full account of the data, training and evaluation as a playbook for building language models for under-represented languages.

replace-cross When Does the Dependency Graph Help Code-to-Architecture Mapping?

Authors: Rakhshanda Jabeen, Morgan Ericsson, Jonas Nordqvist, Anna Wingkvist

Abstract: Architectural technical debt arises when a system's implementation drifts from its inten ded modular decomposition, while the mapping between source files and architectural modu les is missing, incomplete, or costly to maintain manually. We formulate code-to-archite cture mapping as semi-supervised node classification over a directed multi-relational de pendency graph, with files as nodes, typed dependencies as edges, and lexical features d erived from code identifiers and directory layout. Starting from a small labeled sample, an iterative self-training procedure expands the mapping to the remaining files. We com pare a graph convolutional network (GCN), a graph attention network (GAT), and a graph-f ree multi-layer perceptron (MLP), using identical features and training procedures for t he learned models, against a Naive Bayes reference. Across 100 runs on ten open-source J ava and C/C++ systems, all three neural models outperform the reference on both F1 metri cs. GAT achieves the highest averages of system-level median macro and micro F1, at 83.5 and 92.2, respectively, whereas MLP remains competitive or superior on several systems. On Chrome, the largest system with 18,343 files, GAT and GCN exceed MLP by 22 and 19 ma cro-F1 points, respectively, and have substantially fewer persistently misclassified fil es. Connectivity and homophily are associated with where neural message passing helps, w hile multi-relational aggregation outperforms a single merged edge type on every system for both GCN and GAT. A follow-up study finds that several model rankings depend on the training seed, qualifying smaller advantages without removing the large graph benefit ob served on Chrome.

replace-cross BiDex-100: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Bimanual Dexterous Manipulation Across 100+ Simulation Tasks

Authors: Yong Wang, Kecheng Zheng, Xing Zhu, Chao Xu, Yujun Shen, Shengze Cai

Abstract: Bimanual manipulation with dexterous hands has attracted growing attention in research on robotic action models. However, few studies have developed benchmarks specifically tailored for dexterous manipulation, leaving the dexterous manipulation capabilities of existing models insufficiently characterized. In this paper, we present BiDex-100, a simulation benchmark for bimanual dexterous manipulation that combines broad task coverage, multi-dimensional evaluation, and high-DoF hand embodiments. Concretely, the benchmark comprises 106 tasks organized along five capability axes: generalization, instruction following, limited demonstrations, long horizon, and memory. Each task is paired with an out-of-domain variant featuring controlled visual and spatial changes, allowing systematic assessment of robustness. In addition, the benchmark supports two embodiments and provides 8,080 success-validated teleoperated demonstrations, totaling 6.25 million frames. We evaluate seven recent Vision-Language-Action models under a unified protocol, establishing baselines for capability-specific performance, robustness, and data efficiency. The website is available at https://robbyant-research.github.io/BiDex-100/.

URLs: https://robbyant-research.github.io/BiDex-100/.

replace-cross From Code Generator to Engineering Companion: A Plant-aware Agent for PLC Programming

Authors: Joschka Kersting, Gesa Benndorf

Abstract: Programmable Logic Controller (PLC) coding remains a manual, IDE-centric task that demands constant context switching between documentation, project files, and code editors. Recent work has demonstrated that retrieval-augmented large language models can generate vendor-compliant Structured Text at high compilation rates, yet these systems treat code generation as a one-shot pipeline: the engineer submits a query and receives code, with no persistent knowledge of the specific plant. We present a plant-aware conversational copilot that extends this paradigm in three directions: plant-specific context ingestion, a chat-first interaction model for explanation and debugging, and a graduated tool-access ladder from chat to deployment. The system is designed for real plant infrastructure and keeps the engineer in control of each step that produces real-world effects.

replace-cross What Prediction-Market Contracts Reveal at Launch: Trading Volume and Dispute Risk

Authors: Ahmed Mahrous, Roberto Di Pietro

Abstract: Using a near-census of 802,863 resolved markets listed on Polymarket between January 2023 and May 2026, and only information available at listing (the market's title and description, named resolution source, and contract metadata), we predict lifetime trading volume and whether settlement is later disputed. Contract information predicts trading volume well (out-of-sample $R^2 = 0.747$ for log volume), and most of the signal is interpretable: market topic provides the largest gain ($+0.289$ $R^2$ over a $0.220$ contract-feature baseline), wording and resolution-rule design add smaller but measurable improvements ($+0.027$ combined), and neural text models add little beyond the interpretable features. This performance does not survive a period of platform change coinciding with a shift in listing formats: accuracy in predicting whether a market reaches \$1,000 falls from AUC $0.940$ under same-period evaluation to $0.577$ when training on past quarters and testing on later ones. The loss concentrates in markets whose question forms were absent from training (76.9% of test markets; on familiar forms the model still reaches $0.778$), and a decomposition attributes more of it to a changed relationship between contract characteristics and demand than to an outdated mapping from text to features. Dispute predictors are more stable but less precise: launch-time features rank dispute risk far better than chance (AUC $0.78$), yet at a 0.31% base rate precision is too low for case-by-case prediction (average precision $0.012$), so the scores suit ranking groups of markets rather than forecasting individual disputes.

replace-cross EgoHand: Monocular 3D Hand Reconstruction from Egocentric Video

Authors: Chao-Yeh Chen, Tiffany Chen, Lin Duan, Tianyi Hu, Yu-Hsiang Huang, Umar Iqbal, Jiefeng Li, Edy Lim, Yanchen Liu, Yihan Pang, Hang Qiu, Jun Saito, Shao Su, Qingsi Wang, Xiufeng Xie

Abstract: We introduce EgoHand, a monocular 3D hand reconstruction model that achieves state-of-the-art performance on egocentric video. Central to our approach is the curation of a high-quality training corpus of 14 million images, combining synthetic data, in-the-wild video, and multi-camera motion capture to balance scene diversity and supervision quality. The model adopts SOMA-Hand as its parametric representation and processes 2D hand crops with a transformer encoder, incorporating camera cues and an auxiliary 2D branch to improve geometric consistency and image alignment. Zero-shot evaluations on HOT3D, ARCTIC, and H2O demonstrate strong out-of-domain generalization, with lower joint-position, root-translation, and acceleration errors than competing baselines at an inference latency of approximately 9.01 ms. A blinded user preference study further shows that participants favor EgoHand's reconstructed hand poses over those from Apple EgoDex and Ropedia Xperience-10M. We release the EgoHand model and source code under the OpenMDW license, allowing free commercial use of the model.

replace-cross How Much Do You Trust A Dog? Quantifying Hint-Following in Reasoning Language Models

Authors: Anxin Guo, Chirag Pabbaraju, Sara Ahmadian, Kamesh Munagala, Aravindan Vijayaraghavan

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) often exhibit sycophancy, altering their answers to align with user suggestions even on objective factual and reasoning tasks. Yet the quantitative form of this influence--- and whether it is calibrated to source reliability--- remains poorly understood. In this work, we investigate this behavior through hint-induced answer shifts on MMLU questions, evaluating prompts of the form ``[Source] thinks the answer is (X)''. We systematically vary the source to which the hint is attributed (a professor, a friend, or a dog) across three open-source model families, Qwen, Gemma, and SmolLM. We show that the change induced by the hint follows a predictable structure well-characterized by an exponential tilt: the hint induces an approximately constant mean shift in the hinted answer's log-odds, with question--target susceptibility largely shared across sources. To explore how this pattern can arise, we introduce a formal decomposition of the hint response and illustrate in controlled synthetic experiments how KL-regularized reward-based training reshapes both hinted and unhinted answer distributions. Finally, we evaluate whether this shift is calibrated to source reliability, finding that the log-odds shift persists even when the hint-giver is declared inaccurate or accompanied by explicit warnings not to trust the source, highlighting the vulnerability of LLMs to adversarial steering.

replace-cross Combinatorial Lock for Trojan-Resilient AI Engines via Hash-Assisted Spectral Filtering

Authors: Soumita Chatterjee, Sudip Ghosh, Tamal Ghosh, Bhargab B. Bhattacharya, Hafizur Rahaman

Abstract: Multi-trigger backdoor attacks, in which heterogeneous trigger mechanisms (e.g., patch, blend, warping, and the latent-domain Spy-Watermark) coexist within a single poisoned model, cannot be addressed by existing shortcut-based defenses that assume a single, static attack mechanism. Current multi-trigger remedies (BU+FT, BU+ANP, and BU+ABL) rely on backdoor unlearning, which incurs high training costs and loses effectiveness at low poisoning ratios. This paper proposes Combinatorial Lock, a training-free, reference-calibrated defense that reduces multi-trigger identification to a combinatorial problem over binary activation signatures. This formulation is the key novelty: it yields a multi-trigger defense that is training-free, trigger-oblivious, and directly implementable in hardware, since its decision rules can be evaluated with integer and logic operations. The framework combines anomaly-gated spectral scrubbing, which removes the malicious subspace only from inputs identified as anomalous, with a hash-compressed combinatorial signature verified by an ensemble of detection experts, including a compound-attack-aware expert for multi-trigger inputs. A joint threshold-shrinkage search identifies an operating point ($\tau$ at the 97.1st percentile, $\gamma = 0.78$) that reduces the attack success rate (ASR) to as low as 0.1% while preserving 97.8% clean accuracy. Evaluation on six models (ResNet18, VGG16, MobileNet, AlexNet, ViT-Small, ViT-Base) shows strong generalization: near-100% ASR drops to single digits across poisoning rates of 1-10% and 1-9 simultaneous triggers, with only gradual degradation in clean accuracy. ASIC and FPGA implementations confirm practicality, adding a consistent 19-20% latency overhead across all evaluated architectures and establishing Combinatorial Lock as a hardware-native approach to multi-trigger backdoor mitigation.

replace-cross An actively trained neural operator for near-explicit atmospheric chemistry

Authors: Zhi-Song Liu, Wenqing Peng, Ammar Kheder, Risto Makkonen, Michael Boy

Abstract: Earth-system models simplify atmospheric chemistry into condensed mechanisms, because near-explicit mechanisms such as the Master Chemical Mechanism (MCM) are too costly to solve in every grid cell. Neural emulators could lift this limit, but most advance the chemistry in small time steps and cover only a few species or a reduced mechanism. Here we introduce FNO-MoE, a Fourier neural operator with a mixture of experts that predicts the full near-explicit chemical state over 30 minutes in a single call. The same network also guides its own training, using the disagreement among its experts to select the most informative box-model simulations. At equal cost, this active sampling yields a more accurate emulator than random or evenly spread sampling. Fine-tuning on longer trajectories keeps it stable over multi-hour runs in which models trained on short simulations diverge. The resulting emulator reproduces the evolution of thousands of species with high fidelity, remains accurate for unseen chemical compositions and runs orders of magnitude faster than the numerical solver. These results open a new direction for emulating complex atmospheric systems: neural operators that predict the full chemical state over long windows and choose their own training data. Training under changing meteorology is the next step towards bringing near-explicit chemistry into Earth-system models.

replace-cross Attack logics, not outputs: Concept-based Property Attacks for Shortcut Elimination in Neural Networks

Authors: Raik Dankworth, Felix M\"achtle, Thomas Eisenbarth, Gesina Schwalbe

Abstract: Deep neural networks (NNs) for computer vision are vulnerable to adversarial attacks, such that minuscule malicious changes to inputs may induce unintuitive outputs. One key approach to verify and mitigate such robustness issues is to falsify expected output behavior, which enables local robustness guarantees and adversarial training. Due to the black-box nature of NNs, current attacks only falsify a class of the final output, such as flipping from $\text{stop_sign}$ to $\neg \text{stop_sign}$. In this work we argue that robustness analysis should focus more generally on logical inconsistencies in concept-level reasoning rather than solely on output misclassification. We formalize this perspective through concept-based properties, i.e., constraints over human-interpretable concepts such as $\text{red} \land \text{octogonal} \implies \text{stop_sign}$ and study the falsification of such properties as a principled form of adversarial attack. Furthermore, an implementation of concept-based properties on already trained NNs is proposed using techniques from concept-based explainable artificial intelligence (C-XAI). For that we formally prove that attacks on concept-based properties are expected to have a reduced search space compared to simple class falsification, whilst arguably be more aligned with intuitive robustness targets. The practical effectiveness of our approach for logically informed adversarial training is shown in an empirical study across multiple models and datasets, including both vision and a security-relevant code processing task. Our results demonstrate that concept-level falsification generalizes classical adversarial attacks: Retraining on our novel attacks maintains robustness gains as compared to classical adversarial retraining whilst better eliminating semantic biases.

replace-cross mdRNAcat: A Molecular Dynamics RNA Catalog of REST2 Conformational Ensembles

Authors: Anton E. Dorn, Emile de Bruyn, Fabrice von der Lehr, Philipp Knechtges, Stefan Kesselheim, Alexander Schug

Abstract: Learning molecular models of RNA requires simulation data that capture conformational variability and provide consistent physical training signals. Here, we introduce mdRNAcat, a curated dataset of all-atom molecular dynamics (MD) simulations of 48 RNA systems selected to promote sequence diversity. Each system is simulated using replica-exchange with solute scaling (REST2), with 32 Hamiltonian states and production lengths of 200-300 ns per state. The dataset provides atomic coordinates, instantaneous forces, and forces reevaluated under the unscaled reference Hamiltonian, together with structural annotations and replica-exchange metadata. Coordinates and forces are available at 1 ps intervals for the reference state and 100 ps intervals across all states in a standardized HDF5 format. We characterize conformational sampling, exchange behavior, base pairing, and sugar-pucker distributions to document the properties of the simulated ensembles. mdRNAcat provides a reusable resource for developing and evaluating data-driven RNA models, including coarse-grained force fields.

replace-cross Pretraining Knows What, Simulation Teaches How: Compositional Generalization in a Sim-Trained Robot Agent

Authors: Benjamin Pikus, Daniel Saunders, Mircea Mironenco, Salman Mohammadi, Mathew Mathew, Sirou Zhu, Divya Makkar, Xuhua Huang, Qinbo Li, Supreeth Narasimhaswamy, Siyuan Cen, Dejia Xu, Shyamal Buch, Thomas Neff

Abstract: A robot policy needs to understand what a task is asking for in a scene, and how to move the robot to accomplish that task. To achieve these two skills, we introduce Zero-real Agent Policy (ZAP), a recipe that teaches a small off-the-shelf vision-language model to control a robot via a simple tool harness. We use a pretrained 27B parameter model to bootstrap world understanding and reasoning, followed by supervised fine-tuning and GRPO on simple simulated tasks to teach it how to move the robot. Without observing real-world robot data during training, ZAP reaches 96.7% success on real-world tasks, matching GPT-6 Astra in our harness and outperforming the MolmoAct2 VLA (33.3%), while running 3x faster and 17x cheaper than GPT-6 Astra. Furthermore, ZAP also generalizes to an unseen embodiment as well as new objects, goals and strategies never seen during training. On a single-arm Franka, ZAP reaches 55.9% on RoboLab-120, 4.9 percentage points ahead of the leading VLA/WAM baseline.
